![Cover for Meditations of the Pali Tradition, Author, L. S. Cousins; edited by Sarah Shaw](../images/9780834844438_cover.jpg)

“*Meditations of the Pali Tradition* shines an incisive light on the multifaceted jewel of the meditation practices of Southern Buddhism across the ages, drawing in sidelights from related material in other early Buddhist schools and their transmissions to China. Cousins writes with deep knowledge of and informed interest in a network of meditative practices as interpreted and developed from the Pali Canon, *abhidhamma*, and commentaries. He explores the nature and role of the *jhānas* and formless attainments, their effects on feeling and perception, and how these practices concerning calm (*samatha*) relate to those of insight (*vipassanā*). He addresses not only the roots and developments of modern *vipassanā* meditation, but also the more esoteric visualization practices of the *Porāṇa* meditative traditions that were sidelined by modern *vipassanā* but are now being increasingly uncovered and explored. In an impartial and discerning way, he leads us through some of the rich intricacies of these different systems and their emphases. This illuminating work, which broadens and deepens our picture of meditation in the Southern ‘Theravāda’ tradition, will be of great interest both to scholars and to practitioners of these and other meditations.”

—Peter Harvey, cofounder of the UK Association for Buddhist Studies and author of *An Introduction to Buddhism: Teachings, History, and Practices* (Cambridge University Press)

“*Meditations of the Pali Tradition* is both calming and insightful. For a forest monk, it can be painful to read the overly convoluted and contentious diatribes which are found too often in academic discourse. Cousins’s book is a welcome relief from such thickets. In addition to his skill in arranging, elucidating, and reconciling disparate texts in multiple languages and from various traditions, two other qualities shine through in this work: his respect for ‘forest specialists’ and his interest in the practical application of his erudition. This book provides fresh perspectives into both much-loved discourses and arcane treatises, all the while presenting them with concern, humility, and the estimable sincerity of someone who was trying to translate the Dharma into their own heart’s vernacular.”

<!-- Page 2 -->

—Ajahn Kovilo, senior incumbent, Clear Mountain Monastery

“Cousins’s wide interests inspired him to delve deeply into the core questions of Buddhism, mysticism, and religious studies. His writings are the fruit of a keen and sensitive wisdom tempered by personal experience and an unparalleled knowledge of the original texts in the original languages. They offer fresh and timely insights into Buddhist thought, practice, and history.”

—Peter Skilling, author of *Questioning the Buddha: A Selection of Twenty-Five Sutras*

# Editor’s Introduction

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

Buddhist meditation has been practiced, often in private, for over two thousand years. Until the twentieth century it was still taught mostly from one person to another, frequently in confidence; it is the flower that flourishes in the shade. Those who practice meditation learn much of how to do it from other living people. We learn as we watch how they sit, or feel something about their presence, or respond to their tone of voice or silences. Because of all this, writing a history of meditation is a difficult thing: we have to reconstruct and speculate from texts and manuals. Perhaps because of the immediacy of the subject, accounts of developments in meditation have not been “university subjects” in the way that, for instance, the key philosophical ideas in Buddhism have. Meditation does not have the same histories and obvious continuity. So perhaps it is not that remarkable that no study of the history of Southern Buddhist meditation, to my knowledge, has been written before. What is remarkable is that the scholar who wrote this one was probably the only person who could have done so: I cannot think of one better placed. As far as I know, it is the only book of its kind.

This book is published posthumously; its author, Lance Cousins, died in 2015, after spending a lifetime immersing himself in the literature on the subject and finding out about its practices from scholars and exponents. He had spent his whole career on the languages and theory of Pali and Sanskrit, Buddhist texts and commentaries. He engaged in conversations with traditional teachers from all Asian Buddhist schools, as well as theologians, philologists, historians, grammarians, and Buddhist scholars in the West. He was the leading scholar in the West on *abhidhamma*, the philosophical and psychological system so heavily dependent on the practice of meditation for its patterning, style, content, and delivery as a chanted tradition.

<!-- Page 5 -->

In this introduction I shall give some account of his work and his academic achievement. I shall also interpret some of the threads in the book—with the proviso that they are only my readings. Lance had a very subtle mind, and the only thing I can be quite sure of is that he would have expressed some things quite differently from the way I have done here! I will also give a brief survey of his life and work, though it does not feel important to give much biography. Lance once gave me his opinion on this subject, saying with amusement that he agreed entirely with Ajahn Tate on the matter. This great forest meditation teacher claims in the preface to his autobiography that he had hurriedly completed it to prevent other people doing the job for him—and also says that after his death people could do what they liked![[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-1 "note") Lance would not have wanted, perhaps even posthumously, any work of that kind. But a little background and context helps to understand the great contribution he made to Buddhist studies.

## **Life and Work**

Lance Selwyn Cousins was born in 1942 in Hitchin, Hertfordshire. In 1961 he won a scholarship to read history at St. John’s College, Cambridge. He then changed to Oriental studies and studied with Professor Sir Harold Bailey and Professor K. R. Norman, scholars who both left a mark on his scholarly methods and understanding. This gave him a solid philological and linguistic grounding in Pali and Sanskrit. He went on to read *sutta*s and commentaries, as well as more widely in Buddhism, mysticism, and theology. He founded the Buddhist Society at Cambridge University, and used to visit the Buddhist Society in London, where he met the few meditation teachers that were around then, before finding his lifelong allegiance in meditation with Boonman Poonyathiro, a Thai teacher in *samatha* breathing mindfulness. From the outset the two sides of his interests, practice and academia, coincided.

Lance was the violinist who studies the history of the violin and its music. But it needs to be said that he was a scholar of the old school and saw the academic arena as one in which to drop one’s personal “voice.” In this book and in his academic writing, he quotes from first-person accounts, texts, and commentaries but does not speak of his own experience. He preferred L. S. rather than Lance on his articles, an expression of that caution. But a historian of a musical instrument like the violin or the clarinet and its music, inscribed in scores and on records, is able to piece together the information therein and see what is relevant, especially if they have actually spent a long time working directly with the instrument involved. They understand why a particular kind of string may produce a better note or understand the breathing techniques needed to play a wind instrument in a certain way. Lance knew how to sift through information and records and see what was pertinent and what would interest scholars in further understanding the subject. He also appreciated keenly the needs of people whose interest might not be academic but who wanted to find out more about meditation as a support and background to their own practice.

<!-- Page 6 -->

While in Cambridge Lance married Barbara. They lived in Denmark for a few years, and had two children, Randal and Halla. In 1970 Lance went to Manchester University to lecture in comparative religion, in a department headed first by Professor Trevor Ling and then Professor John Hinnells. During this time he started to teach meditation and founded the Manchester Centre for Buddhist Meditation (Samatha Centre) and served as the founding chairman of the Samatha Trust, which acquired its own national center in Powys, Wales, in 1987. In the nineties he and Barbara sadly divorced and Lance took early retirement. He had always wanted more time to pursue academic study, and so in 2000 he moved to Oxford, where he became a member of Wolfson College and taught for the faculties of Oriental Studies and Theology. He was delighted to be able, at last, to pursue his love of scholarly research, and wrote a number of articles then. Though no longer actively involved in the administration of the Samatha Trust, he continued to practice and take retreats in *samatha* breathing mindfulness.

Richard Gombrich sums up well the two sides of Lance’s career, in a discussion published in the *Journal for the Oxford Centre for Buddhist Studies*:

> Lance sometimes pointed out that he had a sort of dual Buddhist career. Nearly 50 years ago, when he moved to Manchester to take up his first academic post, he took the leading role in founding the Samatha Trust. He specialized in Buddhist meditation in both Thailand and Sri Lanka, and became a guide and inspiration to many of those around him—how many and how durably was demonstrated by the number who attended his funeral and the many tributes that then appeared on Facebook. He was explicit, however, in refusing to allow his religious experiences to influence his academic work, which he pursued without deviating from the canon of philological orthodoxy. The time and energy that he devoted to Buddhist practice, both his own and that of others, did however mean that he was slow to publish academic work, so that in worldly terms he had a less successful career than would otherwise have been the case. Only after he took early retirement from Manchester University and moved to Oxford, some ten years ago, did he publish more prolifically and enhance his scholarly reputation.
>
> Those who knew him better had long been aware that Lance’s knowledge of the Pali language and of Pali literature (as well as other Indian religious and philosophical traditions) was rivalled by few people anywhere in the world and the age of email made him the constant target of requests for help and advice—not least from me and my graduate students. His residence in Oxford was fortunate for the University, in that he could take over the Pali teaching of undergraduates. As I write, it is not clear how he can be replaced in this role.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-2 "note")

<!-- Page 7 -->

Despite the fact that there were not many, it is worth noting here that the articles he wrote in the seventies to the end of the twentieth century are widely read and appreciated as the best introductions one might find to meditation systems in Southeast and South Asia, and to the *abhidhamma*. Many of these are on his academia. edu site. Lance in effect created a subject area and in this book he gives the overview of a history that has not been explored in the larger context he presents us with here.

## **The Genesis of This Book**

Lance, characteristically, did not say much about the genesis of this book, other than that it was based on lectures and talks he was giving in his last couple of years. Despite longstanding heart problems—he had had two heart attacks, so he knew he did not have long—in his last years he was supporting work on the *Yamaka* translation, as well as writing this book. He clearly had some innate sense of timing—he wrote the preface to the *Yamaka* translation a few weeks before his death.

I knew about this book’s existence and possible completion rather by chance. A week before he died, Lance and I had a coffee together in Oxford. It was a sunny day and we mostly laughed and let the conversation roam. He was happily busy in Oxford Hilary term. I had asked him to give me an update about each one of his seven grandchildren, of whom he was glowingly proud. But I had always been intrigued by an aside he had made a couple of years before, that he had decided to write a book on meditation. This time I saw a good moment to ask about it, so I did, as casually as I could. He was noncommittal—he said it was “all there” in a folder on his computer, and he would come back to it when term was over. We moved to other topics. Later, I was glad I had raised the subject. When Halla and Randal kindly offered me the chance to go through his computer after his death, I knew what to look for. I didn’t even know the title, or how far he had got with it—he was reticent about that sort of thing, and after his death I found he had not even told others about the book. We did manage to find it, a complete book neatly arranged in eight drafts in a folder with a working title of *Buddhist Meditations: Old and New*.

<!-- Page 8 -->

So here it is. Its subject matter is the meditative traditions of Southern or Pali Buddhism, with some inclusion of material from other sources. I have edited it for publication, but left it pretty much as it was, except for the addition of some notes and references he had earmarked to look up later. Lance came from a long family line of printers and was a dedicated scholar. He would, I am sure, have had all sorts of things he would have wanted done a certain way. Luckily, he also—equally passionately—felt people should do whatever was right for the time. I hope that what is here satisfies the latter condition at any rate! The book has been read by scholars, practitioners, and people interested in the subject, all of whom have offered valuable advice. In this regard, particular thanks are due to Professor Peter Harvey for his close editorial work on edited versions of Lance’s original script. Professor Jas Elsner also read the script closely, offering comments and edits. Rob Adkins, Dr. Francis Beresford, Dr. Mark Rowlands, Charles Shaw, and Dr. Chris Westrup made extremely helpful comments and suggestions on reading the entire book. Thanks are due too to Professor Kate Crosby, for reading over chapter 8. Professor Rupert Gethin and Dr Petra Kieffer-Pülz patiently sifted through some translated passages to check for consistency in style conventions. Professor Brockington helped out with a last-minute reference.

## **Influence in Pali Studies**

Over the last decades, understandings of Theravāda Buddhism, or Southern or Pali Buddhism, have undergone radical shifts in perception. The role Lance played in this is perhaps not usually acknowledged as he did not necessarily write on some subjects he was teaching. In the early seventies, for instance, he was stressing the consonance of the heaven realms and the meditation states. He gave a talk on this at the Manchester Buddhist Society and taught the heaven realms to his students in this way. A number of distinguished works by Professor Rupert Gethin, Professor Steven Collins, and Professor Peter Harvey have now extended and explored this understanding in various new directions. The notion that the heaven realms may enact, embody, or act as metaphors for states we can experience in meditation—constituting a psychological as well as cosmological map—is now accepted. The understanding of how this works, and the way it is communicated through canon, commentary, and practice have been developed in highly sophisticated and insightful ways, which Lance would never have attempted to examine himself. That is rather the key to how he worked for many years: chatting with people, teaching them, and suggesting texts that particularly suited someone’s own interests was for many years his *métier*. Much of the material one finds now on *abhidhamma* and meditation is written by people who were given such leads by Lance; he had a capacity for invisibility in that regard.

<!-- Page 9 -->

## **Primary Themes and Investigations**

What can we draw from the book? The initial theme is the centrality of *jhāna*, with which he starts the discussion. Lance translates and draws together a number of often uncited sources to defend the core importance of this meditative state both within the canon and also, crucially, within the Indian schools, commentarial traditions, and manuals of the centuries after that. This raises the question as to why the book starts with the commentaries in chapter 1 rather than the earlier canonical material, dealt with in chapter 2. That he did this is not surprising. Lance was alive to critical differences between canon and commentaries. He did not, however, share the distrust felt by some modern scholars of the later explicatory material as being some kind of depleted adaptation of the original meditative and teaching situation at the time of the Buddha. He felt commentaries showed methods, practice, and the reception of the teachings in the centuries after the Buddha’s death. Given that some are so early and the method itself predates Buddhism, many of their pieces of advice, meditational instructions, and explications could well date from the Buddha’s lifetime. The Buddha, an innovative and exploratory teacher, encouraged those who worked with him to be so too; we know there were commentaries applying and extending his work from the time of Mahinda in Lanka, even if not exactly in the form we have them now. It was natural for Lance to suppose that much of the material of the later commentaries is probably itself ancient, arising from explorations and questions posed, perhaps even to the Buddha, from the earliest times. Their interpretations throw light on his own understanding of the role of *jhāna* and the formless meditations. Practical information on procedures and techniques might also not be recorded in the canon, as people would have been surrounded by practitioners who would show them how to do the practices involved or could discuss and explain them further. Meditation on the device (*kasiṇa*), for instance, is barely explained at all in the canon; later it receives extensive analysis by Buddhaghosa. One can see why Lance felt later accounts, so influential throughout the history of Buddhist meditation practice and theory, were a better place to start his book.

<!-- Page 10 -->

After his discussion of *jhāna*, Lance makes careful exploration of the terminology and subsequent understanding of the formless meditations that traditionally follow the development of the four *rūpa jhāna*s, offering important perspectives for scholars on these specialized and sometimes misunderstood or sidelined practices. This discussion in itself offers an important contribution to our knowledge on this subject. He also, crucially, looks at reception—not just how the Pali commentators took the early texts, but also how centuries of subsequent Indian philosophers and exegetes understood key terms.

As part of this discussion, he offers an analysis of the *jhāna* system based on the five aggregates. This is a very unusual approach, and I have not seen it given such a careful exposition before. It offers a creatively insightful perspective for modern scholars and practitioners written, as one reader noted, “with the easy assurance of a lifelong scholar.” As Lance points out, his analysis is based almost entirely on early Indian sources, such as Aśvaghoṣa. He carefully authenticates his argument with corroborations within the Pali Canon and subsequent commentaries, providing many quotes and translations from texts that have not been translated before. This certainly offers an arresting template for understanding such states and how they were felt for centuries to be integrated within a larger overall system.

The key terms of “directing the attention of the mind” (*vitakka*) and “exploring and sustaining attention” (*vicāra*) are discussed too. As indicated before, Lance did not bring personal experience into academic matters, but here some private interest could have informed this public and academic exposition. For instance, where *vitakka* and *vicāra* occur as factors in meditation, he notes the need for a lighter form of attention than some have suggested. In his discussion of one-pointedness (*ekaggatā*), Lance, in contrast with some recent scholars, stresses the canonical importance of this factor in the attainment of *jhāna*. As he notes, the now common assertion that this factor is a later addition to the *jhāna* factors is challenged by its presence in the *Mahāvedalla-sutta* (M 43). In addition, the *Anupada-sutta* (M 111)—a *sutta* that speaks, if any do, with the authentic “voice” of the *arahat* Sāriputta—lists one-pointedness as present in his delineation of the factors present in *jhāna*. Lance’s careful sifting through sources sometimes finds many such canonical references that others overlook; though he doesn’t say so, one feels his own practice animates this precision.

<!-- Page 11 -->

Lance also explores the insight traditions, carefully noting and examining the threads of the ancient creative tension between insight and calm. Chapters 4, 9, and 10 discuss these traditions and their recent history in detail; his image of the corkscrew in chapter 5 to describe the interplay of calm and insight is memorable and convincing. Chapter 6, on the various combinations of the meditation on the in-and-out breath, knits together the complex themes of the book with an almost *abhidhamma* method, trying out variations against one another to discover the whole.

Chapters 7 and 8 are compelling. Here Lance investigates the diverse and colorful meditative traditions from which both Mahāyāna and some of the now lost schools derived—meditative traditions that continued to flourish in many places. He argues that many features we identify as Mahāyāna may have emerged before the movement itself. The lack of what we now see as Mahāyāna doctrine in the practices usually associated with the Mahāyāna rather than the Theriya traditions provides further evidence that the categories we now use must have operated far more flexibly in the early days. We see features in these practices that perhaps surfaced and crystallized in some Mahāyāna traditions in ways that have led us to think they originated there. Lance suggests a very rich and varied practice line in meditation, hinted at by the art of Sanchi and Amaravati as well as in texts, for which we see evidence in many early Buddhist schools. He does not write of Chan, Tibetan Buddhism, or Pure Land, but he enjoyed finding out about them, and did not feel the modern splits into regions for Buddhism told the whole story. He felt that a creative teacher and teaching elicits creative responses: the history of Buddhism could be seen as a series of explorations, in some cases following on from earlier impulses that we might not know about now.

Lance left no overall conclusion to this book. I suspect he did not intend to add much to the book as there are few changes in his eight drafts. Both simplicity and profundity are key themes in the content of this book and its delivery. The last chapters do provide an overview: he looks at the Buddha’s message from a number of different angles and examines various outcomes of the patterns that emerge. Whether or not he hoped to write a short personal summary at the end I do not know, but it seems apt to conclude the book with his translation of chapter 14 of *The Entrance to Abhidhamma* (*Abhidhammāvatāra*), which he published in the public journal of his own meditation group a few months before his death. This discusses meditation, and is one of our earliest commentaries on *samatha* practice.

<!-- Page 12 -->

This raises the whole interesting question about consideration of books written immediately before someone dies: Had the author finished? Edmund Spenser, the English poet, also left a work, *The Faerie Queene*, in that case supposedly “unfinished.” But the “Two Cantoes of Mutabilitie,” fragments of an apparently unrealized Book VII, themselves offer a sense of the inevitable movement and transience of days, seasons, cycles, and the transformations of all phenomena, as if the author was moving to see life in a different perspective before his death. From a Buddhist slant it feels rather like an insight practice after the measured and highly structured calm of the six prior books of the poem.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-3 "note") I think Lance also felt that his book had said what he wanted to say. He too seems to move to an overall perspective, and it feels as if he does not want to step into speculation. Steven Collins, another great scholar of Buddhist literature, practice, and narrative, died in 2017 and also left behind a book, *Wisdom as a Way of Life*. I find myself in the comparably odd position as Dan Arnold, Justin McDaniel, and Charles Hallisey, who brought that work to publication. Hallisey’s afterword notes that we should be wary of the usual tendency to regard a work that was left just before the author’s death as unfinished, and could rather assume that it is complete, perhaps in a challenging way. Comparing Steve’s book to qualities found in the harmonies of Bach’s Cello Suites, he eloquently suggests that

> a good way of reading … is not to try to add to it, out of an assumption that its form is only because its author did not finish it, but rather to read it with an attentive ear to the implied harmonies and resulting polyphony in interpretation that are to be found throughout, attentive to how a “complete picture” gradually comes into view in our minds as we read and re-read this book.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-4 "note")

This is curiously apposite here. Lance’s book leaves many clues and bases for speculation. Many conjectures, however, remain tacit and never imposed. There do seem to be surprising and interesting harmonies; he notes as much but does not always speculate as to how they arose. Looking back on my last conversation with Lance, he didn’t say exactly whether he had finished it or not—just that it was “all there,” a phrase that could be understood in many ways. I don’t think he would have added much.

<!-- Page 13 -->

One question that came to my mind working on this is whether anyone else could have written it. The simple answer is no. Lance was the only person in the world with both the range of knowledge and the specific interest to take on this job. Aerial photography has given modern archaeologists an extraordinary insight into ancient sites, as the layers of subsequent boundary lines, edifices, and structures that leave no trace on the ground are so clearly revealed from the distance of a plane. Modern scholarship, the internet, and our capacity to compare and examine a number of different Buddhisms, in different places, are giving modern scholars of Buddhism the chance to examine this “aerial” view of differing periods and terrains; much of this was not even possible until the twenty-first century. But such work demands many skills. Lance was probably the only person who could take advantage of our new knowledge and overall perspective and could also feel the soil in his hands and pay attention to the details “on the ground.” He gives an accurate and well-defined aerial photograph of the history of the Southern or Pali meditative traditions, what we know about how they were practiced and their Pali exegesis. But you feel that he has picked up the earth too—such as the details of how people practice, or the way things work in specific regions and temples—and knows what to look for. The larger history, the view from above so to speak, has never been considered realistic. No books make links of this kind and demonstrate the patterns in that history. Lance’s book does. He sets parameters for such study, and we can hope that others try to pick up the threads.

Lance’s skills are rare. He was a high-resolution scholar, working on times and terrains that are in some places uncertain. You need an eye for detail on the ground as well the ability to shift perspective and see the whole. Very few scholars can do both well, but if one can do that and not offer overly hasty conclusions, everyone benefits. A lifetime of discussion with practitioners and scholars contributed to Lance’s expertise. Some of the material in this work has dramatically significant implications: the book shows surprising and important affinities that cross vast geographical distances. Configurations in one location markedly resemble those in another, separated by great distances of space and time. The assessment of consonances between the Sri Lankan esoteric traditions and the early Chinese *Yogalehrbuch* is thought-provoking, to say the least. Lance suspected that many such meditative traditions, often esoteric, circulated even before the advent of the different distinct schools, and felt they probably continued for many centuries after that, flourishing in all kinds of places.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-5 "note") As he writes in his chapter on the Silk Road routes, archaeological and textual evidence about meditation tends to be from libraries, monuments, and edifices built at times of prosperity. Such landmarks could have come into being well after what could have been centuries of travel and hence the possible interchange of teachings. The dense presence of tangible remains could even indicate the onset of a decline. What was the true history of the way these practices traveled and flourished? As an academic, Lance valued observation over conclusion. He observes what phenomena he can and leaves the rest of us to draw our own interpretations from this: we are the ones who must try to read the aerial photograph.

<!-- Page 14 -->

But he certainly did sense patterns through his work. In private conversations and some of his written work he made it clear that he felt that Buddhism and Buddhisms did not so much undergo revolutionary changes, as is sometimes supposed from its gradual geographical spread, but that sometimes some features or ways of understanding became less evident, while others emerged again and resurfaced, perhaps in a different form. The image that comes to my mind is of an underground river that spurts up or flows up in different places in different terrains. Clearly, to say Buddhism was unchanging would be both untrue and eternalist. But to say it somehow “lost” some things and then “found” others would not quite be Lance’s way of thinking. Rather, in different conditions, historical periods, and cultures, the eightfold path fared differently. He felt Buddhism adapted creatively and flexibly, and perhaps possibilities latent at the outset, or somewhat historically hidden, became available when needed. This book continues to suggest that to me.

One feature of the book lies in its academic style and manner, as much as content. In Buddhist studies, like any other field, people can be hasty to pronounce or dogmatic. Lance’s careful caution is well communicated, and always interesting to read. It offers an antidote to potential “band-wagoning.” An example of this is in his discussion of the various recensions of *sutta*s/*sūtra*s. He applauds both the movement toward looking for versions of texts in the Chinese and the assessment of newly discovered material. But he also offers some useful weather warnings: a Chinese counterpart of a canonical text certainly suggests an earlier layer, but its absence from the Chinese recensions does not mean that a Pali text is necessarily thereby later; other factors could be at play. The Chinese were apparently less interested in Moggallāna, for instance, a feature that could help to explain the absence of much material relating to him in Chinese recensions of early texts. The presence of so much material relating to Moggallāna in the Indian schools suggests such material was early and was perhaps sidelined by Chinese practitioners for their own recensions. He warns against modern scholarship taking the formulaic route that early Pali textual scholars did, in assuming that only Pali texts also found in Chinese versions are authoritative. Texts *not* found in Chinese could be early too. Exploring these issues and the exciting developments of the texts recently found in Gandhāran regions offered an interesting jigsaw for Lance, and the reader senses his willingness to go for a number of alternative interpretations.

<!-- Page 15 -->

### ***Abhidhamma***

Something should also be said about the system whose presence may be felt throughout Lance’s book, the *abhidhamma/abhidharma.* The world of Buddhist scholarship is probably mildly divided between those who admire, enjoy, and benefit from this third “basket” of the teaching and those who see it as a rather dry scholastic exercise after the event of the Buddha’s death. Lance defends it; he knew the living traditions of South and Southeast Asia, and saw it being learned with enjoyment and delight by laity and monks there. Although it was composed somewhat later than the *sutta*s, and is distinctively different in its method and content, he felt some works represented an early commentary on the canon as well as offering a valid method of investigation in themselves. In the seventies, a Cambodian monk, Venerable Candavaṇṇa, came to Manchester, escaping from the terrible events in his own country. Lance spent hours discussing *abhidhamma* with him. Lance realized that he was encountering a living tradition of practice and theory—the reception of *abhidhamma* one might say—that was at that time apparently unthreatened. He later realized this had been a very lucky meeting. The way that Cambodian monastic style of that time encouraged lists learned as chants first, known “by heart,” and then integrated into the practitioner’s own experience through line-by-line discussion, teaching, and examples from daily life was, he felt, probably how it had been taught at its inception. Similes were used from the commentaries, and new ones were created in an ongoing process of engagement with the text. Cambodian methods were distinct, but comparable approaches are found in Burma/Myanmar and Thailand now. He prized such consultations. In his own work, arguments tend to be based on textual and philological grounds rather than close examination of, for instance, Asian pedagogic methods of exegesis and ways of encouraging interaction with the texts—though these features would themselves be seen as a useful object of scrutiny in our more anthropological and ethnographic times.

The fact that Lance saw those who have been steeped in *abhidhamma* understanding teach, debate, and explain it as a living tradition helped him to understand why it is so perennially popular in South and Southeast Asia. It is, in some areas, regarded as a workable and practical system of psychology, and a way of explaining and decoding daily experience and meditation in a way that opens doors and does not shut them. Knowing, for instance, that skillful consciousness is always accompanied by one of the divine abidings, that mindfulness always arises with the intuitive ethical factors of self-respect and fear for consequences, and that wisdom is sometimes present, but not always, at such times, endows each moment with possibilities. According to the *Dhammasaṅganī* and the *Vibhaṅga*, every moment of skillful consciousness has factors that are there at the moment of awakening, just in weaker form. Steven Collins wrote of “felicities” on the way to awakening. Any moment of skillful consciousness is, when the *abhidhamma* is taught and understood well, such a moment of felicity: a chance to glimpse through the overgrown leaves and jungle roots of our forest and see the view that lies behind.

<!-- Page 16 -->

## **The Academic World and Wider Contacts**

One feature of this book is that it is the product of discussion, friendship, and interchange in addition to close research. Lance’s international contacts were extensive and frequently included monastic scholars and practitioners from Buddhist regions. He felt it was as important to know the reception of *abhidhamma*, for instance, in its original form: How was it used, taught, and explained at the time of its evolution and in the centuries after the Buddha’s death? Traditional training techniques, methods, and interpretations could give clues about this. How were meditation practices taught and practiced historically? Variations in modern methods and techniques could give some hints. So he consulted with Sayadaw Khammai Dhammasāmi, Sayadaw U Ñāṇika, Sayadaw U Rewata Dhamma, Sayadaw U Tiṭṭhila, and Dr. Friedegard Lottermoser for Burmese perspectives on *abhidhamma*. He visited Sri Lanka five times, including some extended stays at monasteries, where he visited and spoke with friends such as Professor P. D. Premasiri and Professor Asanga Tilakaratne. He had many monastic Sri Lankan contacts, perhaps most notably Venerable Anandamaitreya and Venerable Saddhatissa, both of whom he invited to Manchester. He valued his Korean contacts, in particular Venerable Kim Doo Wan. He knew and consulted many Thai monks; he invited Ajahn Chah to visit Manchester and always enjoyed discussions with monks from the Rama IX temple and Wat Maha That, Bangkok. He respected Phra Maha Laow’s knowledge of chant styles deeply. He asked Ato Rinpoche, a Tibetan incarnate lama, to stay in Manchester several times. In his own tradition, he was a lifelong fellow practitioner with Dr. Paul Dennison and their meditation teacher, Boonman Poonyathiro. Paul gave a moving address at Lance’s funeral.

He liked discussion with knowledgeable monastic scholars, so well versed from childhood in chant, recitation, explication, and Pali. He compared this to the way a Sanskritist benefits from interchange with *paṇḍita*s in India. Of course, many of the approaches to grammar differ from modern Western methods and analysis, but there is also so much to learn from explanations, recitative methods, and ways of understanding Sanskrit’s linguistic forms. He also saw nonacademic monastics as important sources of theoretical explication that had implications for academic study. He commented, for instance, on the precision of the doctrinal understanding of Forest Tradition monastics, both the Asian and some of the more experienced Western monks and nuns, despite their occasional claims *not* to be theoretical! To him, their interpretations and articulations of traditional material often offered key sources of information on the historical reception of meditative texts and the still-living traditions of practice in Southeast Asian Buddhism.

<!-- Page 17 -->

Throughout his professional life, Lance maintained his loyalty to academia, which he saw, at its best, as an arena where it was possible to step away from partisan alignments and the sometimes well-intentioned but erroneous convictions always possible in any community. He was not blind to shortcomings in supposedly neutral scholarly discourse: he saw misguided belief in one’s own clear-headedness as a threat to anyone! He felt academia itself could be subject to unhelpful fashions and buzzwords. Agendas can come from any viewpoint, and he was concerned at the pride involved in academic reductivism, with its attempts to debunk or dismiss features of Buddhism and its practice on the basis of doubtful scholarly pretexts. Certain aspects of Buddhist studies, such as the ritual, the spiritual, the somatic, and the devotional were also, he felt, often seriously undervalued. He felt they showed often subtle enactments of Buddhist principle. Good scholarship, for him, required ongoing skill in testing one’s impartiality and preconceptions. It offered a chance for people from all kinds of nationalities, backgrounds, and personal allegiances to discuss Buddhist studies on a fair playing field. In practice, I think he liked a genuine spectrum of participators in the subject, offering many different perspectives. Richard Gombrich noted at Lance’s funeral that he was one of those scholars who did not just make do with one citation in references but preferred to ensure that there were several.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-6 "note") These details were important to him. I should add here that Lance knew that academia was not some halcyon bed of roses. Lance had major differences with some scholars. One or two of these conflicts caused him, and I suspect them, great unhappiness. I cannot comment on that other than to say that in my experience, when he saw he had been wrong, he would say so; some apparent foes became surprisingly close allies in time. He certainly never ceased to respect and love the world of academic discourse, which he saw as his lifeblood.

In his last years Lance taught a great deal for the Theology and the Oriental Studies faculties at the University of Oxford. He also developed professional relationships and sometimes close personal friendships with others involved in his subject. Philology was a particular specialty, and he felt lucky to be part of a community where he could consult and discuss so many features of the early texts with people he regarded as world experts in their fields. Here are some people I know he liked to chat with in and around Oxford and London: the late Dr. Nick Allen, Dr. Naomi Appleton, Geoff Bamford, Dr. Imre Banghra, Dr. Jim Benson, Dr. Shailendra Bhandarake, Dr. Pathompong Bodhiprasiddhinand, Professor John and Mary Brockington, Dr. Cathy Cantwell, Professor Kate Crosby, Professor Florin Deleanu (when visiting Oxford), Dr. Gillian Evison, Professor Gavin Flood, Dr. Camillo Formigatti, Professor David Gellner, Dr. Georgios Halkias, Dr. Elizabeth Harris, Professor Paul Harrison (on visits to Oxford), Professor Julia Hegewald, Dr. Soonil Hwang, Dr. Pyi Phyo Kyaw, Dr. Chris Jones, Dr. John Lowe, Professor Chris Minkowski, Dr. Rob Mayer, Peggy Morgan, Sarah Norman, Professor Isabelle Onians, Dr. Noa Ronkin, Professor Ulrike Roesler, Dr. Julia Shaw, Professor Peter Skilling (on visits to Oxford), Dr. Andrew Skilton, Dr. Elizabeth Tucker, Dr. Alexander Wynne, the late Professor Stefano Zacchetti. On the continent, he held in esteem scholars such as Professor Dieter Schlingloff and Professor Jens-Uwe Hartmann, as well as many I do not know. They were all people with knowledge and expertise he thought crucially important in different ways for his work. These are just some I can think of. I apologize to the many I know I have left out.

<!-- Page 18 -->

Special mention, however, should go to Richard Gombrich, who founded the Oxford Centre for Buddhist Studies in the early 2000s. Lance was made an honorary fellow. Both showed—and Richard still does—a capacity for great generosity and personal engagement with their students. Lance had great respect for Richard’s work. He particularly admired the way Richard had conducted his doctoral study in Sri Lanka and had made real connections and lifelong friendships with people he had met there. This, he felt, had given Richard the intuitive understanding of Sri Lankan Buddhism “on the ground” that informs his academic analyses. Lance always attended the talks organized by Richard and the ebullient, scholarly, and kindly Stefano Zacchetti. It is sad to think there will be no more talks quite of this type; Stefano died suddenly in 2020. These talks were often highly entertaining, with some verbal swordplay! Richard and Lance sometimes exhibited radically different understandings. They were frequently *en garde*, a fencing term for a stance that describes very precisely both men’s attitudes to academic discourse. On some occasions the *épée*s of both Richard and Lance, equally trained and delighting in academic combat, flashed against one another rapidly. The rest of us ducked as arguments on both sides compelled. They almost always found common ground, but the “match” still stimulated everyone and moved our understanding along. Meals at Gino’s afterward, our local Italian restaurant, were accompanied by convivial continuations of discussion. Richard had also started Sanskritists’ lunch, an Oxford ritual that has extended over a few decades now, a means whereby the many Oxford scholars in Indic studies could have a chance to meet one another. Lance went every week. As a man on his own, Sanskritists’ lunch gave him what he needed: a chance for chat and debate. Events Richard organized gave Lance many opportunities for this. Lance also had a deep admiration for Dr. Sanjukta Gupta, Richard’s wife, whose Sanskrit lineage, Lance felt, was rooted in a rigorous living tradition of linguistic and philosophic practice, with nonetheless profoundly spiritual origins too.

Lance’s academic legacy extends far beyond his written work. This introduction has moved to talking about people he liked and admired. To understand this book one needs to know that was the key to how he worked. His research and academic study were dependent on his many sources and contacts and the information he sifted from them. Scholars he taught, had friendships with, or debated with—many online via the scholarly forum H-net and other platforms—included some of our most distinguished and renowned figures in Buddhist studies today. He had a quirky interest in middle names. He jokingly saw them as the key to the real character of the person involved, where the first name just gave the outward appearance. His first name, Lance, was clearly apt. His second name, Selwyn, is a Middle English word probably composed of *sele*, a “hall” or “castle,” and “*wine*” or “*wyn*,” a friend or sea friend: Selwyn is a friend in the halls. It was perhaps this part of Lance that liked communal meeting places where he could be friends with people, whether in college, in coffee bars, in his own or others’ houses, or even in online forums. His sense of common purpose with other academics, and his capacity to see them as friends all the more for their being sparring partners—he could look slightly disconsolate when people agreed with him too much—were crucial elements in what he left behind. This attitude shaped his academic work; this book was the product of his own research but also of so many interchanges of that kind.

<!-- Page 19 -->

This book came out of Lance’s teaching. As one undergraduate said to me recently, works like that tend to be the most readable and useful. For those who are reading this book who are not academically inclined, it is helpful to know that the audience for the lectures he gave included many members of the general public, who were primarily practitioners of many kinds, and he tried to address them. While he does not discuss his own personal practice, he knew that many people who attended his talks would want to understand what he was saying from that point of view. He answered questions from everyone patiently and with care, often sensing such needs. To those who come from this perspective, it is worth saying he felt people deserved illuminating philological explanation of terms that could be useful, a way of understanding the descriptions of meditation states discussed in the canon, and a clear historical account of how traditions and styles developed in Southern Buddhist meditation so they could place their own. I think this book will prove a very helpful resource in all those regards for such readers.

Lance avoided sentimentality, perceiving it as a threat to real feeling. But he could let his English reserve slip sometimes. A year or so before Lance’s death, a distinguished lady scholar from China visited him. I noticed on his mantelpiece afterward a beautiful tiny calligraphy set she had given him. He handed it to me, and it was clear he was unusually moved: “It is just so apt,” he said. The Chinese calligrapher works within a great tradition; aware of that, they try to get the brushstroke right to enact the teaching in that very moment. Lance felt that his job as a scholar was to contribute to such a great historical endeavor and lineage. He wanted people to enjoy finding out about Buddhism, its terminology, its language, and its praxis, and he wanted to help them do this as best he could. I don’t think he wanted the focus to be on him, but rather on what he said and what people could make of the clues we have about early and later Buddhism. This is what he wrote as the afterword in a small book on the *abhidhamma*, compiled by some people in a group he had led.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-7 "note") It is a good way to describe this book on meditation:

<!-- Page 20 -->

> I would like to say that I feel great happiness at having been able to take part in this work. These deeper aspects of dhamma are profound and work on them is both profound and worthwhile for its own sake. Perhaps one could compare it to the building over many generations of the great mediaeval cathedrals of the West and the great monuments and temples of Asia. Even to take part in the clearing of the site might appropriately give rise to gladness and awe.

Lance received special acknowledgment during his lifetime when, in 2013, he was awarded an honorary doctorate by Mahachulalongkorn University, Bangkok. After his death, obituaries appeared in Buddhist studies publications. A few years later, in 2019, editors Dr. Naomi Appleton and Professor Peter Harvey published a commemorative volume entitled *Buddhist Path, Buddhist Teachings: Studies in Memory of L. S. Cousins*. A collection of essays by friends, colleagues, and students influenced by Lance, the book’s topics include narrative literature, *abhidhamma*, and meditation teaching—a testament to the range and depth of his scholarship.

It has been a great honor to me to be asked to edit this book. I would like to express my thanks to Randal and Halla, and the rest of Lance’s family, for letting me do so. I would also like to thank Nikko Odiseos for asking me about it and reading it on behalf of Shambhala Publications. Thanks also to Peter Schumacher at Shambhala, who made scrupulously careful suggestions and edits. We were lucky to have Dr. Grace Burford as our alert and thorough copyeditor. I apologize sincerely for any errors that may have occurred at my end.

Translations are Lance’s except where otherwise stated. We have decided to retain Lance’s system of notation as well. The one exception to this is that we have also cited *Majjhima-nikāya* and *Dīgha-nikāya sutta*s by number in addition to the name (D 3, M 16, etc.), except where a specific reference is needed. The book also cites a number of works that are as yet untranslated. Those that have no English translation I have made titles for, where possible using a translation that Lance preferred for each term. Titles of untranslated Pali texts in this book appear with an asterisk in front of them: *Niddesa*, for instance, becomes \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*). For ease of reading, works that may be familiar to many readers in an English translation have been given with both the English and the Pali or Sanskrit title.

<!-- Page 21 -->

Sarah Shaw

Wolfson College, Oxford

January 2021

# Introduction

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

Buddhism today is divided into three main traditions—the Northern or Tibetan tradition, current especially in Tibet, Mongolia, and neighboring areas; the Eastern or East Asian tradition that developed in China, Vietnam, Korea, and Japan; and the Southern or Theravāda school, which is dominant in Sri Lanka, Burma, Thailand, Laos, and Cambodia with minorities in contiguous countries. Each of these is derived from particular aspects of ancient Buddhism, which was established in various parts of South and Central Asia from the time of the Buddha (c. 400 b.c.e.) until at least the fourteenth century c.e. This book concentrates on the Southern Buddhist tradition and its antecedents in ancient Buddhism, although similar books could certainly be written on each of the other two main varieties of Buddhism.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-8 "note")

Southern Buddhism is often referred to as Theravāda Buddhism, although the ancient term “Theravāda” has only relatively recently been adopted as a collective name for this tradition. The first person to utilize the term appears to have been the British monk Ānanda Metteyya (Alan Bennett, 1872–1923), the first Western-born Buddhist monk to visit Europe.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-9 "note") We cannot, however, rule out the possibility that it was already in use in Burma and adopted by him from his sources there. In any case, it is now widely used to refer to that form of Buddhism whose monastic order derives from the lineage of the ancient Theriyas in Ceylon and elsewhere and whose texts have been preserved by that lineage in the Pali language.

This book explores meditation in the Southern Buddhist tradition from its beginnings at the time of the Buddha and after, with a survey of some of the many schools of meditation practice found in recent Theravāda Buddhism and an interlude in Central Asia.

L. S. Cousins

# 1. *Jhāna* in the Commentaries

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

## **On the Very Idea of “Meditation”**

The four *jhāna*s, or meditative absorptions, are described in the Pali commentaries of the fourth or fifth century c.e. This is a method that later sources know as “form meditation” or “calm (*samatha*) meditation.”

<!-- Page 22 -->

The term “meditation” itself can be problematized. This becomes very clear if we now ask the question: To what term in Pali or Sanskrit does the word “meditation” correspond? The most obvious answer is the term *bhāvanā*—“practice,” or more literally, “bringing into being.” But this has a much wider usage than the English word “meditation,” which is taken to refer to techniques of mental cultivation of rather specific kinds. *Bhāvanā*, however, refers to the bringing into being not only of mental states but also of the whole eightfold path, including such things as right speech, action, and livelihood. It is much more than just a mental technique.

If one goes on to ask when the word “meditation” comes into use for Buddhist contexts in English, the answer seems to be around the early 1930s and among a small network of Buddhists and Buddhist scholars in London and Paris. At this point in time there was a single Buddhist group in Paris under the leadership of Grace Constant Lounsbery, a Buddhist Lodge (later to become the Buddhist Society) in London led by Christmas Humphreys, and a Buddhist Vihāra supported by the Mahabodhi Society in Ceylon. A number of mainly Sinhalese and Indian monks stayed there during the course of the 1930s.

It is noticeable that at least two of these had regular meditation meetings (under that name) around this time. From the Buddhist Lodge’s Meditation Circle eventually emerged in 1935 a book, entitled *Concentration and Meditation*.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-10 "note") In the same year Lounsbery published her *Buddhist Meditation in the Southern School*. It is clear, however, that a major underlying influence in all this was the Sinhalese monk Ven. Paravahera Vajirañāṇa, who eventually went to Cambridge to do a PhD on the subject of meditation with the Pali scholar E. J. Thomas. He is frequently cited by Lounsbery and also by W. Y. Evans-Wentz (of Jesus College, Oxford) in his introduction to Lounsbery’s book. With the 1956 publication in London and New York of Edward Conze’s *Buddhist Meditation*, the term becomes very widespread, if not universal. We should note that this was written in the 1950s during the period of Conze’s maximal involvement with the Buddhist Lodge, by then renamed the Buddhist Society.

<!-- Page 23 -->

But where does it come from? The ultimate answer is very clear and has its roots in medieval Christian mysticism. It derives from the Latin writings of the influential twelfth-century Augustinian prior Richard of St. Victor. Of course, the word itself is much older and intrinsically means “repeated thinking” on some subject, as in the famous *Meditations* of the Roman emperor Marcus Aurelius.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-11 "note") And this wider usage continues. Yet the work of Richard, a native of the British Isles, was influential among later Christian writers on mysticism and was certainly well known during the revival of interest in Christian mysticism during the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. But how does it come to influence Buddhist circles?

The use of the English word “meditation” to render the Sanskrit *dhyāna* seems to have first been popularized by Swami Vivekananda (1863–1902) after his attendance at the Parliament of Religions in Chicago in 1893. It is employed together with “concentration” to render *dhāraṇā* already in his first book.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-12 "note") The usage must be partly due to the Theosophical and Universalist influences to which the Swami was exposed in this period. It continued to be adopted in various translations of the *Yogasūtra* and subsequently in the widely available writings of Ernest Wood (1883–1965).

## **The Account in the Commentaries**

Our first inquiry must be as to the aim of *jhāna* practice: “This mind, O *bhikkhu*s, is radiant, but it is stained by stains that arrive as visitors from outside.”[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-13 "note") This passage from the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* is frequently referred to in the commentaries.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-14 "note") They understand it to mean that the natural state of the mind (*pakati-mano*) is pure and peaceful, free from all impurities and immoral states. The stains are seen as arriving spontaneously or superimposed. For Theravāda this natural state of mind is the *bhavaṅga-citta*, the state of the mind, or consciousness, in deep, dreamless sleep.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-15 "note")

<!-- Page 24 -->

The purpose of concentration in this context is aptly stated by Vajirañāṇa: “The mind which is wholly given to a single perception of a salutary kind becomes purely radiant and illuminant in its original state.”[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-16 "note")

We already find in a late canonical work, \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*),[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-17 "note") the formal distinction stressed by later commentaries between the non-lasting suppression (*vikkhambhana*) achieved by the *jhāna*s and the final extirpation (*samuccheda*) of all unskillful tendencies caused by the paths and fruits. This distinction is already implicit in the *sutta* collections (*Nikāya*s), even with reference to the formless attainments.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-18 "note") At AN III 394–97 we find the impermanence of the four form (*rūpa*) *jhāna*s firmly stressed, and numerous other examples could no doubt be cited. *Jhāna* practice is, then, intended only as a means to prepare the mind for understanding. It is not a goal in itself in the Buddhist context.

A considerable moral preparation is in many cases stressed in the *sutta*s and this is followed by the commentaries. The *Sāmaññaphala-sutta* (D 2) gives the following preliminaries: the *bhikkhu* must observe the moral precepts (*sīla-sampanna*);[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-19 "note") he must guard the sense doors (*indriyesu gutta-dvāra*), possess mindfulness and clear comprehension (*satisampajañña*), be content (*santuṭṭha*), and dwell in a suitably secluded place. He then suppresses the five hindrances (*nīvaraṇa*s) and acquires the opposing qualities. More often these preliminaries seem to be taken for granted in the *sutta*s and only the hindrances are mentioned. The commentaries hold that the hindrances alone are specifically opposed to the attainment of *jhāna*.

### ***The Five Hindrances***

These five hindrances may be looked at in more detail. The first is *abhijjhā*, “longing for things,” or more frequently *kāma-cchanda*, “desire for objects of the five senses.” The second is *vyāpāda-padosa*, “the corruption of ill will.” According to the commentary, this is synonymous with anger (*kodha*).[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-20 "note") The third is *thīna-m-iddha*, which may originally have meant “increase of sleepiness,” but at a very early date came to be understood as a *dvandva* compound, with two qualities of equal status: “sloth and torpor.”[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-21 "note") The fourth, *uddhacca-kukkucca*, “excitement and remorse,” or “excitement and depression,” seems to refer to mental fluctuations somewhat similar to bipolar disorder—that is, the mind becomes overexcited and subsequently drops into a depressed state.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-22 "note") The last, *vicikicchā*, is literally “desire to discern,” but has the sense of “incapacitating doubt.”[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-23 "note") The simile is given of the traveler in a desert who out of fear of imaginary robbers stops his journey or even turns back. Fear of the unknown is perhaps also implied.

<!-- Page 25 -->

The removal of these obstacles is no mean task in itself, nor is a merely negative removal intended. In the *Sāmaññaphala-sutta*, the removal of ill will involves also “sympathy for the good of all beings” (*sabba-pāṇa-bhūta-hitānukampī*), and similar changes are required in the removal of the other hindrances.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-24 "note") In another *sutta*, the five hindrances are directly equated to the stains or defilements (*upakkilesa*s) mentioned previously.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-25 "note") They are elsewhere likened to canals around a mountain stream that have to be shut off if the stream (of the mind) is to lose its turbulence.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-26 "note")

The removal of “sloth and torpor” is of particular interest. The phrase used in the *Sāmaññaphala-sutta* is “perceiving light, mindful, clearly conscious, he purifies the mind from sloth and torpor.” *The Book of Analysis* (*Vibhaṅga*) does not seem to consider this as anything more than a metaphor for clarity of thought. The commentary to the *Dīgha-nikāya* explains it more literally: “endowed with pure perception free from the hindrances, capable of perceiving light, which is seen by night as by day.”[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-27 "note") Light phenomena of various kinds are characteristic of mysticism, and of many unusual states of mind.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-28 "note")

In the commentarial literature, the abandoning of the five hindrances is regarded as synonymous with the attainment of the first stage of concentration (*samādhi*), known as access or approach (*upacāra*). The expression “approach *jhāna*” is also occasionally employed, but more often the term *jhāna* is reserved for full absorption (*appaṇā*), which we shall consider later.

### ***The Three Kinds of* Nimitta**

Of some interest is the concept of the *nimitta*.[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-29 "note") This, in the older literature, seems to denote the object of meditation, of whatever kind.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-30 "note") In the commentarial literature we find an important distinguishing of three types of *nimitta*: first, the preparatory (*parikamma-*) *nimitta* is the initial object—a prepared disc, a part of the body, the breath, and so on. In modern times, objects such as a candle, a bowl of flowers, or a Buddha image are also used.

<!-- Page 26 -->

Eventually it becomes possible to retain a visual or other representation of the preparatory object. This is the acquired (*uggaha-*) *nimitta*, which, according to the commentarial literature, is visible whether the eyes are open or closed and is perceived “through the mind door as if seeing with the eye”—that is, an eidetic image of the object.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-31 "note")

Thirdly there arises the semblance (*paṭibhāga-*) *nimitta*. This is a more abstract form that lacks irrelevant detail still present in the acquired *nimitta*. It is described as soft and delicate, much purer than the previous one.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-32 "note") It is a pure concept, lacking physical reality and deriving from ideation (*sañña-ja*).[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-33 "note") If then the object of concentration is, for example, the idea of blueness (*nīla-kasiṇa*), the semblance *nimitta* arises as soon as this idea is brought to mind in the same way as the reflection of the face appears immediately upon glancing at a mirror. It is referred to as the semblance *nimitta* here in two senses: (a) because it bears a general resemblance to the acquired *nimitta*, and (b) because it arises immediately with the idea like a reflection in a mirror.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-34 "note")

The later *abhidhamma* manuals summarize this process in verses, as in this excerpt from \**The Delimitation of Mind and Matter* (*Nāmarūpapariccheda*):[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-35 "note")

This (meditation) practice is fourfold—preparatory,

and after that preparatory concentration, approach, and absorption. (400)

They explain that there are just three kinds of *nimitta*—the preparation *nimitta*, and after that the acquired and the semblance [*nimitta*s]. (401)

For the novice *yogin* apprehending the initial *nimitta*, the *kasiṇa*

and other [meditation subjects] are called the preparatory *nimitta*. (402)

Now when someone begins (meditation) practice with that *nimitta*,

the (meditation) practice is first of all called preparatory. (403)

When that has been acquired by the mind and known clearly at the mind door, then it is said that the acquired *nimitta* has fully arisen. (404)

<!-- Page 27 -->

The (meditation) practice is explained as “preparatory concentration” when it is freed from the five [sense] doors and concentrated upon that *nimitta*. (405)

It is explained as the semblance *nimitta* when it arises as a form of acquisition, free from real objects and produced by (meditation) practice. (406)

After that, mind door [consciousness] occurs with that as its object,

leaving visual and other objects and interspersed with the passive mind. (407)

When concentration has reached its culmination and is free from defilement,

it is referred to as approach concentration, which belongs to the sense-sphere. (408)

Absorption arises, produced by the power of (meditation) practice

due to approach concentration on the semblance *nimitta*. (409)

These three *nimitta*s are not found as such in the *sutta*s. The commentaries, however, understand the term “form” or “matter” (*rūpa*) in the canonical list of the eight spheres of mastery (*abhibhāyatana*s) to refer to the preparatory object and the acquired *nimitta*[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-36 "note")—and some of the specific objects are mentioned occasionally.[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-37 "note") *The Points of Controversy* (*Kathāvatthu*) states that “concentration arises in one enjoying mental objects as such”[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-38 "note") and this was certainly understood later as referring to the semblance *nimitta*.[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-39 "note") The *Abhidharmakośa* of the Sautrāntika attributes to the meditation masters (*yogâcārā*) the teaching that “through the power of concentration there arises for the practitioner of *dhyāna* a form (*rūpa*), which is the object of concentration and which cannot be located because it is not an object of the sense of sight nor does it impose resistance [to the touch] because it is non-spatial.”[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-40 "note") Clearly this is based upon some similar experience.

Nevertheless, the most striking evidence for the antiquity of the concept of the *nimitta* is to be found in the *Upakkilesa-sutta*.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-41 "note") Here emphasis is laid on the necessity of perceiving both a shining light (*obhāsa*) and the appearance of forms (*rūpa*s).[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-42 "note") This perception is hindered by a series of defilements or stains (*upakkilesa*s): doubt, lack of attention, sloth and torpor, terror (*chambhitattaṃ*), elation, depression (*duṭṭhulla*), too much or too little energy, desire, various perceptions, too much thinking about forms (*rūpa*s). The commentary understands this list as a continuing process in ordered succession. Interestingly, the doubt is attributed to seeing varying forms, the terror to seeing (demoniac) *dānava*s, *rakkhasa*s, snakes, and the like, while the desire is attributed to seeing a company of *deva*s.[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-43 "note") These experiences are ascribed to increasing the light (*ālokaṃ vaḍḍhetvā*). Some of Melford Spiro’s contemporary Burmese informants reported visionary occurrences.[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-44 "note")

<!-- Page 28 -->

In sum, it may then be assumed that although the terminology around the concept of the *nimitta* is late, the substance it describes is much earlier in date. Whether this is so or not, for these exercises, the arrival of the third kind of *nimitta* is synonymous with reaching the stage of access, or approach *jhāna*. It is important to note that this description in *The Path of Purification* (*Visuddhimagga*) is specific to meditation on one of the ten *kasiṇa*s—it occurs somewhat differently in the case of other meditation objects that involve the repetition of formulas or those that develop particular emotional states. The *Visuddhimagga* takes the *kasiṇa* as the basis for its normative description and it is convenient to follow it in this.

A difference of view Buddhaghosa cites from these ancient commentaries, the old *Aṭṭhakathā*, makes it clear that the concept of the *nimitta* is much older than the time of Buddhaghosa. His comments on one *sutta* suggest that the concentration produced by breathing mindfulness should be carefully attended to if one desires to develop various states, including the four formless attainments.[[36]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-45 "note") These will be discussed in chapter 3, but the commentary to this *sutta* raises the question whether breathing mindfulness (as opposed to meditation on colors and elements) can lead to the formless attainments. Cūlābhaya (a leading figure in the Mahāvihāra around the first century b.c.e.) answered in the affirmative.[[37]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-46 "note") He comments that this is possible because the *nimitta* of in-and-out breathing is known as like a star, a pearly ball, and so on.[[38]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-47 "note") Another leading figure, Cūḷanāga, rejects this, and Buddhaghosa adopts his stance in the *Visuddhimagga*. The view of Cūḷanāga is not discussed in the *The Path of Freedom* (*Vimuttimagga*), a meditation manual by the arahant Upatissa, which appears to be slightly older than Buddhaghosa’s work.

### ***Joy***

Once approach concentration has been reached, the task is to gather the five *jhāna* factors. Of significant interest is the third factor—*pīti*, or joy. The concept of *pīti* is perhaps central to the understanding of early Buddhism. Although a frequently employed technical term in a number of differing contexts, its importance has often been overlooked. This may have the result that early Buddhism comes to seem a somewhat dry and unemotional form of religion, even perhaps desiccated and intellectual. In the *Saṅgīti-sutta* (D 33; DN III 141) we find five alternative situations that can lead to great joy, tranquility, and related states. The first four are hearing a sermon, teaching *dhamma*, reciting *dhamma*, and studying *dhamma*. Only the fifth, *samādhi-nimitta*, is a subject of meditation as such. Each of these sets in motion a process leading from the good feeling that arises after abandoning the hindrances, through joy, to tranquility and happiness, finally bringing the mind to enter concentration. To these others can easily be added—worship of relics, recollecting the qualities of the Triple Gem, and in later times the cult of the image. From these ingredients derives the ceremonial and devotional side of Theravāda Buddhism, so often underestimated today.

<!-- Page 29 -->

How then do the commentaries interpret the experience of *pīti*? The description given in the *Visuddhimagga* is worth careful consideration. Here five kinds of *pīti* are enumerated in ascending order. Minor (*khuddikā*) *pīti* is able to excite the hairs on the body. According to the sub-commentary, it does so lightly and subsides, not to reappear.[[39]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-48 "note") Momentary (*khaṇikā*) *pīti* is like flashes of lightning from time to time. Rising (*okkantikā*[[40]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-49 "note")) *pīti* rises up the body again and again, as a wave rises up the seashore and subsides. Transporting (*ubbegā*) *pīti* (or one might translate frightening *pīti*) is powerful.[[41]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-50 "note") It makes the body buoyant and reaches the degree of causing it to jump in the air. The commentaries cite two examples of this levitation, both brought on by strong religious devotion. The last kind, pervading (*pharaṇā*) *pīti*, pervades all through the whole body so that it is like a bag filled with air by blowing or like a rock-cave flooded by a great mass of water. According to the sub-commentary, “Pervading [*pīti*] is much more excellent than transporting [*pīti*], due to absence of motion and long-lastingness.”[[42]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-51 "note") It is the fifth kind that leads to full *jhāna*.[[43]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-52 "note")

The modern literature in Thailand and elsewhere contains much more detail as to the physical and visionary side effects of *pīti*.[[44]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-53 "note") In any case, the intensity of the experience is already made clear in the frequent canonical similes that stress the superiority both in degree and in quality of the pleasure arising from *jhāna* to any pleasure deriving from the senses. Reference is probably intended here particularly though not exclusively to sexual pleasure.

A translation of the term *pīti* merely as “pleasurable interest” or as “zest” or “exuberance” is then inadequate from the point of view of conveying the intensity of the later stages of *pīti*. The commentaries interpret the word *pāmujja*, which precedes *pīti* in the canonical description given above, as immature *pīti* (*taruṇa-pīti*). This would seem to require a fairly strong translation for *pīti* itself. In some ways the most appropriate English word for the general *sutta* usage would be “love,” a term of suitably wide connotation in English, but if this is felt to be now too debased in modern usage, no doubt the best alternative where translation is required is the more usual “joy.”[[45]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-54 "note")

<!-- Page 30 -->

Particularly important here is the common Indian use of *prīti* to refer to the joyful and blissful aspects of family and marital love. In Pali a similar distinction is found between *pīti* associated with sensual passions (*sāmisa*) and *pīti* dissociated from sensual passions (*nirāmisa*). *Pīti* is also explained by the term “bliss” (*ānanda*);[[46]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-55 "note") the *Yogasūtra* substitutes this for *pīti* as a factor of concentration (*samādhi*).

It is interesting to compare the description of this experience of meditation given by one of Spiro’s Burmese informants: “There is a tingling sensation in every part of the body, much more pleasurable than any sexual experience, including orgasm. In orgasm, the pleasure is restricted to one organ, whereas this meditation pleasure suffuses the entire body; it is felt in all of one’s organs.”[[47]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-56 "note")

A few more of the commentarial glosses seem worth noting. In the *Vimuttimagga*, it is explained as a sixfold satisfying (*pīṇanā*) of the mind produced through sense desire, through faith, through absence of depression (*akukkucca*), through a secluded state (*viveka*), through concentration (*samādhi*), or through the factor of awakening (*bojjhaṅga*).[[48]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-57 "note") This is not taken up in later works, where *pīti* is usually explained as having two senses: the sense of causing delight (*tappeti*) (where *pīti* < Sanskrit *prī*) and the sense of increasing (*vaḍḍheti*) (where *pīti* < Sanskrit *pī*). It has the characteristic mark of great fondness, the properties of strengthening or delighting or suffusing mind and body, and the manifestation of a state of elation.[[49]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-58 "note") “The property of suffusing” is understood as meaning “the property of pervading the whole body with subtle forms (*paṇītehi rūpehi*).”[[50]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-59 "note")

Another definition is “satisfaction of the heart.”[[51]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-60 "note") According to the *Dīgha-nikāya* commentary, *pīti* arises “causing the whole body to shake,” but the sub-commentary understands this to refer specifically to the stage of rising *pīti*.[[52]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-61 "note") An *udāna* is also explained as an utterance produced spontaneously under the influence of *pīti*.[[53]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-62 "note") Again *pīti* accompanied by greed brings excitement and is an obstacle to *jhāna* in contrast to *pīti* without greed.[[54]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-63 "note")

<!-- Page 31 -->

It is clear that *pīti* refers to all the stages of joy between a mildly pleasurable interest and an ecstatic transport of delight. As suggested above, the English word that historically conveyed this range of meaning (mild interest, sexual pleasure, mystical love) is love, although *pīti* refers rather to a state or experience than to a feeling directed toward an object. Indeed, the commonplace contrast in mysticism between carnal love and mystical love bears a family resemblance to the various uses of *pīti*. *Pīti* is perhaps too central and fundamental a concept in early Buddhism for any portmanteau translation to be generally acceptable.

### ***The Factors or Limbs of* Jhāna**

In the present context, the particular importance of *pīti* lies in its inclusion in descriptions of the first *jhāna*.
