This is described in the *Majjhima*-*nikāya* as “abandoning five factors, possessing five factors.”[[55]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-64 "note") Not surprisingly, the former are the five hindrances, while the latter five factors given here constitute the standard list of factors of *jhāna* as repeated in later texts. It may be noted that we do not find here or elsewhere in the Pali Canon the one-for-one correspondence of the two lists cited in the commentaries: “it is said in the \**What Belongs to the Basket of Teachings* (*Peṭaka*) that concentration is the enemy of desire for objects of sense, *pīti* of ill will, thinking (*vitakka*) of sloth and torpor, happiness (*sukha*) of excitement and remorse, exploring (*vicāra*) of incapacitating doubt.”[[56]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-65 "note") This passage appears to have been lost from our text of the paracanonical *Piṭaka Disclosure* (*Peṭakopadesa*).[[57]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-66 "note")

This correspondence is spelled out in the later *\*Entrance to Abhidhamma* (*Abhidhammāvatāra*) of Buddhadatta:[[58]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-67 "note")

The mind is greedy for various objects of sense due to sensual purpose,

wandering hither and thither like a monkey in the forest. (789)

Because of unifying (*samādhāna*) the mind on just one object,

concentration is called the opponent of sensual purpose. (790)

<!-- Page 32 -->

Due to its goodness of feeling (*pāmojjabhāva*) and its nature being to cool,

joy is spoken of as the opponent of ill will. (791)

Because it spreads out (*savipphārikattā*) as thought free from sensuality and the like,

thinking is explained as the opponent of sloth and sleepiness. (792)

Because their nature is unquiet and because happiness is itself very peaceful,

happiness is the opponent of the pair of excitement and remorse. (793)

Because it resembles wisdom, exploring that has the characteristic of constant scanning

is shown as the opponent of doubt. (794)

This is explained in the sub-commentaries as a particular way of exposition, by which they mean that it does not accord with strict *abhidhamma* analysis. So Dhammapāla explains (Vism-mhṭ II 494):

> For happiness is not itself an opponent of excitement and remorse, but it is an opponent because it is a condition for concentration, which is an opponent. And exploring is not an opponent as wisdom or commitment are, but it resembles an opponent [that is, wisdom]. Likewise, joy is not a direct opponent of ill will as non-hate is, but is spoken of as an opponent because it is associated with non-hate.[[59]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-68 "note")

Some of the sources preserved in Sanskrit present the overcoming of the five hindrances slightly differently. They suggest that desire for objects of sense is overcome by meditation on the uglinesses (*aśubha*s), ill will by meditating on loving-kindness, sloth and torpor by increasing light, excitement and remorse by fixing and concentrating the mind or by calm (*śamatha*), and doubt by contemplating conditioned origination and similar teachings.[[60]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-69 "note") A comparable Pali version is the “Division on Abandoning the Hindrances” (*Nīvaraṇapahāna-vagga*) of the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*. This declares that the hindrances are overcome respectively by means of the sign of ugliness (*asubhanimitta*), the heart’s deliverance of loving-kindness, the three aspects of vigor (*viriya*), mental calming, and appropriate attention.[[61]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-70 "note")The first two of the factors of *jhāna* are *vitakka* and *vicāra*, translated above as “thinking” and “exploring.” Historically these are little more than two synonyms for “thinking,” but in Buddhist usage they are given a distinct meaning that is followed also by the *Yogasūtra*.[[62]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-71 "note") The first denotes the directing of the mind to an object of thought or imagination, while the second indicates rather the subtle exploration that retains the object and makes it clearer.[[63]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-72 "note") They are also described as the constructors of speech (*vacī-saṅkhāra*).[[64]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-73 "note")

<!-- Page 33 -->

The next two factors of *jhāna* are *pīti* and *sukha*. In the technical sense with which we are concerned, the former is differentiated as “delight through obtaining sight of the thing desired” as opposed to *sukha*, which is “experiencing the flavor of what has been obtained.”[[65]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-74 "note") The difference intended appears to be between a relatively excited state (*cetaso ubbilāvitā*) and a more calm and peaceful type of happiness.

The remaining factor, literally “one-pointedness of mind” (*cittass’ ek’-aggatā*), is considered to be synonymous with concentration as a *jhāna* factor. It refers to a state in which the mind is absorbed in a single object. In the present context, it is the ability to keep the attention, without wavering or trembling, aware only of the object of meditation. It should be noticed that this fifth factor, although absent from the ancient formula of the first *jhāna*, is considered by the commentators to be implied by the epithet “born of concentration” (*samādhi-ja*) applied to the second *jhāna*.

### ***Approach Concentration* (Upacāra-Samādhi)**

Fully developed, these five factors constitute *jhāna*, but it is important to note that they may already be present in a weaker form in normal consciousness. So we read in the *Visuddhimagga*, “But although these five factors are also [present] at the moment of approach and moreover are stronger in approach than in normal consciousness, they are even stronger here [that is, in absorption] than in approach and acquire the characteristics of the form sphere.”[[66]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-75 "note")

How exactly are we to understand this distinction between approach concentration (*upacāra*-*samādhi*) and absorption concentration (*appaṇā-samādhi*)? The description of the commentaries is precise:

> In approach the factors have not become strong. Just as a baby boy lifts himself up and stands but again and again falls to the ground because his limbs (*aṅga*) have not become strong, even so, when approach has arisen, because the factors (*aṅga*) have not become strong, the mind at one moment makes the *nimitta* its object and at another moment descends into *bhav’-aṅga*.[[67]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-76 "note")

<!-- Page 34 -->

The process of developing concentration has reached a point at which there is still a tendency for the mind, as in ordinary consciousness, to move in and out of *bhavaṅga.* But when full absorption is reached,

> just as a strong man might rise from his seat and stand even for a whole day, even so, when absorption concentration has arisen, the mind breaks the flow of *bhav’-aṅga* at a single attempt and stands for a whole night and day, carrying on by means of a succession of wholesome *javana*s.

Stripped of the technical terminology of Theravādin psychology, this is to say that the state of *jhāna* is not merely conscious, it is much *more* conscious than the normal state of mind.

This requires some elaboration. According to the Theravādin explanation of the process of consciousness (*citta-vīthi*), the series of thought moments that produces the illusion of a continuous entity occurs in a complex manner, which may be approximately described as two principal modes together with a number of transitional modes.[[68]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-77 "note") The two most frequent modes are *bhavaṅga* and *javana*. The former is the type of consciousness met with in an unmixed form in deep dreamless sleep. We can call it the passive state of consciousness. *Javana*, or swift-moving consciousness, is the kind with which good or bad mental acts are normally performed: in short, the active state of consciousness. Ordinary consciousness consists of a complex admixture of the two principal modes together with the transitional modes. In this way the varying phenomena of perception can be ingeniously accounted for; for example, very clear perceptions involve a higher proportion of *javana* moments, while less clear perceptions (as in dreams or at the margins of attention) involve a lower proportion.[[69]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-78 "note")

In effect, normal consciousness consists of a constant flickering between *bhavaṅga* and *javana*. In the *jhāna* state, this flickering is brought to a stop and a form of *javana* consciousness alone continues. This is considered to be a higher consciousness (*mahaggata-citta*; *adhicitta*), particularly in comparison with the normal state in which less than half of the thought moments are consciously experienced. The bulk of the normal waking mental process is conceived of as below the threshold of awareness.

<!-- Page 35 -->

The term “approach,” or sometimes “access,” is not known to the earlier parts of the Pali Canon, although it does seem to be implied in *The Points of Controversy* (*Kathāvatthu*) and the concept is perhaps fully developed in *The Path of Discrimination* (*Paṭisambhidāmagga*)*.*[[70]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-79 "note") A very similar idea, but employing different terminology, is found in Sarvāstivādin literature.[[71]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-80 "note") This would seem to imply either that it is older than the literary evidence would suggest or that it is implicit in the *sutta* formulation.

Before taking leave of approach concentration, it is worth noting that from the arising of this stage, direct knowledge (*abhiññā*) occurs. It is therefore here that the first parting of the ways arises. From this point it is possible to embark upon insight practice without entering full *jhāna*. If *jhāna* practice is undertaken, it will be necessary to return from that in order to develop insight. This is the normative position of the commentaries, although some modern insight teachers suggest that only momentary concentration and not approach concentration is necessary for insight development.

### ***The Five Masteries***

The clear distinction given above by the commentaries needs some qualification. It would appear that for the beginner, absorption is only achieved for a very brief period.[[72]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-81 "note") Indeed, the first occurrence is for one moment only. Subsequently the length of time depends at first upon the quality and length of the preliminary exercises but ultimately upon the skill acquired in the exercise of the five masteries (*vasitā*). These are first recorded as a list in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga*, but they have a greater antiquity.[[73]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-82 "note") The intention appears to be to habituate the process of entry into absorption and to bring it completely under voluntary control.

The first of these, adverting (*āvajjanā*), appears to be the ability to bring the *jhāna* factors into consciousness at speed. In other words, the ability to reach a stage close to approach at will. The second, attaining (*samāpajjanā*), is the ability to enter into *jhāna* as desired. This would naturally last a period of time corresponding to the preliminary work. The third is will (*adhiṭṭhāna*), by which the meditator predetermines the period that the *jhāna* will last. Then comes emerging (*vuṭṭhāna*), which refers to proficiency that enables speed in the process of withdrawal from *jhāna.* The last is recollection (*paccavekkhaṇā*), which is apparently the ability to maintain the *jhāna* factors in consciousness continuously. According to the commentary, this process of withdrawal can be compared to waking from sleep.[[74]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-83 "note") Indeed the whole in its developed form is reminiscent of the ability of some individuals to go to sleep at will or for a predetermined duration. The final result is that the individual can enter and leave *jhāna* within seconds.

<!-- Page 36 -->

The meditator may then attempt to achieve the higher *jhāna*s. This is a process of gradual simplification by eliminating the factors until only one-pointedness of mind is left. In the very frequent old formula, seen for instance in the *Sāmaññaphala Sutta*, initial and sustained thought are eliminated to attain the second *jhāna*, *pīti* is eliminated to attain the third, and happiness to attain the fourth and last.[[75]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-84 "note") A variant list found occasionally in the *nikāya*s divides the first into two and combines the third and fourth into one.[[76]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-85 "note") Sometimes we find another list in three sections:

1. concentration (*samādhi*) with both initial and sustained thought, with only the latter, with neither
2. concentration with or without joy (*pīti*)
3. concentration with delight (*sāta*) or with equanimity (*upekkhā*).[[77]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-86 "note")

The Theravādin *abhidhamma* organizes this into an alternative list of five *jhāna*s. The Sarvāstivāda prefers to interpose a kind of halfway stage (*dhyānântara*) between the first and second *jhāna*s.[[78]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-87 "note") In any case, the difference seems to be mainly terminological, as it so often is with the disputes of the early schools. Both were clearly based upon the same material, which must therefore be assumed to be of some antiquity.

We should not, however, make the mistake of supposing that the list of *jhāna* factors represents the totality of the qualities experienced therein. The old formula itself adds a number of others at different stages. In particular, we may note internal repose (*ajjhattaṃ sampasāda*) for the second *jhāna*, mindfulness (*sati*) and clear comprehension (*sampajāna*) for the third, and purification of mindfulness by means of equanimity for the fourth. According to the commentaries, these qualities are present also in the first *jhāna* in lesser degree. In the case of mindfulness, the commentary lays some stress on the point: “For even mere approach does not succeed for one of confused mindfulness without clear comprehension, let alone absorption.”[[79]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-88 "note")

<!-- Page 37 -->

In this the commentaries are clearly following an old tradition. In the *Majjhima-nikāya* we find a list of *dhamma*s present in the first *jhāna* that includes among others energy (*viriya*), mindfulness, equanimity, and attention (*manasikāra*).[[80]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-89 "note") *Buddhist Psychological Ethics (Dhammasaṅganī*) gives a much longer list, which is identical with the list given for consciousness, defined as normal, happy, and wholesome.[[81]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-90 "note") So, rather surprisingly, the *abhidhamma* does not reckon that *jhāna* consciousness is strongly distinct in makeup from the better kinds of normal consciousness, as far as the accompanying factors (*cetasika*) are concerned. Good qualities are no doubt to be understood as much more powerful, and purer, through being unmixed subliminally with opposite qualities. Hatred in any form is totally absent. Moreover, physical pain is not felt. In fact, by the time the fourth *jhāna* is reached, physical pleasure, as well as both pleasant and unpleasant emotion, has been gradually eliminated.[[82]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-91 "note")

### ***Trance or Not?***

Some writers translate the term *jhāna* as “trance.” Given the way in which Buddhists use the expression, this seems quite inappropriate to me.[[83]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-92 "note") It is clear that the use of the term “trance” in this context is only possible if it is understood in a strictly medical sense as referring to specific physiological phenomena. *Jhāna* is certainly not trance in the sense of a dull stupor, nor does it involve catalepsy in the sense of “suspension of consciousness.” Even a lucid trance, if it were accompanied by the five hindrances, would be considered quite distinct from *jhāna* as an aspect of Buddhist practice.

As far as *jhāna* is concerned, it is by now amply clear that we are not dealing with an unconscious state. If we are to understand *jhāna* as being a trance state, it must be as a lucid trance. It is equally clear that, as described in the commentaries, it possesses many of the characteristics of trance. The mind does not perceive through the five senses and is incapable of speech from the first *jhāna*.[[84]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-93 "note") By the fourth, all bodily activities have ceased.[[85]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-94 "note") The movement of the breath is explicitly mentioned, and the heartbeat is doubtless implied.[[86]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-95 "note") A form of catalepsy of the body is the rule in *jhāna*. Later texts elevate this to the status of a proposition: “The *javana*s of absorption control even posture (*iriyā-patha*).”[[87]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-96 "note") The allied concept of mind-produced (*citta-samuṭṭhāna*) matter is used as the theoretical justification for such commonplaces of mysticism as survival without eating food, passing through the air, walking on water, and the like.[[88]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-97 "note")

<!-- Page 38 -->

Many of the phenomena ancillary to visionary states have also been recorded. Shaking of the body,[[89]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-98 "note") several different kinds of light,[[90]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-99 "note") the acquiring of a mental body,[[91]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-100 "note") and a special type of breathing distinct from normal breathing,[[92]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-101 "note") are all mentioned. Some of the miraculous occurrences—conversations with deities and the like—so frequent in the Pali Canon, are surely to be considered as genuine accounts of visionary experiences.[[93]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-102 "note") To these, one should perhaps add (in the development of insight) a relatively early stage at which meditators think they are already enlightened.[[94]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-103 "note") All these phenomena are more or less normal to visionary states the world over. Nowadays one would add to the list hypnotic states, drug-induced states, and experiences resulting from sensory deprivation.

After wandering afield into states, some of which are, in the Buddhist conception, far from *jhāna*, perhaps we should return and remind ourselves of the positive importance ascribed to the experience. Possibly we can accord some evidential value to the words of the Burmese Buddhist S. Z. Aung, written at the beginning of the twentieth century:

> Attainment in *jhāna* is a very important psychological moment, marking an epoch in his mental experience for the person who succeeds in commanding it. He has for the first time in his life tasted something unlike anything he has ever experienced before. The feeling is simply indescribable. He felt an entirely changed person, purged from the Hindrances. He was living a new, higher life, the life of a god of the heavens called *Rūpa*; experiencing the consciousness believed to be habitual to those dwelling there.[[95]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-104 "note")

We may, however, note that the technique of attainment of *jhāna* is informative as to its general role in Buddhist practice. It is apparently not possible to go directly from a lower *jhāna* to a higher.[[96]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-105 "note") In each case it is necessary to return to a more normal state of consciousness. The interpretation of this seems clear. *Jhāna* is conceived of as calming and purifying the normal consciousness. This achieved, a higher *jhāna* becomes possible, which in turn further purifies the normal state. The process obviously continues in some way, but, although it could no doubt be quite long-lasting, it cannot achieve a permanent improvement. To that extent it might seem valueless. However, the improved normal state of consciousness is itself of use in the process of increasing awareness by means of the vehicle of insight, which constitutes the eventual aim of Buddhist practice.

<!-- Page 39 -->

To sum up, *jhāna* practice involves the systematic induction of a very specific type of “trance” state, under controlled conditions, which necessarily require a previous clarity of consciousness and a well-balanced, happy frame of mind. The term *jhāna* is best left untranslated; or if rendered as “meditation,” this must be understood as quite distinct from the general English usage as employed in the title of this book.

### **Abhidhamma *and the Commentaries***

Those who base their understanding upon the *Visuddhimagga* and the other commentaries of the school of Buddhaghosa often misinterpret the intention of Buddhaghosa and his associates because they do not understand the *abhidhamma* base that underlies this work. Essentially, Buddhaghosa and his associates are providing an *abhidhamma* interpretation of materials derived from the *sutta*s. They provide an impressive exegesis, but it can mislead those without an adequate knowledge of how to apply *abhidhamma* ideas.

The basic system of the Pali *abhidhamma* is set out in the first two books of the *Abhidhamma*-*piṭaka*—*Buddhist Psychological Ethics* (*Dhammasaṅgaṇī* or *Dhammasaṅgaha*) and *The Book of Analysis* (*Vibhaṅga*). This system classifies the four *jhāna*s as form-level (*rūpāvacara*) arisings of consciousness (*cittuppāda*). These consist of an arising together of fifty-six *dhamma*s or mental events in the case of the first *jhāna*, with slight variations for the other three *jhāna*s.[[97]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-106 "note") All of these mental events differ in kind from the same mental events when they occur in non-*rūpāvacara* consciousness in the sense that they are more subtle and more refined. This kind of consciousness must originally have been called form-level (*rūpāvacara*) in the sense of “frequenting a meditation object,” although later it can be taken as referring to the subtle materiality possessed by *jhāna* practitioners when they are reborn as higher deities.

The basic description of form-level (*rūpāvacara*) consciousness omits reference to the six kinds of object, which it does list for consciousness that frequents desirable sense objects (*kāmāvacara*). This must be because *jhāna* does not have such objects—that is, in *jhāna* proper no visual images arise, no sounds, etc. This is spelled out in *abhidhamma* passages that specify the objects that particular types of consciousness can have. *Jhāna* is specified as not classifiable as having a past object or a present object or a future object—that is, its object is a concept.[[98]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-107 "note")

<!-- Page 40 -->

This can lead one to think of *jhāna* as a purely empty state in which only the *nimitta* is known. However, this is not quite correct. As Buddhadatta indicates (Abhidh-av 908), following the first momentary occurrence of *jhāna* comes a mind that recollects the *jhāna*. At this point in actual experience, both the object of the *jhāna* and the content of the *jhāna* consciousness are experienced together. This can be compared to the difference between the *abhidhamma* account in which seeing and hearing do not occur at the same time and how we usually experience it—namely, that we seem to see and hear at the same time. That is understood to be due to the rapidity of the succession of moments. Similarly, when the mind flicks in and out of absorption in this way, there is awareness of both the *nimitta* and the mental states that are focused upon it.

What Buddhaghosa then emphasizes is the practice of the five masteries. He cites a canonical simile (AN IV 418): a young inexperienced hill-dwelling cow that tries to go to unfamiliar pastures may come to grief, whereas the same cow when it is more experienced can do so without difficulty. Similarly, an inexperienced meditator who reaches the first *jhāna* and tries to go on to the second *jhāna* without first acquiring facility in the first *jhāna* may end up losing both. Buddhaghosa specifies this for the transition from the first to the second *jhāna*.[[99]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-108 "note") Although Buddhaghosa and Upatissa give this simile only for the movement on to the second *jhāna*, the intention of the *sutta* must be to apply it also to the subsequent stages—it goes on to list the remaining *jhāna*s and formless attainments, followed by the various kinds of higher knowledge (*abhiññā*).

Buddhaghosa also gives a specific *abhidhamma* analysis of the mastery of adverting. When the mind turns toward the *jhāna* factors after emerging from *jhāna*, it takes as its object the five factors in sequence. When there is full mastery, four or five moments of the active mind and two moments of the passive mind follow after the adverting to each *jhāna* factor.[[100]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-109 "note") What is meant here is that one becomes able to maintain active awareness of the five *jhāna* factors indefinitely with only brief periods in the passive mind. Normally, the mind is continually dropping into the passive mind between thoughts and sensory processes for indefinitely long periods.

<!-- Page 41 -->

Initially, however, the emphasis is on rapid access to absorption until it becomes possible to enter *jhāna* at will under all conditions. Buddhaghosa stresses frequency to gain speed and facility of control here. The aim is to enter and emerge in the time of a finger snap. This contrasts with the emphasis in some modern traditions that recommend sitting for long periods, even for many hours. For Buddhaghosa it is the adverting to and recollection of the *jhāna* factors that is important. Even so, he suggests that they should not be reviewed too much at first until the *jhāna* is fully familiar and controllable. Afterward, however, the succession to the second *jhāna* and onward is prepared by distinguishing *jhāna* factors that are gross from those that are more peaceful, so as to overcome attachment to the lower *jhāna*. Then the gross factors are suppressed by entering absorption again. This will then be a higher level of *jhāna*.

The commentaries differentiate the *jhāna*s with reference to the *jhāna* factors, following the *Abhidhamma-piṭaka*.[[101]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-110 "note") This can be set out as in [Table 1](#tbl1).

### **Table 1: *Jhāna*s by Factors**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| *Jhāna* | *Jhāna* factors |
| First | *vitakka*, *vicāra*, joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Second | joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Third | happiness, one-pointedness |
| Fourth | equanimity, one-pointedness |

What happens at the stage of approach to each of these *jhāna*s is not set out as clearly by the commentators, but we can organize them, as in [Table 2](#tbl2).

### **Table 2: Approach *Jhāna*s and *Jhāna* Factors**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| Approach *jhāna* | *Jhāna* factors |
| First | *vitakka*, *vicāra*, joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Second | [*vitakka*, *vicāra*,] joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Third | [joy,] happiness, one-pointedness |
| Fourth | [happiness,] equanimity, one-pointedness |

The square brackets indicate the factors to be abandoned. So, for example, *vitakka* and *vicāra* are abandoned only at the stage of second *jhāna* absorption. At the stage of approach, desire for them is relinquished—the compulsive need to think becomes much less at this point. The process is similar regarding joy and happiness in the approach stages for the remaining *jhāna*s.[[102]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-111 "note")

<!-- Page 42 -->

Each of these approach stages is preceded by the process of recollecting the *jhāna* factors as above and then seeing the factor(s) to be abandoned as gross.

### **Jhāna *Factors by the* Suttanta *Method***

There is sometimes a marked difference between the approach found in *sutta*s and that of the *abhidhamma*. The former is generally known as the *suttanta* approach. So, it is worth noting that the texts also recognize an alternative set of *jhāna* factors, which here follows the *suttanta* account more closely. [Table 3](#tbl3) presents this set of factors as it is given in the *Vibhaṅga* (Vibh 258–61).

### **Table 3: *Vibhaṅga Jhāna* Factors**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| *Jhāna* | *Jhāna* factors |
| First | *vitakka*, *vicāra*, joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Second | stilling (*vupāsamā*), joy, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Third | equanimity, mindfulness, clear comprehension, happiness, one-pointedness |
| Fourth | equanimity, mindfulness, one-pointedness |

The list given in the paracanonical *Peṭakopadesa* (Peṭ 139) and utilized in Sarvāstivādin sources differs mainly by the addition of neutral feeling to the list of factors for the fourth *jhāna*. The *Vimuttimagga* seems to have used the same list as the *Vibhaṅga*.

# 2. *Jhāna* in the Pali Canon

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

## **The Texts of the *Tipiṭaka***

According to the Theravāda Buddhist tradition, the Pali texts included in the Three Baskets (*Tipiṭaka*) were recited at the First Collective Recitation (Council) shortly after the Buddha passed away. Of the ancient Buddhist schools—traditionally eighteen—only the Theriya school, the early precursor to what we now term “Theravāda,” has preserved a complete version of its canon in an Indic language. Otherwise, we have large parts of the canon of one other school: the Sarvāstivāda. These are mostly preserved in Chinese and Tibetan, although considerable amounts of material from this in Sanskrit have been recovered, especially in the twentieth century. For other schools of ancient Buddhism, we have mainly material concerning their disciplinary rules for monks, in addition to much material of uncertain origin. We do not know the extent to which they had similar canons, and comparable traditions with regard to them, as those of the Theravāda. Nor do we know exactly what was contained in their collections.

<!-- Page 43 -->

The canon of the Theriya tradition (with the possible exception of a few late additions) was written down at a collective recitation in the first century b.c.e. in Ceylon. Sources that spell this out in detail, however, are rather late, and some scholars are skeptical as to the reliability of this tradition. The actual formalization of the content of the canon in writing may be a century or two later. We have no information as to when other schools first collected and wrote down their canonical works, but it is likely that individual texts were written down well before the attempt was made to organize complete collections in writing. Indeed, recent discoveries from the Northwest region of the Indian subcontinent have produced fragments of a number of texts. Many of these must date from the first and second centuries c.e., but some seem to be older.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-112 "note")

One text in particular—an *Avadāna* work in Gāndhārī Prakrit—has been radiocarbon dated to the middle of the second century b.c.e.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-113 "note") The legendary tales known as *Avadāna*s in Sanskrit (Pali *Apadāna*s) represent probably the latest phase of the canonical literature, which strongly supports the notion that the various genres of literature that can be found in the *sutta*s existed by the third century b.c.e. at the latest. Indeed, that is confirmed by the fact that in the third century b.c.e., the emperor Aśoka mentions in an inscription the names of a number of texts. We do not know for certain how far they correspond to texts known to us at a later date, but one does refer to a discourse addressed to the Buddha’s son concerning wrong speech. The specification of the subject matter indicates the existence of several discourses addressed to the Buddha’s son (Rāhula/Lāghula). This is exactly the case we find in the Pali Canon. So Aśoka’s inscription strongly suggests that well before his time, the *sutta*s and the *suttanta* method of discourses involving particular people on specific occasions were already well established in what is a now-recognizable collection of texts.

All Buddhist traditions agree on the existence of the First Collective Recitation (Council) shortly after the Buddha’s passing away, but details as to who was involved, where exactly it took place and what it did vary somewhat between schools. In any case, some kind of exercise to formulate the Buddha’s teachings probably took place around this time. Otherwise it would be difficult to account for the large number of shared teachings in the early Buddhist schools, even allowing for later ideas spreading from school to school.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-114 "note") We could call this a “First Council by any other name.” In fact, the process of recording the Buddha’s teaching in memorable oral forms probably began during his long lifetime of teaching, given that his contemporaries would have been well aware of techniques for memorizing and transmitting ideas without the use of writing.

<!-- Page 44 -->

Some scholars have attempted to identify a core of the teachings of the historical Buddha in the early texts.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-115 "note") The problems involved are very similar to those found in the “search for a historical Jesus,” and I do not attempt here to separate out the teachings of the Buddha and those of his early disciples. I also think that the oral nature of early Buddhist teachings may have involved a more flexible performative aspect in the early period.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-116 "note") This means that I do not attach too much significance to the variations between versions of the discourses as they are preserved by different schools. I believe that these often simply represent a survival of oral practices of slotting standard passages and phrases in and out to suit particular contexts.

## **The Context of *Jhāna* in the *Sutta-Piṭaka***

*Jhāna* is mentioned rather frequently in the discourses of the old canon—with something approaching 250 mentions in just the *Sutta-piṭaka*. This alone is enough to show how central it is to Buddhist practice in the early sources.

Often it is part of a general account of the path. This occurs in slightly varying versions in different discourses scattered across the four main *nikāya*s of the Pali version and similarly in the parallel Sanskrit texts. It usually begins with an account of precepts or training rules (*sīla*/*sikkhāpada*) for mendicants. Observing this basic lifestyle is seen as generating a sense of happiness that is not open to criticism (*anavajjasukha*) and should be followed by a careful guarding of the doors of the senses, which generates a further sense of happiness (*avyāsekasukha*), based on preserving a sense of integrity and not getting caught up in sense perception of either general features or details. Then mindfulness (*sati*) and clear comprehension (*sampajañña*) are described, involving acting with awareness in every kind of bodily activity, including even waking and sleeping or speech and silence. Many versions include also at this point contentment with a robe for covering and with subsisting simply on alms food.

Only after these preliminaries, usually qualified as “noble” (*ariya*), does the account turn to actual meditation. The practitioner resorts to a secluded lodging-place—examples given include a forest, a hill, a hillside cave, a cemetery, even just a heap of straw at the root of a tree. Having returned from the alms round and after taking a meal, the meditator sits in a cross-legged posture, setting the body upright and establishing mindfulness “in front” or “around the face” (*parimukhaṃ*).

<!-- Page 45 -->

No information is given as to the actual technique of meditation, but the abandoning of the five hindrances is described. In some cases, similes for each of the hindrances are added. “Desire for objects of the five senses” is compared to the burden of a debt. The “corruption of ill will” is described as like a sickness. “Sloth and torpor” is compared to being in prison, while “excitement and remorse” is depicted as a kind of slavery. As previously mentioned, “incapacitating doubt” is compared to finding oneself in a dangerous wilderness. In this way, overcoming the five hindrances is like becoming solvent, recovering health, release from prison, freedom from slavery, and finding a place of security.

This is a quite vivid description of the overcoming of the five hindrances. What is meant here is the overcoming of the resistance that naturally arises to any attempt to control or settle the mind. This is very clearly seen with the third hindrance. Anyone who has tried to study for a final exam might have experienced this. If they found themselves overcome by sleepiness as soon as they sat down to study—a sleepiness that mysteriously goes away whenever they stop studying and returns whenever they start again—they have experienced what is meant by this hindrance. Overcoming all five hindrances, even if temporary, is a major step in training the mind.

We find in the *Dīgha-nikāya* this description of the process leading to concentration—a description that occurs fairly widely in early Buddhist canonical literature:

> When one sees that these five hindrances have been abandoned in oneself, gladness (*pāmujja*) arises; to one who is glad, joy (*pīti*) arises; the body of one whose mind is joyful becomes calm; one whose body has become calm experiences happiness (*sukha*); the mind of one who is happy becomes concentrated (*samādhiyati*).[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-117 "note")

Elsewhere this sequence occurs in the context of developing the factors of awakening (*bojjhaṅga*s)[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-118 "note") and in related longer summaries of the path. It also occurs after the various recollections of the qualities of the Buddha, and before the experience of loving-kindness meditation and so on. In these contexts, it is widely known in Buddhist traditions.

<!-- Page 46 -->

The occurrence of this sequence prior to first *jhāna* in some texts of the Theriya tradition must be intended to imply some kind of process of concentration similar to the approach concentration of the commentaries. Its absence in some (or all?) other recensions is taken by some scholars to imply that it is a late addition. But it is quite possible that other schools whose texts do not survive also included this passage.

## **The First *Jhāna* Formula**

The stock passage describing the first *jhāna*, on the other hand, is given in almost identical form in the Pali texts and in surviving early Buddhist Sanskrit literature. We can translate it as follows:

> Quite secluded from sense desires/sense objects and secluded from unskillful states, one enters upon the first *jhāna* and remains there. The first *jhāna* has thinking and exploring and possesses joy and happiness that arise from seclusion.

Some texts, both in Pali and in Sanskrit, add this passage:

> It drenches, floods, fills, and suffuses this very body with the joy and happiness that arises from seclusion. Every part of this whole body is suffused with the joy and happiness that arise from seclusion.

The same texts add a simile:

> Just as, O king, a skilled bath attendant or assistant would sprinkle bath powder in a bronze vessel and knead it repeatedly with water. The ball of bath powder would become full of liquid, covered in liquid, suffused with liquid within and without, yet there would be no surplus to trickle out.
>
> In just the same way a monk drenches, floods, fills, and suffuses this very body with the joy and happiness that arises from seclusion. Every part of this whole body is suffused with the joy and happiness that arise from seclusion.

What is clear from this simile is that *jhāna* is not conceived of as an ordinary state of quiet thinking—it is clearly described as a state of contained ecstasy. That it was conceived as a special state at an early date is clear also from the *vinaya* literature.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-119 "note") The four *jhāna*s are listed among the higher states (*uttarimanussadhamma*). A Buddhist monk is explicitly prohibited from falsely claiming to have attained *jhāna*. If he does so, he is obtaining alms food by theft and can be expelled from the order. Even if he has attained such a state, he is prohibited from telling someone who is not a fully ordained monk.

<!-- Page 47 -->

## **Debating *Jhāna***

In recent decades there has been a fair amount of discussion among scholars of the nature of *jhāna*. It is probably fair to say that the majority opinion these days, especially among non-Buddhist scholars, is one that rejects the traditional Theravāda Buddhist understanding of *jhāna*. For these scholars *jhāna*, or at least the first *jhāna*, is a type of thinking that is characterized by the presence of joy and happiness and is free from the hindrances, but is not otherwise radically different from ordinary consciousness. For me, it is difficult to understand what this could be. Perhaps they conceive it as rather like the state of mind when a scholar intensively explores some abstract question. I find it difficult to reconcile this with some of the things the Buddha says in praise of *jhāna*.

Let us first of all note that such an understanding does not seem to have been current in India in the first millennium c.e. As far as I can see, Theravādins, Sarvāstivādins, Yogācārins, and probably Madhyamakas all share the same basic understanding of *jhāna*. The position that rejects the traditional Theravāda Buddhist understanding of *jhāna* claims that the whole later Indian Buddhist tradition has misunderstood the basics of its own meditation tradition. Personally, I have problems with this.

It seems useful to look a little at the arguments that have been presented to support that view. There seem to be five main points:

1. The list of *jhāna* factors
2. The description of unification of mind in the second *jhāna*
3. The manner of understanding *vitakka* and *vicāra*
4. The fact that the complete absence of the functioning of the senses is sometimes specified in the case of the formless attainments
5. A selective use of arguments based on dating *sutta*s

### ***The List of* Jhāna *Factors***

Already, in the *Mahāvedalla-sutta* (M 43; MN I 294), the first meditation is described as having abandoned five items and as endowed with five items. The five items that have been abandoned are the five hindrances. The five items or factors with which the first meditation is endowed are four given as above—thinking, exploring, joy, and happiness—with one addition. The addition is one-pointedness of mind (*cittekaggatā*), generally understood as a synonym for *samādhi*.

<!-- Page 48 -->

It has been suggested that this is a later addition because it is not found in the Sarvāstivādin version of this *sutta*. However, it is found in the Sarvāstivādin version of the closely associated *Cūḷavedalla-sutta* (M 44).[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-120 "note") And it seems to be taken for granted in all early *abhidharma* literature. In part, this must be because the word *jhāna* is taken as implying concentration (*samādhi*). So, for the Vaibhāṣika *abhidharma* tradition, *dhyāna* is defined as concentration.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-121 "note") For them, four factors accompany concentration in the first *dhyāna*; one is concentration itself. The Theriya *abhidhamma* tradition, however, defines the first *jhāna* as a collective label for the five *jhāna* factors. And each of the following *jhāna*s similarly labels the factors that arise in it.

In the *Dīgha-nikāya*, the addition prior to the first *jhāna* of the formula quoted above must also be intended to make clear that a process similar to approach concentration precedes the first *jhāna*. That does not seem to appear at this point in corresponding texts from other schools, but that may only be due to the incompleteness of our sources. As suggested previously, this kind of formula is inherently mobile.

In effect, the argument here depends entirely on whether *jhāna* was already being used as a technical term for a deep concentration by the Buddha or his predecessors. I think it probably was.

### ***Unification of Mind***

This is the formula for the second *jhāna*:

> And again a monk, as a result of the thorough stilling of *vitakka* and *vicāra*, enters upon and abides in the second *jhāna*, which has unification of mind that brings about complete clarity within, is without *vitakka* and without *vicāra*, and has joy and happiness born of concentration.

Or, a very slightly different Sanskrit version has this:

> As a result of the thorough stilling of *vitakka* and *vicāra*, as a result of complete personal stilling, and as a result of unification of mind, the monk enters upon and abides in the second *jhāna*, which is without *vitakka* and without *vicāra* and has joy and happiness born of concentration.

<!-- Page 49 -->

The replacement of adjectival compounds qualifying *jhāna* with expressions in the ablative case is likely to be a product of the process of Sanskritizing an earlier Middle Indian version.

Several features of this description have led some scholars to suggest that it is only with the second *jhāna* that we have an actual state of *samādhi* free from discursive thought, dependent on the concentration of the first *jhāna*:

1. The joy and happiness of the second *jhāna* is qualified as “born of concentration,” whereas that in the first *jhāna* is described as “born of seclusion.” This is taken to mean that concentration only arises in the second *jhāna*. That position is rather unconvincing, however—the epithet “born of seclusion (*viveka*)” for the joy and happiness in the first *jhāna* refers back to the process of seclusion (*vivicca*) from the five hindrances prior to that *jhāna*. One would expect “born of concentration” to refer to something prior to the second *jhāna*. That prior condition could well be the simple attainment of the first *jhāna*; some interpreters prefer to see it as referring to the process of attaining the second *jhāna*.
2. The second *jhāna* is described as “unity” or “unification” of mind (*cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ*). This is usually explained as equivalent to one-pointedness of mind, but it is probably derived from Sanskrit *ūti*, “a web,” and therefore means “part of a single web”—that is, the mind is harmoniously unified. That would seem natural for a state without the constant activity of *vitakka* and *vicāra*, but is taken by some as indicating that full concentration is not reached until the second *jhāna*.
3. The epithet in Pali of *ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ* has been variously interpreted. Literally, it seems to mean that the second *jhāna* or unity of mind “makes [the mind] completely clear within.” This can be understood as comparable to the settling of waves or mud in a pond to make it completely transparent. Such a result is often understood to be due to faith (*saddhā*), and both the Pali *abhidhamma* texts and the Vaibhāṣikas explained the expression used here as equivalent to faith.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-122 "note") The Vaibhāṣika explanation seems to be based upon an earlier Middle Indic phrasing. It seems slightly more natural here to interpret the Sanskrit as signifying complete personal stilling and those who rejected Vaibhāṣika authority seem to have explained it rather as “a calm flow of the (mental) continuity due to being without the disturbance of *vitarka* and *vicāra*.”[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-123 "note") Very likely, however, the original understanding is that it refers to unification of mind as bringing about an inner clarity that brings stillness of mind and is an expression of faith.

<!-- Page 50 -->

Some, however, see the reference here as specifically indicating the quieting of discursive thought. In that case, such thinking would not cease before the second *jhāna*.

### ***The Interpretation of* Vitakka *and* Vicāra**

If *vitakka* and *vicāra* are interpreted as meaning thinking and exploring in general terms, then their absence is indeed only signaled in the second *jhāna*; they are present in the first. However, this is not how they are understood in the later Pali texts. In an article published in 1992, I discussed the meaning in those works in detail.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-124 "note") I concluded that their explanation of the meaning is as follows:

For the canonical *abhidhamma*, *vitakka* at its weakest results in a tendency to speculate and fix upon ideas. More strongly developed, it is the ability to apply the mind to something and to fix it upon a (meditative) object. *Vicāra* at its weakest is simply the tendency of the mind to wander. More highly developed, it is the ability to explore and examine an object. In one way we can say that *vitakka* is “thinking of” something, whereas *vicāra* is “thinking about” that same thing, but the latter is probably intended to refer more to what we would now describe as the mind’s associative faculty.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-125 "note")

The question then is whether these two terms already have that technical sense in the earliest texts. My belief is that they do, and that this notion derives from a pre-Buddhist classification of concentration into three kinds: concentration with both *vitakka* and *vicāra*, concentration with only *vicāra*, and concentration with neither of these. It is this which the Buddha reformulated into the system of the four *jhāna*s. I believe that before the Buddha, various meditation states were not seen as part of a sequential path but rather as isolated practices without the context of a full, graduated system.

### ***Absence of the Functioning of the Senses***

The absence of sense functioning is specified for formless attainments, discussed in the next chapter, because they follow fourth *jhāna*, which is strongly connected with higher knowledge (*abhiññā*). Some see this as allowing that sense functioning of some type can exist at lower levels of *jhāna*.

<!-- Page 51 -->

### ***A Selective Use of Arguments Based on Dating* Sutta*s***

There are a number of passages in the *Sutta-piṭaka* that bear upon these points. They are often dismissed because either the whole discourse or a particular passage is not found in the Chinese translations. There are three main problems with this stance. Firstly, the Chinese texts are a selection of materials from more than one source. This means that a discourse can easily be missing or doubled up simply because different traditions placed it in a different collection. Secondly, we have no way of knowing how far the selection of texts in Chinese is representative. For all we know, the collection in the Pali Canon could be more typical of most Indian Buddhist schools and the material in Chinese mainly representative of Buddhist schools in the Northwest of the Indian subcontinent. Thirdly, if we assume that whatever is missing in Chinese is a later addition, we are simply reversing the view that holds the Pali texts to be authentic original Buddhism. Some of the features peculiar to the Pali texts may be authentic ideas of early Buddhism preserved only in this school.

In the first *sutta* of the *Moggallāna-saṃyutta*, the Buddha is portrayed as appearing in a vision to Moggallāna and telling him to “fix your mind in the first *jhāna*, unify your mind in the first *jhāna*, concentrate your mind in the first *jhāna*.”[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-126 "note") Similar instructions follow for subsequent *jhāna*s and attainments. There can be no doubt that the first *jhāna* is here understood as a state involving concentration. The absence of most of this *saṃyutta* in Chinese is most unlikely to be because it is later than other texts preserved in Chinese. We should look rather to the role played by Moggallāna in different schools. It may be later than some other *sutta*s, but it certainly is not a product of *abhidhamma* influence.

The mention of one-pointedness as a fifth factor of the first *jhāna* in the *Mahāvedalla-sutta* (M 43) has already been discussed. In the *Anupada-sutta* (M 111), we find a list of sixteen *dhamma*s that Sāriputta found present in the first *jhāna*. This includes one-pointedness. The *sutta* is celebrating the marvelous acuteness of Sāriputta’s insight in recollecting the different *dhamma*s present in this and other states. Again, the absence of any parallels to this discourse outside of the Theriya tradition does not establish lateness, given the limited nature of our evidence, and certainly not the kind of extreme lateness sometimes inferred. In particular, it does not show any very obvious relationship to the *abhidhamma* texts.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-127 "note")

<!-- Page 52 -->

Partly a relationship to *abhidhamma* is suggested because of a model of the historical development of Buddhism that associates *abhidhamma* literature with scholasticism. But if we are not going to refer to the early Mahāyāna *prajñāpāramitā* literature as scholasticism, it seems invidious to apply this epithet to the early *abhidhamma* literature.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-128 "note")

It is, however, true that for the early *abhidhamma* literature, one-pointedness is described as present in all states of consciousness but in a varying capacity.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-129 "note") So even for the weakest kinds of thought arising (*cittuppāda*), there must be some degree of one-pointedness present for it to be focused on a single object. In other words, it is one-pointedness (or concentration) that focuses consciousness in every moment. When it is very strong, it is able to keep consciousness focused on the same object for a series of successive moments. It follows from this understanding that one-pointedness must be present in the first *jhāna*. Although it is never stated, this seems to imply that one-pointedness in the first *jhāna* would be weaker than in the second *jhāna* onward, even if stronger than in ordinary skillful consciousness.

What is meant by concentration here and no doubt in earlier literature too is not any kind of forced attention, as we sometimes see it described in some of the insight meditation schools. It is a relaxed settling of the mind. Concentration, *samādhi*, is literally the “placing (*dhi< √dhā*) [of the mind] on (*ā*) [an object] in an integrated way (*sam*).” That is why it is a synonym of *samatha*, “peace.”

### ***Positive Arguments***

So far, I have rejected various arguments that have been put forward. Now I wish to present a positive reason for evaluating the first *jhāna* as already a special state. For this we need to refer to the word *kāma*.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-130 "note") In the Buddhist texts this usually has one of two meanings. It can simply mean desire. Or, in the plural, it can mean sense objects.[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-131 "note") Both of these meanings are frequent. For example, in the third precept we have the second meaning: wrong behavior concerning sense objects (*kāmesu-micchācāra*). In compounds we usually cannot tell if it is singular or plural; so either meaning may apply. Quite often, the first sense—*kāma* meaning “desire”—is adopted by translators, even when it is almost certain that the second is correct. We often read about the lower heavens of the Buddhist cosmos as the heavens of desire and their inhabitants as the gods of desire, when it is almost certain that we should think rather of heavens in which all five sense objects are experienced as opposed to those in which the senses of sight and hearing are mainly operative or those in which even visual forms and sounds cease to be experienced.

<!-- Page 53 -->

Two types of description within the *sutta*s can be considered here. The first begins by referring to the five kinds of attractive sense objects (*kāma*). These are the source of sensual pleasure (*kāmasukha*). That pleasure is contrasted with the pleasure that is experienced through the *jhāna*s.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-132 "note") It is inferior, not to be practiced; one should be fearful of it. The happiness of *jhāna*, on the other hand, should be developed; one should not be afraid of it.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-133 "note") We learn too that the joy (*pīti*) that is derived from attractive sense objects is inferior in quality. Joy that is not so derived is compared to a magical fire that burns without fuel and hence its flames are clearer and more radiant.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-134 "note")

The second type of description is one that refers to the five hindrances and a state of mind that abandons those hindrances. Sometimes similes are given to illustrate the pleasurable sense of freedom or safety that arises when the mind is freed from those hindrances. Although usually incorporated into the larger accounts of the path, this usage does also occur separately. We learn that there is *jhāna* that is not praised by the Buddha and *jhāna* that is praised by him. *Jhāna* that is not praised by the Buddha is that which is based upon the five hindrances.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-135 "note")

In the light of such passages as these, it seems clear that *vivicc’ eva kāmehi* in the first *jhāna* formula means “quite secluded from sense objects.”[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-136 "note") Note that in the Pali version, this separation from the senses is stressed by the emphatic particle *eva*. This is also the case in the Mahāsa*ṃ*ghika Sanskrit version recorded in *The Great Story* (*Mahāvastu*), but not in other extant (mostly Sarvāstivādin) Sanskrit sources. We can then be confident that the Theriya and Sarvāstivādin commentaries that present *jhāna* as a state in which sensory functioning is suspended for a shorter or longer period are preserving a much older understanding.

That is confirmed by the statement that gives sound as the “thorn” of the first *jhāna* and the factors that are successively dropped in the following *jhāna*s as the “thorns” of those *jhāna*s. In other words, sound (one of the five kinds of *kāma*) is present in a stage prior to first *jhāna*, just as thinking and exploring are present prior to second *jhāna* and so on.[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-137 "note") It follows that sound is not present in the first *jhāna*. Probably related to this is the statement that speech has ceased in the first *jhāna*.[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-138 "note")

<!-- Page 54 -->

### ***The Positive Evaluation of* Jhāna *in the* Sutta*s***

The discourses emphasize that even a brief moment (a finger snap) of *jhāna* is valuable. The monk who experiences it is one who “dwells not devoid of *jhāna*. He is one who follows the Teacher’s instruction and fulfills his advice. Not in vain does he enjoy the alms food of the land.”[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-139 "note")

It is also emphasized that *jhāna* is something rather special and profound: “The range of *jhāna*, monks, cannot be conceived of; it should not be explored in thought. Anyone exploring it would partake of madness and distress.”[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-140 "note")

In the *Pāsādika-sutta* (D 29; DN III 132) it is emphasized that the four *jhāna*s are practices involving happiness that lead to full disenchantment, dispassion, cessation, peace, higher knowledge, full awakening, and *nibbāna*.[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-141 "note")

That *jhāna* always involves the presence of the five faculties and the other *dhamma*s that contribute to awakening is explicit in the canonical *abhidhamma* texts, but it is also nicely expressed in a *Dhammapada* verse:

There is no *jhāna* for one without wisdom. Nor wisdom for one not practicing *jhāna*.

He who has both *jhāna* and wisdom is truly in the presence of *nibbāna.* (372)[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-142 "note")

Similarly, in the *Saṃyutta-nikāya*, the simile of the Ganges River is employed to emphasize that anyone who practices and makes much of the four *jhāna*s is on the way to *nibbāna*:

> Just as, monks, the river Ganges tends toward the east, goes down toward the east, inclines toward the east, in the same way, monks, a monk who brings into being and makes much of the four *jhāna*s, tends toward *nibbāna*, goes down toward *nibbāna*, inclines toward *nibbāna*.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-143 "note")

This is part of a general treatment of the seven sets that later tradition refers to as the *dhamma*s that contribute to awakening.[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-144 "note")

In the Buddha’s life story, *jhāna* is given a very special role. Accounts of his entrance into *nibbāna* (*parinibbāna*) present him as ascending through the *jhāna*s to the formless attainments and the cessation of labeling and feeling, but then returning and finally ascending again to the fourth *jhāna* and entering *nibbāna* from there.[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-145 "note") Such an account can only be intended to stress the importance of the fourth *jhāna*. Similarly, the standard account that is incorporated into three discourses of the *Majjhima-nikāya* presents his enlightenment as following on from development of the four *jhāna*s (M 19; M 26; M 36). Versions preserved in Sanskrit and parallels of various kinds in Chinese and Tibetan are similar in this respect.[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-146 "note") Again, this can only be intended to stress the importance of the four *jhāna*s.

<!-- Page 55 -->

These sources also relate his earlier experience of *jhāna* as a child or young man.[[36]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-147 "note") Remembering that experience and realizing that “only this is the way to awakening,” he asked why he should be afraid of that happiness “quite secluded from sense objects, secluded from unskillful *dhamma*s.” Realizing that he was not afraid of that, he turned away from ascetic practice and ended his extreme fasting. This in effect depicts the Buddha-to-be’s discovery of the Middle Way as directly related to the experience of *jhāna*. Taken together, it is clear that the texts we have intend to portray *jhāna* as fundamental to the experience of the path.

That said, the *jhāna*s are very commonly referred to as “happy abiding in the visible reality” (*diṭṭhadhammasukhavihāra*), and it is emphasized that even those who are enlightened may practice them for this purpose. The intended implication must be that they are simply the basis, although there is no suggestion that they are merely an optional basis. The difference from other practices is set out very clearly in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*, which refers to four kinds of bringing into being of concentration:

1. The four *jhāna*s—conducing to “happy abiding in the visible reality”
2. Developing a mind of radiance—conducing to knowledge and vision
3. Knowing feelings, labeling, and thinking as they arise, remain, and cease—conducing to mindfulness and clear comprehension
4. Contemplating the rise and fall of the five aggregates—conducing to the destruction of the corruptions (*āsava*s) (that is, awakening) (DN III 222; AN II 45)

Here the four *jhāna*s are given first and are understood as providing a basis of peaceful and comfortable living. Then comes the development of internal light that is the basis for the various ordinary higher knowledges. Only after that do we find practices that can loosely be labeled as insight—that is, the knowledge of the aggregates that leads to the awareness of rise and fall in insight practice proper. This will be discussed in chapter 4.

This kind of succession is clear from the many sequences that begin with the *jhāna*s and then lead on to the formless attainments (to be addressed in the next chapter) or to the various kinds of higher knowledge or both of these, before coming to the final stage.

<!-- Page 56 -->

### ***The Remaining* Jhāna*s***

We have already discussed the main features of the second *jhāna*, so I give now just the formula:

> And furthermore, O king, as a result of the quieting of thinking and exploring, a monk enters upon the second *jhāna* and remains there. The second *jhāna* has mental unification that brings inner clarity and possesses joy and happiness that arises from concentration. He drenches, floods, fills, and suffuses this very body with the joy and happiness that arises from concentration. Every part of this whole body is suffused with the joy and happiness that arises from concentration.

After this follows the third *jhāna*:

> And furthermore, O king, as a result of being dispassionate about joy, a monk enters upon the third *jhāna* and remains there. He remains equanimous, mindful, and clearly comprehending. He experiences with his body the happiness concerning which the noble ones declare, “he is equanimous, mindful, and abiding in happiness.” He drenches, floods, fills, and suffuses this very body with happiness undisturbed by joy. Every part of this whole body is suffused with happiness undisturbed by joy.

The Pali and Sanskrit versions are closely parallel here. We can note that there are essentially two main features:

1. The loss of joy leaves a quieter kind of more peaceful happiness. Exegetical traditions have differed on the exact meaning of happiness here, partly because of the specific reference to the body. For the Pali tradition, what is meant is that the practitioner experiences a type of pleasant mental feeling in the third *jhāna* that is followed by the experience of bodily pleasant feeling on emerging.
2. Being equanimous, mindful, and aware are specifically mentioned. This can only mean that the state is understood as more alert than the previous two *jhāna*s.

Finally, we have the fourth *jhāna*:

> And furthermore, O king, as a result of the abandoning of pleasant and unpleasant bodily feeling and the prior disappearance of pleasant and unpleasant mental feeling, a monk enters upon the fourth *jhāna* and remains there. The fourth *jhāna* has neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling and has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity. He is seated, suffusing this very body with a mind that is pure and clear. Every part of this whole body is suffused with a mind that is pure and clear.

<!-- Page 57 -->

The Pali and Sanskrit versions are again closely parallel and there are again two main features:

1. The feeling now becomes “neither pleasant nor unpleasant,” by which is meant a very balanced kind of feeling. The process by which pleasant and unpleasant mental feeling previously disappeared and the corresponding kinds of bodily feeling were abandoned is less clear. In the Pali tradition it is usually interpreted on the basis of a *sutta* in the *Indriya-saṃyutta* (SN V 213–217). There it is understood that painful bodily feeling is abandoned in the first *jhāna* because awareness of sense objects, and hence painful bodily feeling, ceases; in the second *jhāna*, painful mental thinking is abandoned, because thinking and exploring that tend toward aversion have ceased. Pleasant physical feeling disappears in the third *jhāna* because the presence of *pīti* no longer affects the body, while pleasant mental feeling disappears in the fourth *jhāna*.
2. There is again reference to equanimity and mindfulness, but this time it is in a compound that can be variously translated as “the fourth *jhāna* has purity due to equanimity and mindfulness,” or “the fourth *jhāna* has equanimity and mindfulness and purity,” or “the fourth *jhāna* has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity.” The last of these is usual in the Theriya tradition, following the *Abhidhamma-piṭaka* (Vibh 261). The Vaibhāṣika interpretation seems to have been similar.[[37]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-148 "note")

The intention clearly is to portray a state of mental clarity in which the mind is not easily disturbed by bias or inclinations of any kind.

### ***The Similes for the Remaining* Jhāna*s***

The joy and happiness of the second *jhāna* is compared to upwelling streams of cool water in a pool whose only source is a spring that suffuses the whole pool. The happiness undisturbed by joy of the third *jhāna* is compared to cool water that suffuses from top to bottom lotuses of various kinds that are completely submerged in the water of a lotus pond. The suffusing of the body by a purified mind in the fourth *jhāna* is compared to a seated man whose whole body including his head is covered by a white garment.[[38]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-149 "note")

<!-- Page 58 -->

All of this makes quite clear that with the *jhāna*s we are dealing with a special state that the *abhidhamma* quite reasonably classifies as of a different level from ordinary consciousness.

## ***Abhidhamma* Issues**

### **Jhāna *in the Commentaries***

For the commentaries of the fourth or fifth century c.e., *jhāna* is defined as full absorption with a purely conceptual image or *nimitta*, while in a given *jhāna* there can be no change of mental object nor any kind of thinking or sense perception, including awareness of the body. Such an understanding is based upon *abhidhamma* concepts and is found only in the very latest parts of the canon.

### ***The Process***

It is important not to confuse this *abhidhamma*-based presentation with the *suttanta*-based presentation in the oldest texts. In effect, the early discourses do not distinguish between approach and absorption in *jhāna* and hence do not spell things out in the way that the *abhidhamma* literature indicates. But we should not misunderstand what the commentaries are saying. From the strictest point of view, there is no awareness of body or other sense objects and no mental object other than a *nimitta* in the first three *jhāna*s—that is, in absorption. However, this does not mean that this is always the case as we ordinarily experience the *jhāna*s in meditation. The mind can flicker between absorption and other types of consciousness in just the same way that it flickers between seeing and hearing. According to *abhidhamma*, seeing and hearing do not occur at the same moment, but one may be seeing these words and hearing the television at the same moment; so this is not how it is in ordinary life.

For the forest specialist, *jhāna* may be developed with intensive practice and sitting for many hours. This obviously gives more control. For the experienced monk who lives in a more typical monastery and engages in many other appropriate activities but still maintains regular meditation practice, it may operate differently. When he sits, he may pass through a number of *jhāna*s very quickly in a single sitting without necessarily being able to say exactly what is occurring every time. What he experiences is more like the account in the *sutta*s but can be analyzed in terms of the commentarial accounts.

<!-- Page 59 -->

In the end there seems no good reason for seeing the description of *jhāna* in the earliest texts as significantly different from that given in the later texts. Certainly, developments have occurred. But they do not seem to indicate a fundamentally altered experience.

# 3. Formless Attainments in the Pali Canon

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

We will now look in more detail at the formless *jhāna*s, the meditation states that “surmount” even the subtle objects of the form meditations. The main concern of this chapter will be to present a coherent account of the origins of the meditative system taught in early Buddhism. Some scholars have argued that the formless attainments are introduced to Buddhism at a relatively late period, considerably after the time of the Founder. I disagree—and we will explore some of these arguments later in this chapter. I propose a more positive account. I am going to argue that although the system of the formless attainments was most probably first taught by the Buddha himself, some aspects of it derive from his predecessors and early teachers. This relates the methods of meditation, as given in the texts known earlier as *nikāya*s and later as *āgama*s, to the system of the five aggregates (*khandha*s).

I propose that the four *jhāna*s and the four formless attainments form the interrelated core of a single systematic description of the stages of meditation. This has many implications for our understanding of the earliest Buddhism. The main reason why it has not been understood in this way in the past is the distinctive terminology used to describe some of the formless attainments. I will address this issue and attempt to show that this terminology is in part an inheritance from the Buddha’s own early teachers.

## **Grasping the Formless**

The overall picture can be presented diagrammatically, as in Figure 1. In form meditation, the mind apprehends a specific shape or object, gradually simplifying subjective activity. This reaches its climax with the fourth *jhāna*, represented here at the apex of the lower triangle. From here on, the meditator proceeds by simplifying the mind’s object; so the upper triangle of the four formless attainments can be seen as modifications of the fourth *jhāna*.

<!-- Page 60 -->

### **The Stages of Jhāna**

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_052_Image_0001.jpg)

\*The numbers denote the *jhāna* factors: (1) *vitakka*, (2) *vicāra*, (3) *pīti*, (4) *sukha*, and (5) *ekaggatā*

## **The Early Life of the Buddha**

Let’s begin briefly with the two teachers of the *bodhisatta*, “the one bent on awakening.” This takes us into the whole question of the biography of the Buddha-to-be, or at least the depiction of his early years in that nascent biography. André Bareau has presented much of the available material from many sources in a series of works in French.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-150 "note") Interesting as this is, I very much disagree with a great deal of it, but let me begin with something that I do agree with.

He points out that there appear to be in the oldest sources two alternative accounts of the events or motivation that led to Prince Siddhattha’s departure from the confinement of household life. Simplifying slightly, the more mythical *sutta*s describing the corresponding events in the lives of past Buddhas seem mostly to provide an account in terms of the famous four sights. By contrast, the more historical discourses seem to relate the departure to an experience of the first *jhāna*.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-151 "note")

We may then suppose that at an earlier stage it was some kind of meditational experience that led to the decision to take up his spiritual quest. Only later has that event been moved to a very young age, as low as one month old in some sources.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-152 "note") If that is the case, we might imagine that even before the renunciation the young Buddha-to-be was a disciple of Āḷāra the Kālāma. It has also often been suggested on the basis of some of the *sutta* accounts that he might have started his quest at a rather younger age than twenty-nine. If so, we might plausibly think in terms of a rather longer period of training under each of his teachers.

## **Āḷāra the Kālāma and Uddaka the Son of Rāma**

Given that one expects antecedents to be minimized by a new and successful movement, we should not be at all surprised that little is said about the Buddha’s teachers. The fact that they are mentioned at all suggests rather that the tradition of their existence was simply too strong to be entirely neglected. André Bareau is quite skeptical as to whether the two teachers even existed. I will not enter into that here, since Ghiorgo Zafiropulo has very effectively answered most of his objections.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-153 "note") Alex Wynne in his recent book has also advanced further evidence in support of the historical existence of these figures.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-154 "note") I will at this point, however, register disagreement with one part of Bareau’s methodology.

<!-- Page 61 -->

On numerous occasions Bareau suggests that *sutta*s found in Pali but not in Chinese translations or elsewhere are probably late. One example of this in the present context is the *Mahāsaccaka*-*sutta* (M 36).[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-155 "note") Unfortunately for Bareau’s argument, a Sanskrit manuscript containing substantial portions of a (*Mūla*-) *Sarvāstivādin* version of the *Dīrghâgama* has subsequently come to light. One of the discourses found in it corresponds to the Pali *Mahāsaccaka*-*sutta*. Indeed, around ten other discourses corresponding to *sutta*s in the Pali *Majjhima*-*nikāya* have so far been identified by various scholars.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-156 "note") Evidently, the only reason they are not found in Chinese translation is that the Chinese *Madhyamâgama* is Sarvāstivādin, whereas the Chinese *Dīrghâgama* is not. These particular discourses are quite long and are included in the *Dīrgha* by the Sarvāstivādins, but not by the school the Chinese *Dīrgha* comes from. They have fallen through the gap, as it were. Quite evidently nothing can be concluded about their age from the fact of their absence in Chinese. All of Bareau’s arguments based upon this are simply false.

Returning to the life story of the *bodhisatta*, we come to the account of the practice of austerities. Bareau comments, “Let us besides note that of the four Pali Suttas which inform us about the search for Awakening, only one, the *Mahāsaccaka* … speaks of these austerities.” That is somewhat doubtful, but it is certainly the case that the *Ariyapariyesana*/*Pāsarāsi-sutta* (M 26) does not include any account of the austerities.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-157 "note")

Bareau points out that the other extant early sources (with the solitary exception of a very brief mention in the *Dharmaguptaka Vinaya*) also have no account of this. Although Zafiropulo sees no reason to doubt the antiquity of this episode, I suppose that it was originally found only in the *Mahāsaccaka* in the context of a discussion with a Jain, exemplifying the Buddha’s skill in means.
