We may suspect that the episode has been subsequently inflated by hagiographers, drawing on material from Jain and Ājīvaka sources. Other material of a biographical kind has surely been imported from other *sutta*s. Moreover, we may doubt that anything like the six years given first by Aśvaghoṣa around the second century c.e. was taken up by the practice of austerities.

<!-- Page 62 -->

The most likely reconstruction of this part of the biography of the Buddha seems to me to be the following: The youth who is to become the Buddha begins the practice of the *jhāna*s at home at a relatively young age, perhaps in his early twenties or earlier. He proves to have a natural talent for it and is moved to adopt the mendicant life, joining his teacher for further studies. At some point he moves on to another teacher, probably from the same general tradition. Subsequently, he strikes out on his own. This may have involved an episode of more ascetic practice at some point. Evidently, he later believed he had in important ways gone beyond the teachings he had previously studied. No doubt, he added important new elements to them. Nevertheless, much of what he taught must have been derived from the tradition in which he first studied and practiced.

For me, then, much of the meditational technique and cosmology he accepts is derived from his predecessors. In particular, I see no reason to doubt that he *in some way* inherited the methods of the *jhāna*s and formless attainments. Others take a different view; let me turn now to the formless attainments.

Some scholars believe that the four formless spheres—the sphere of boundless space; the sphere of boundless discrimination or consciousness; the sphere where nothing is owned, often translated as “nothingness”; and the sphere neither with nor without labeling (*saññā*)—are a later introduction to Buddhism. The fullest recent presentation of this position is probably that by Zafiropulo. I will not address these arguments in detail. Suffice it to say that I do not find his evidence persuasive. Part of what he has to say concerns the general credibility and coherence of the sequence of the four formless attainments. I will try to address that by giving an account of how I understand the role and logic of these attainments within the context of the meditative system outlined in the Pali texts.

Other scholars who claim that the formless attainments are a later development refer to the ideas of Friedrich Heiler, first put forward in 1919.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-158 "note") They have been rejected by many, perhaps most, scholars, but this viewpoint has been more recently revived by Lambert Schmithausen.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-159 "note") Heiler himself simply gives an account of the four formless stages based upon an interpretation of the *Visuddhimagga* and follows that up with a strongly theological evaluation. That, I think, has much to do with a long history of Protestant polemic against various forms of Catholic mysticism, Counter-Reformation Catholic polemic against particular types of false mysticism, and, even earlier, various kinds of Christian critique of Pagan mysticism. I will simply say here that Heiler sharply contrasts the four *jhāna*s as wholly different to the four formless attainments.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-160 "note")

<!-- Page 63 -->

Heiler too is better addressed by presenting more positively my own understanding of the place of the formless attainments in the meditative system as a whole.

## **The Meditative System of the Pali Texts**

In my view, the Buddhist meditative system is based on the five aggregates: form or matter (*rūpa*), feeling (*vedanā*), labeling or perception (*saññā*), activities or constructions (*saṅkhāra*s), and consciousness (*viññāṇa*). As we will see, the system of the four *jhāna*s is directly related to the second aggregate of feeling (*vedanā*). We can then go on to demonstrate that the four formless attainments are related to the third aggregate of recognition or labeling (*saññā*), in a closely parallel manner.

### ***The First Aggregate***

The first of the five aggregates is *rūpa* in its sense of physicality or matter, primarily in one’s own body. The name is perhaps generalized from the sense of form as the object of the sense of sight. Or we may see it as related to earlier notions of *nāma*-*rūpa*. However we take it, there can hardly be any doubt that much Buddhist meditation is for the specific purpose of detaching from the body as something that is clung to or appropriated.

This is quite obvious in the case of different meditative techniques. Some visualize the parts of the body, some analyze it into four elements—or six spheres, with the five senses and the sixth “sense,” the mind—and some explore the differing degrees of ugliness of decomposing corpses. But these are special cases intended for specific psychological types. The primary focus is on overcoming attraction to and rejection of what we would now call the physical world. As we saw in the last chapter, the end of that process is the stilling of the five hindrances and the entering of a meditative state that is *vivicc’eva kāmehi, vivicca akusalehi dhammehi—*secluded from desirable objects of sense, secluded from unskillful states. The result is, among other things, a joy and happiness that is *vivekaja*—arisen from that seclusion.

### ***The Second Aggregate***

The second aggregate is *vedanā*, literally “what enables one to know,” but in practice what is meant here is the general state of feeling. Most commonly, this is divided into three: *sukha*, *dukkha*, and *adukkha*-*m*-*asukha*: pleasant or happy feeling, painful or unpleasant feeling, and a third kind referred to as neither the one nor the other. It does not refer to what in English we would call “emotion;” that is a rather different matter. Feeling can be classified in many different ways from different standpoints—some texts enumerate as many as a hundred and eight types of feeling. In the meditative context, however, a list of five types of feeling is particularly important. Here pleasant feeling is differentiated into *sukha* in the sense of pleasant physical feeling and *somanassa* in the sense of pleasant mental feeling, while unpleasant feeling is differentiated into *dukkha*, here meaning painful physical feeling, and *domanassa*, unpleasant mental feeling.

<!-- Page 64 -->

As we have seen, the point of this kind of classification is to portray the progression through the *jhāna*s as a movement from the grossest forms of feeling to the most subtle kind. The first *jhāna* in some way abandons painful physical feeling; this is also expressed in another way by saying that sounds are the “thorn” of the first *jhāna*.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-161 "note") Subsequently, in the Pali tradition at least, painful mental feeling is abandoned by the second *jhāna*, quite appropriately given that this is the *jhāna* in which joy (*pīti*) is at its highest (SN V 213–17). It is perhaps less obvious why pleasant physical feeling is abandoned with the third, but the intention must be to understand *pīti* as giving physical effects. At all events, it is clear enough that the fourth *jhāna* entails the abandoning of even pleasant mental feeling. From now on, all the higher meditative states have only the kind of feeling that is neither painful nor pleasant. They cannot be referred to as having pleasant feeling of any kind, although there are other senses in which they can be spoken of as happiness (*sukha*).

Such a detailed analysis is only found in a few discourses, but it is clear enough from the basic descriptions of the four *jhāna*s that they involve the experience of a very happy state and the subsequent experience of a more subtle kind of mental clarity and flexibility, accompanied by a more neutral feeling. It follows that the four *jhāna*s are in essence the experience of more and more refined levels of feeling; they also entail detaching from at least the grosser kinds of feeling. So the *jhāna*s do indeed represent, from one point of view, a method for at least partial distancing from the second aggregate.

It is also important to note that they constitute a group of three *jhāna*s with pleasant feeling, followed by a final culminating *jhāna* of a different kind. The feeling is now classifiable as not painful and not pleasant—that is, it is not the pleasantness that the meditator has been trying to develop hitherto and not the aversion-related unpleasantness that they have been trying to avoid. This is the identical pattern that we shall meet with the formless attainments. It is also incidentally in part the logic of the sequence of the four *brahmavihāra*s.

<!-- Page 65 -->

At this point I would like to refer to a passage from Bart Dessein’s English translation of the *Saṃyuktâbhidharmahṛdaya* of Dharmatrāta.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-162 "note") This is a Sarvāstivādin *abhidharma śāstra* preserved only in Chinese translation and almost certainly dating from the early fourth century c.e. Dharmatrāta addresses the question as to why feeling (*vedanā*) and labeling (*saññā*) are treated as separate aggregates, when so many other mental phenomena are grouped together in the fourth aggregate. The explanation given is that they are separated because the two main causes of dispute are craving and views, which arise from the second and third aggregates respectively. But, most interestingly, Dharmatrāta then adds that it is through feeling that the *jhāna*s are developed and through labeling (*saññā*)—the third aggregate—that the immaterial states are developed. A similar point is made in Vasubandhu’s *Abhidharmakośa*.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-163 "note")

### ***The Third Aggregate***

First of all, let us note that if Dharmatrāta and Vasubandhu are correct in seeing a link between the formless attainments and the third aggregate, then one might also expect a link in early texts between the treatment of views and the treatment of the formless attainments. This is because the labeling and classifying activity of the third aggregate is usually considered to underlie views. I think we do find this—it is noticeable in particular that such discourses as the *Brahmajāla-sutta* (D 1) or the *Pañcattaya-sutta* (M 102) contain a considerable number of related references. But the matter requires more research.

The immediately crucial issue is whether there is a link between the formless attainments and *saññā*. There, I think, we can unequivocally say that the answer is yes. To start with, the formula for entry to the first formless attainment is quite explicit about what is involved. It is the result of (1) overcoming (*samatikkama*) *saññā*s connected with form (*rūpa*), (2) the ending of *saññā*s connected with sensory impact (*paṭigha*), and (3) ceasing to bring to mind (*amanasikāra*) diverse *saññā*s. There are problems connected with at least two of these, but the one thing that is quite clear is that the process is concerned with *saññā*s and with almost nothing else!

<!-- Page 66 -->

The precise nature of the *saññā*s that are being turned away from is open to some dispute. The first kind can be taken as referring to (a) the physical body or first aggregate; or (b) the objects of the sense of sight; or (c) mental objects in meditation; or (d) the *jhāna*s of the form element (*rūpadhātu*), of the form existence (*rūpabhava*), or of the form level (*rūpāvacara*). The second kind must refer to the quality of resistance that is attributed to the objects of the five senses. The third is also problematic, as *nānatta* can be understood in two ways, either taking it as equivalent to a noun derived from the Sanskrit prefix denoting “different selves,” *nānātma*-, or taking it as equivalent to Sanskrit “the state of plurality,” *nānātva*.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-164 "note") In the first case (perhaps preferable), we should understand the meaning as diverse *saññā*s. In the second, we have to understand something like *saññā*s of or connected with multiplicity. The implication must be that henceforward only *saññā*s that are uniform or connected with unity will be sought.

However these problems are resolved, the move here is away from multiple objects of the mind and senses toward an exclusive use of mental apprehension and from the more complex to the more simple. This is also made clear by the emphasis on boundlessness in the names and practice of the first two formless attainments. But it remains the case that all of this is intimately bound up with *saññā* and therefore with a potential for wrong view.

The clearest place to start for an account of the practice of the formless attainments in the *sutta*s is probably the *Pañcattaya*-*sutta*.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-165 "note") This, incidentally, is another of the *Majjhima* discourses not found in any Chinese translation, which have turned out to be part of the (*Mūla*-)*Sarvāstivādin Dīrghāgama*.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-166 "note") In its Pali version, the *Pañcattaya-sutta*—the discourse on “The Five That Become Three”—commences with a discussion of mendicants and brahmins who conceptualize and form views about the future, professing numerous assertions regarding the future; as the *sutta* unfolds, it becomes clear that some of the meditative attainments wrongly grasped as a basis for views are form and formless *jhāna*s. Attachment and then views have arisen *after* the experience of these states. The first three are (a) a soul (*atta*) that has *saññā* and is unimpaired (*aroga*) after death, (b) one that is without *saññā* and unimpaired, and (c) one that is neither with nor without *saññā*.

<!-- Page 67 -->

Straightaway, we can recognize the fundamental structure of the formless attainments in this. We can also notice the very close parallel to the structure of the *jhāna*s in terms of the three kinds of feeling, which has already been mentioned in the discussion of the second aggregate (*vedanā*). In that case we had the second kind of feeling, unpleasant feeling (*dukkha*), which is outside the *jhāna*s;[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-167 "note") the first kind of feeling, pleasant or happy feeling (*sukha*), which is the feeling of the first three *jhāna*s; and a third kind of feeling, neither unpleasant nor pleasant (*adukkha*-*m*-*asukha*), found only in the fourth *jhāna*. The latter two feelings exist in inferior forms outside of *jhāna* too. Now we have three ways of possessing *saññā*. The second way is to be without *saññā* (*asaññin*); this is outside of the four formless attainments and is subsequently criticized as delusion (*sammoha*).[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-168 "note") The first way of possessing *saññā* is found in the first three formless attainments, as we shall see. The third way of possessing *saññā* is that of those who profess a soul that is neither with *saññā* nor without *saññā*. The intended meaning here must originally have been to refer to a state in which *saññā* is extremely subtle. This would be parallel to feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

Although the first kind of feeling (*sukha*) occurs in the first three *jhāna*s, it is equally possible to have gross (*sāmisa*) kinds of pleasant feeling outside of the *jhāna*s. Similarly, if views based on *saññā* are formed, they may or may not be based on the first three attainments. Correspondingly, the soul with *saññā* that one might envisage after death can be seen as having *rūpa* or not having *rūpa*, or both, or neither; it can be seen as having *saññā* of unity or diversity (*ekattasaññin*, *nānattasaññin*), as having *saññā* that is limited or measureless (*parittasaññin*, *appamāṇasaññin*). These are all terms related to meditational practice. The point in each case is that the meditational experience one has had of whatever level occurs with such power and authority that it commands assent as an experience of one’s real nature.

<!-- Page 68 -->

That authority is the basis for the (false) belief that this experience truly describes how one will be after death.

The *Pañcattaya-sutta* then points out that some go beyond this first stance to profess an immeasurable and unmoving universalized consciousness (*viññāṇakasiṇa*).[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-169 "note") Subsequently, it refers to those who profess an immeasurable and unmoving base of *ākiñcañña* as the purified, supreme, topmost, incomparable *saññā* out of all *saññā*s be they form or formless (*rūpa*, *arūpa*), be they of unity or diversity (*ekatta*, *nānatta*). This kind of understanding of the third formless level is found elsewhere also. The *Poṭṭhapāda-sutta* (D 9) describes this base as the *saññagga*, “the peak of *saññā*,” as possible after this state.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-170 "note") In the *Pārāyaṇa-sutta* of the *Sutta-nipāta* (Sn 1071), the same *ākiñcañña* base is described as “in the highest liberation with *saññā*” (*saññāvimokkhe parame*).[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-171 "note") The *Bhaddaji-sutta* refers to it as the “highest of *saññā*s” (*saññānaṃ aggo*).[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-172 "note") Several other passages make it clear that this is the culmination of *saññā*.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-173 "note") We should also note the occasional use of the term *saññāmaya* to describe a postulated formless soul or corresponding *deva*s.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-174 "note") The underlying understanding expressed in this section of the *sutta* is that views can be formed if there is any vestigial attachment to the feeling of a meditation state after it has passed, even if it has been accompanied by joy, happiness, or equanimity and is well advanced on the path. “Just as sunlight suffuses the area where a shadow leaves and shadow suffuses the area that sunlight leaves,” so, the Buddha explains, attachment giving rise to views and ego can arise when such a state has passed away. It is possible to form a wrong view based even on profound meditative experience. The Tathāgata and, by implication, those who are awakened, have gone beyond that; the states themselves, however, represent some of the highest meditative attainments.

<!-- Page 69 -->

### ***How the Formless Attainments Acquired Their Names***

Thus far the formless attainments appear quite tightly and exactly structured in a way that relates to the entry formula for the first formless attainment and to the name of the fourth. So why is it that a number of scholars have thought that this sequence does not follow logically after the four *jhāna*s? A primary reason is the fact that the names are quite different and give the appearance of having a different origin.

The simplest explanation of this seems to me to suppose that the names of these three are taken from the set of the seven *viññāṇaṭṭhiti*, “abidings of consciousness (*viññāṇa*).”[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-175 "note") Very possibly this list is a survival of the teachings of Āḷāra the Kālāma. It has a markedly more cosmological orientation than most of the material that seems likely to come from the Buddha himself. If we suppose that this list was already well known, then it seems quite natural that the names would be retained by the Buddha. We should also note briefly in this context the list of the eight “spheres of mastery” (*abhibhāyatana*s). The first two of these have *saññā* of form (*rūpasaññin*), while the remaining six have *saññā* of the formless (*arūpasaññin*); so the context is without doubt *saññā* again.

These lists are closely related to the list of the ten “spheres of the device” (*kasiṇāyatana*s); so it is easy to see that they form part of a nexus of practices concerned with visualization and conceptualization. The final two *kasiṇāyatana*s are space and *viññāṇa*; we have already met the last of these positioned immediately before the third formless base or sphere. I take the list of the eight freedoms (*vimokkha*s) as a later development, constructed on the basis of these earlier lists.[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-176 "note")

Erich Frauwallner wrote in this particular context, “The old canon has a number of forms of meditation which the Buddha himself had probably recommended for practice to his disciples.”[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-177 "note") I agree. Indeed, I would see no reason to doubt that some of them are much older than the Buddha and likely to have been inherited by him from his teachers. Others he will have developed in the course of a lifetime of teaching. Still others will have been developed by his disciples and their successors.

<!-- Page 70 -->

If the first two formless attainments and spheres derive their name from the last two *kasiṇāyatana*s, what then of the third? Up to this point I have not given close examination as to how *ākiñcaññāyatana* should properly be translated. I am, however, uneasy about the rendering of this as the base or sphere of “nothingness.” *Ākiñcañña* is an abstract formation from *akiñcana*. While the addition of the privative prefix *a*-to *kiṃ* + *cana could* possibly give the meaning “not anything,” it can *equally* give the meaning “without anything.” And the latter is its normal meaning.[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-178 "note")

The adjective means “without anything,” “poor”; as one would expect, that is a bad thing for the average person, but a positive aspiration in a spiritual context. Hence the noun *akiñcanatā* can mean “renunciation of everything, voluntary poverty.” All citations (in dictionaries and other reference works) that give the meaning of “nothingness” are from Buddhist sources and derive from its use in the present context. I suggest it would be better rendered as the base “where one has nothing” or the base “where one is without possessions” or, perhaps more practically for a translation, the base “where nothing is owned.”

Where then does the explanation “base of nothingness” come from? In 1824 Benjamin Clough quite appropriately gave the meaning of *ākiñcañña* as “the state of having nothing.”[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-179 "note") Also in the early nineteenth century, however, the influential Eugène Burnouf declared that “the third sphere is certainly that in which one believes that absolutely nothing exists.”[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-180 "note") He is followed closely by Childers, who gives “the Realm of Nothingness,” and says, “it is so-called because it is peopled by beings who believe that nothing exists (*n’atthi kiñci*).” These are rather off the mark, since there is no suggestion of any belief that nothing exists. Subsequent history shows how once a mistaken notion has taken hold, it can be very tenacious. Probably the currency of this rendering had initially as much to do with theological beliefs about the nature of pagan religion as anything in the term itself.

<!-- Page 71 -->

Burnouf was led to his rendering by the phrasing of the texts. The method for reaching the base where one has nothing is given as to have the idea “nothing exists” (*natthi kiñci*), which he understands as a nihilistic belief. I take it as a slightly more impersonal way of saying, “I have nothing” (*natthi me kiñci*). In any case, there is no justification for rendering *ākiñcaññāyatana* as the sphere or base of nothingness. At most, one could say that the attainment of the base of having nothing is a state in which one contemplates nothingness. But that is simply a technique and no more implies a nihilistic sense than do Madhyamaka meditations on emptiness (*śūnyatā*).

This is spelled out in the *Anupada-sutta* (M 111).[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-181 "note") That *sutta* states that such qualities as mindfulness and vigor are present in the sphere of having nothing. Let us just note further that for the *ābhidhammika*s at a slightly later date this is a state containing wisdom, mindfulness, faith, and many other skillful qualities. The Pali commentaries merely say that it is the state of not having anything and compare the sense of *āyatana* here to the standard example of a heavenly sphere, *devāyatana*.[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-182 "note")

The method of the formless attainments is straightforward. The first formless attainment directs attention outward to space “without boundaries” or “endless” as a means of letting go of all *saññā* of specific mental objects. The second directs attention inward to *viññāṇa* without boundaries in order to let go of that and of all *saññā* of any specific mental *subject*. The third is doing both of those so as to bring about the situation where nothing mental or physical, subject or object is experienced as one’s property. This is spelled out in the *Pārāyaṇa-sutta* in relation to the question of Posāla (Sn 1112–15), where what is seen is that “both within and without [I] have nothing.” The Buddha’s two-verse reply refers to a *tathāgata* as knowing the [seven] abidings of consciousness, *viññāṇaṭṭhiti* (which culminate in the *ākiñcaññāyatana*) and, in the final verse, refers to the origin of the state of having nothing. Since the *Pārāyaṇa-sutta* as a whole begins with a brahmin seeking possessionlessness, there can hardly be any doubt that there is a deliberate play here on the meaning of material poverty as against the inner poverty of the third formless attainment.

<!-- Page 72 -->

I do not think it is without reason that in the second century c.e. Aśvaghoṣa explains the teachings of Āḷāra the Kālāma, founded on this base, in terms of Sā*ṃ*khya. Aśvaghoṣa’s understanding was inevitably based on the ideas of his day and to that extent was certainly anachronistic. What we are dealing with here is, as Gerald Larson and Ram Bhattacharya put it, is “*sāṃkhya* not yet as a fixed philosophical system, but as a general methodology of salvation by knowing or reasoning.”[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-183 "note") There is no reason to doubt that some specific proto-Sā*ṃ*khya ideas may go back a long way. If one supposes that what was taught was the idea of a knower utterly separate from ordinary mind and body, free and independent, then that could indeed be a condition in which one has given up possession of or identification with everything else—a fitting goal for wandering ascetics (*śramaṇa*s)!

I think Aśvaghoṣa’s understanding is correct: “That which has perception (*saṃjñā*) is called the knower of the field because it is cognizant of the field of [the natural states (*prakṛti*s) and their transformations (*vikāra*s)] and those who reflect on the soul declare that the soul is the knower of the field.”[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-184 "note") He quite carefully identifies the Sā*ṃ*khya term the “knower of the field” (*kṣetrajña*) and the Buddhist term “the one who is percipient” (*saṃjñin*). Later he gives Arāḍa’s description of the third formless attainment. After having gained that, when the knower of the field has gone forth from the body, like a bird from its cage, it is named “liberated.” “That is the supreme *brahman* without distinguishing marks, immovable, and undecaying, which thoughtful knowers of the real declare to be liberation.”[[36]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-185 "note")

Turning now to the fourth formless attainment, in one way that too is clear. As we saw, just as the fourth *jhāna* is the attainment of the third kind of feeling, so the fourth formless attainment is the attainment of the third kind of *saññā*—a *saññā* that transcends the opposition between the largely rejected state of the *asaññin* and the supreme form of *saññā* exemplified in the state of having nothing. Not surprisingly, there is a certain ambiguity in the tradition between viewing this as a very subtle kind of *saññā* and saying that it is not really any kind of *saññā*. The Theravādin tradition generally seems to take the former view, while the Sarvāstivādins may have adopted the latter position.

<!-- Page 73 -->

We also see this attainment referred to in the *Saṃyutta*- and *Aṅguttara-nikāya*s as the “summit of existence” (*bhavānaṃ aggo*) or “highest of the kinds of being (*bhavagga*).”[[37]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-186 "note") An earlier version of this is perhaps the statement in the *Āneñjasappāya-sutta* (M 106) that the base that has neither *saññā* nor absence of *saññā* is the best kind of thing that is grasped (*upādāna)*.[[38]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-187 "note") In the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* (AN V 64) we also find the statement that some mendicants and brahmins make known “a purification that is the highest goal (*paramatthavisuddhi*)”—that is, the fourth formless attainment.

Aśvaghoṣa’s description of the teaching of Udraka may not be too far off the mark. Strictly speaking, he gives first Siddhārtha’s criticism of the teaching of Arāḍa, but this seems to be intended to represent in part Udraka’s position. The soul at this stage is criticized because it is not without *saṃkhyā* and the like and cannot therefore be considered without qualities (*guṇa*).[[39]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-188 "note") A little further on, we have two stanzas that spell out very clearly that the issue is about “knowing” and “not-knowing.”[[40]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-189 "note") The argument presented is that a “knower of the field” (*kṣetrajña*) without a body is either a knower (*jña*)—that is, a *saññin*—or a “non-knower” (*ajña*)—that is, a “non-percipient one” (*asaññin*). In the former case he is not liberated; in the latter, what is the use of imagining such a soul—you can get an absence of knowledge even without a soul, as with a log or a wall.

Udraka himself is presented as seeing the defect in both having labeling or perception (*saṃjñā*) and in being without it (*asaṃjñā*).[[41]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-190 "note") “Discernment (*buddhi*)[[42]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-191 "note") is fixed just there, not wandering elsewhere; therefore, because it is subtle and indistinct, there is in that no condition of being without *saṃjñā* nor one of having *saṃjñā*.” The exact force of the notion of transcending the *asaññā*/*saññā* distinction here is uncertain, but possibly the idea is to generate some kind of monistic or non-dual experience.

<!-- Page 74 -->

### ***The Fourth Aggregate***

If the system based on the *jhāna*s is concerned mainly with the second aggregate of feeling and the system derived from the eight freedoms (*vimokkha*s) is concerned mainly with the third aggregate of *saññā*, then we might also expect to find meditative methods concerned with the fourth aggregate: *saṅkhāra*s. I will not address that in detail at this point, but will simply draw attention to one or two points.

An interesting label is applied to the fourth formless attainment: it is known occasionally as the attainment with a residue of *saṅkhāras* (*saṅkhārāvasesasamāpatti*).[[43]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-192 "note") The term seems to derive from the *Pañcattaya-sutta* (M 102), which indicates the following (M II 231–32): “If any mendicants or brahmins proclaim that this base can be attained simply by activities (*saṅkhāra*) connected with what is seen, heard, sensed, discriminated,[[44]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-193 "note") that is taught as a real disaster for attaining this base. For this base, monks, is not taught as attainable by an attainment with *saṅkhāra*s. This base, monks, is taught as attainable by an attainment with a residue of *saṅkhāra*s.”

It would also be possible to translate the last phrase, “an attainment with only activities (*saṅkhāra*s) remaining.” This seems to be the position of some later thinkers, and possibly that of the Sarvāstivādins. Vasubandhu refers to the summit of existence (*bhavāgra*) as produced by activities (*saṃskāra*s) alone (*saṃskāramātra*), which seems to be a Sanskritization of this understanding. For the later Theravādin writers (following Kv 263–66), however, feeling (*saññā*) and, for that matter, all activities or constructions (*saṅkhāra*s) exist, at this level, in some skillful or subtle form. This seems logical, since there would be no point in a subsequent cessation of feeling and *saññā*, if they had already ceased.

The expression is also found in the *Sattadhātu*-*sutta* (SN II 150–51), where the elements of radiance and beauty together with the elements corresponding to the first three formless attainments are said to be attained by an attainment with *saññā*. The element of the base with neither *saññā* nor absence of *saññā* is said to be attained by the *saṅkhārāvasesa* attainment, while the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing (*saññā-vedayita-nirodha*) element is only attained by the attainment of cessation.[[45]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-194 "note")

<!-- Page 75 -->

Where we are given information about the process for attaining the *saññā-vedayita-nirodha* that often succeeds the four formless attainments, it does indeed involve some kind of move to reduce or make more subtle the activity of *saṅkhāra* and especially the element of directed will. This seems to be the motivation behind the emphasis on the absence of a deliberate intention to achieve this cessation. The *Cūḷavedalla*-*sutta* (M 44) expresses this clearly: the monk who is attaining the *saññā-vedayita-nirodha* doesn’t think in such terms as “I will attain” it or “I am attaining” it or “I have attained” it. It is simply that his mind has been previously so developed (*bhāvita*) that it leads to such a condition (*tathattāya upaneti*).[[46]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-195 "note")

But passages that refer to this cessation other than as part of a set list are very few. So it is rather difficult to be quite sure what is intended.

Primarily, however, the system of meditation in the Buddhist texts that is concerned with activities or constructions (*saṅkhāra*s) is insight meditation. So it is perhaps to be expected that at this point there is something of a convergence between insight-oriented practices and concentration-based methods.

## **A Possible History**

On the basis of what has been said so far, one could reconstruct a historical development as follows:

* The *bodhisatta* probably learned from his first teacher the list of the seven abidings of consciousness (*viññāṇaṭṭhiti*s), the *kasiṇāyatana*s, and the spheres of mastery (*abhibhāyatana*s), together with some type of classification of *samādhi*. This was probably not yet as four *jhāna*s but rather something like a threefold classification. From his second teacher he would have learned at least the three kinds of *saññā* and probably the seven abodes of beings.
* The system of the four *jhāna*s and the four formless attainments is subsequent to these and must be the Buddha’s own creation.
* The addition of the *saññāvedayita* cessation to create the list of the nine successive abidings (*anupubbavihāra*s) and the formation of the list of the eight freedoms (*vimokkha*s) came later—either in the Buddha’s own lifetime or subsequently.

<!-- Page 76 -->

# 4. The Roots of Insight Meditation

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

We now turn to the development of insight meditation as presented in the texts. I am going to examine what was meant at the time of the compilation of the *nikāya*s and earlier by the distinction between two types of *arahat*: liberated by wisdom and liberated in both ways or liberated on two sides. For this, we need to explore the evolution of this concept in the debates of the early commentarial period—with the resolution of different ideas initially put forward, as presented in the works of the school of Buddhaghosa and in subsequent literature. This brings us to the relationship between insight and calm in the Theravāda tradition and, briefly, to the goal of Buddhist practice and how it relates to calm and insight.

So far we have seen how *samatha*, or calm meditation, is presented both systematically in the commentarial literature and then, in the more colorful version that we find in the canonical texts, in the varied accounts of *jhāna*. We then looked at the formless attainments: it looks as if these are likely to be part of the earliest teachings of Buddhism and not, as some have suggested, a relatively late introduction. My argument is that the methods of meditation given in the *nikāya*s are related to the system of the five aggregates (*khandha*s): the four *jhāna*s are directly related to the second aggregate of *vedanā* (feeling), while the four formless attainments are related to the third aggregate of *saññā* (labeling, recognition, or identification) in a closely parallel manner. The distinctive terminology used to describe some of the formless attainments can be accounted for if it was in part an inheritance from the Buddha’s own early teachers.

From this, we can conclude that the *bodhisatta* probably learned from his first teacher the list of the seven abidings of consciousness (*viññāṇaṭṭhiti*s), together with some type of classification of concentration (*samādhi*). From his second teacher he would have learned at least the three kinds of *saññā* and probably the seven abodes of beings. The system of the four *jhāna*s and the four formless attainments is subsequent to these and would be the Buddha’s own creation. These are meditative methods intended especially for work on the second and third aggregates, to be used alongside methods of developing wisdom linked particularly to the fourth and fifth aggregates.

<!-- Page 77 -->

## **One Who Takes Insight as Their Vehicle**

How can we understand insight? As we shall see in chapter 8, forms of Southern Buddhist meditation that describe themselves as *vipassanā* or “insight” are widely popular at the present time and have spread far beyond the confines of the Theravāda Buddhist countries. The historical origins of this modern tradition are far from clear, but it certainly has a basis in the ancient texts of the Theravāda school and refers itself especially to the works of the famous commentator Buddhaghosa (fourth or fifth century c.e.), a monk from the Asian mainland who wrote mainly in Ceylon on the basis of earlier commentarial writings that are mostly no longer extant.

For Buddhaghosa, in the *Visuddhimagga*, insight can be aroused on the basis of one or more of the *jhāna*s and formless attainments. Alternatively, it can be developed independently, whether or not one has previously experienced such attainments. In the case of one who does not have such experience, this latter approach is referred to as that of “the one who takes insight as his vehicle (*vipassanāyānika*).” That is contrasted with the “one who takes calm as his vehicle” (*samathayānika*).[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-196 "note") This presents the insight path as an alternative route to the classical development of the *jhāna*s and so on. Such a clearly demarcated choice is not found spelled out in earlier Pali sources. Even the earlier *Vimuttimagga*, preserved only in Chinese translation and a chapter in Tibetan, does not present it in this way.

Both the *Vimuttimagga* and the *Visuddhimagga* do, however, use an interpretation of the same canonical list as the basis for their account of the stages of insight—the sequence of the seven purifications (*visuddhi*s).

### ***The Stages of Insight in the* Visuddhimagga**

The first two purifications concern the keeping of the precepts, which provides the moral basis for meditation practice and the purification of the mind by means of *jhāna* meditation or something similar to that. Insight proper begins with purification of view, the third of the seven purifications (*visuddhi*s)—defined as seeing mental and physical phenomena exactly as they are and hence equivalent to insight knowledge that comprehends to a certain degree the First Noble Truth. The fourth purification, the one accomplished by crossing over doubt, is defined as comprehending the conditions that give rise to phenomena and hence as equivalent to understanding the Second Noble Truth. All of this might be expressed in terms of realizing the worthless and relative nature of ordinary existence and ceasing to overvalue it—not merely conceptually but rather as an existential realization and letting go.

<!-- Page 78 -->

The fifth purification—due to knowing and seeing what is path (*magga*) and what is not path—is explained as equivalent to comprehending and developing the Fourth Noble Truth—that is, the Eightfold Path. It is achieved by seeing the general characteristics of phenomena: impermanence, suffering, and not-self. From this arises what is called young insight (*taruṇa-vipassanā*), equivalent to a direct perception of the constant rise and fall of phenomena. At this point the *Visuddhimagga* introduces a description of the ten defilements of insight.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-197 "note") Most of them are states that appear in themselves desirable: joy, knowledge, tranquility, happiness, mindfulness, and equanimity are included in the list. Their danger is that a meditator may be overwhelmed by the intensity of these experiences and imagine that they have already obtained the goal. Only when one is able to distinguish these tempting but illusory states from the true path is the fifth purification completed and only then can the meditator continue this journey.

The *Visuddhimagga* continues with the sixth purification and gives a full account of the eight contemplation knowledges and the two transitional knowledges that follow. This is the stage of strong insight (*balava-vipassanā*). Buddhaghosa explains the first seven knowledges with a simile. A man fishing with a net reaches into it and takes hold of a snake. Thinking it to be a fish, he is delighted. This symbolizes our delighting in *saṃsāra*. When he sees the three marks on the head of the poisonous snake, his delight changes to fear. This represents the first two contemplation knowledges that comprehend the three signs of impermanence, suffering, and not-self and lead to our realizing that *saṃsāra* is a fearful place. Realizing his danger, the man loses his delight at the supposed fish in his hand and feels a strong desire to be free from it. Similarly, the meditator, knowing the danger in compounded things, becomes disenchanted with them and arouses a strong desire for liberation. The fisherman seeks to get free and unwraps the coils from his arm, starting with the tail. This corresponds to the contemplations on emptiness (*suññatā*) that characterize the seventh contemplation knowledge. Finally, the fisherman swings the snake two or three times around his head in order to weaken it and throws it away from him. He then scrambles back up on to dry land and stands, looking back at the way he came and thinking, “I am freed from a great snake.”

<!-- Page 79 -->

A modern master writes, “Of the nine aspects [of insight knowledge] … not all of them occur to all aspirants. Even their occurrence does not necessarily take place in that order. Any one of the seven may take place, to be followed immediately by the eighth and the ninth.”[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-198 "note") It is not clear how far Buddhaghosa would agree with this, but the difference is perhaps fairly typical of the kind of variation one finds between the practice tradition and the theoretical literature.

The eighth contemplation knowledge is portrayed as a stage in which no further effort is required and equipoise is well established. It is perhaps worth mentioning that at this point the meditator can only wait for the right conditions to occur for the breakthrough to the transcendent. Buddhaghosa illustrates it with the simile of the land-finding crow that may be carried on board ship.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-199 "note") When released, it will go toward land if there is any in sight. If not, it will remain on board. Likewise, if this knowledge sees *nibbāna*, the sphere of peace, it will abandon the whole process of conditioned things and leap forth to *nibbāna*. If not, it will continue with conditioned things as its object.

That process of actual attainment of the *magga*—that is, the path in the sense of the first direct experience of *nibbāna*—is addressed with two further knowledges, but I will pass over that here and for the moment also the discussions of the nature and development of the awakened state.

## **The Literary Sources of the *Vipassanā* Tradition**

If we take the two main features of the insight tradition as, firstly, the acceptance of routes to enlightenment that bypass the development of *jhāna* and, secondly, the mapping of the sequence of insight knowledges, then the immediate source is, as already mentioned, the *Visuddhimagga*. It is with the second of these features that I am more concerned here—the insight knowledges. The *Visuddhimagga* describes what later tradition takes to be the enlarged list of the sixteen insight knowledges in considerable detail. In the earlier *Vimuttimagga*, which was probably Buddhaghosa’s model, most of these (if not all) are present, but in a very abbreviated form.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-200 "note") The additional material, as so often in the *Visuddhimagga*, is derived from a late canonical text, the *Paṭisambhidāmagga.*

<!-- Page 80 -->

Perhaps even more important in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* than the knowledges of the first section is a list of thirty-seven ways of experiencing the Buddhist path, a list that recurs on at least thirty different occasions in this work. Part of this list, following the eight “*jhāna*s” and preceding the four paths, is another and closely related sequence known to the commentaries as the eighteen great insights (*mahāvipassanā*s). It is fairly obvious that the commentarial account of the stages of insight is largely built up from these and other materials provided by the *Paṭisambhidāmagga*. This is not to say that the *Visuddhimagga* account would have been completely acceptable or even recognizable to the author of the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* (traditionally Sāriputta, the exemplar of wisdom). Looking at it the other way around, however, *Paṭisambhidāmagga* already sets forth a fairly sophisticated account of the stages of insight—an account that does provide the main features of the later model. This is clear enough if we note that it begins with detailed analysis of phenomena, moves on to contemplation of rise and fall, then to breaking up (*bhaṅga*), to the experiencing of some kind of sense of danger, and subsequently to the establishment of a settled state of equipoise. After all this, there follows the transitional stage of gaining the lineage, prior to the path knowledge itself.

## **Insight in the Earlier Period**

The material in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* shows us that the elaborated versions of the path of insight begin to take form in the period of the development of the later canonical literature, probably in the last centuries b.c.e. The question is what are the earlier sources, if any, that provide the basis for these enlarged versions. The *nikāya*s contain a large quantity of such material. I shall give as an illustration the sequence from knowledge and vision of things just as they are (*yathābhūtañāṇadassana*) to disenchantment (*nibbidā*) to dispassion (*virāga*) to freedom (*vimutti*).[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-201 "note") Since the first of these may represent the beginnings of insight and the last its result, we can take the two central terms as representing the heart of the process of insight. Indeed they, or their verbal forms, occur rather frequently in the Pali Canon. Overall, disenchantment can be seen as the parallel in insight meditation to joy or energization (*pīti*) in calm meditation. So, in the one case, joy if successfully tranquilized, leads to happiness and success in controlling one’s emotional life. In the other, disenchantment with the things with which one identifies and the notions to which one clings leads to a mental clarity and a deepening of knowledge.

<!-- Page 81 -->

The importance of what is later referred to as the insight knowledges is then already clear in the *nikāya*s. What is less clear is the context in which we are to understand this. Where insight occurs in a sequence, it usually comes after concentration or after emerging from one of the *jhāna*s. Often it occurs without such a context, but in dealing with some aspect of what may be called fundamental theory: such lists as the aggregates, bases, elements, truths, conditioned origination, and the like. It is quite impossible to be sure whether in these passages insight is intended to operate as an exercise even for a beginner, or whether all these passages are addressed to someone who has already developed *jhāna*.

We can say that in the canon, the development of insight after the *jhāna*s is certainly the normative pattern, where a full process is described at all. I exclude from consideration those cases where the *dhamma* eye and the like are said to arise at the end of a discourse, apparently spontaneously. There is no indication, or at least not much indication, of the prior background in most such cases and hence no way of telling if it is envisaged that *jhāna* had already been developed. And the Buddhist tradition assumes that such things may be the product of practice in a previous life.

Modern traditions of insight meditation often lay stress on insight as the Buddha’s particular achievement. In this view *samatha* meditation involving the development of the four *jhāna*s is something pre-Buddhist, even perhaps “Hindu”—something that is not necessary for enlightenment. It is interesting to contrast this with the view of some modern scholars that the development of the *jhāna*s is the typically Buddhist form of practice, or at least that most likely to have been developed by the Buddha himself.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-202 "note")

I wish now to consider how far the view that only insight is the proper or specifically Buddhist practice is actually justified in the canonical works. It is certainly true that there is some stress in that literature on the fact that the stages of sanctity are not found outside the Buddhist tradition. There is also, as was mentioned above, some emphasis that the teaching of the four noble truths is particular to the buddhas. Sometimes too we read that the four establishings of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*s) are a path that is *ekâyana*. This term is often explained as “the only way,” but Rupert Gethin’s excellent discussion of this states the situation differently:

<!-- Page 82 -->

> Given that nowhere is the sense “one and one only” clearly and definitely the proper sense, and in most cases definitely not, it seems rather perverse to adopt this sense in the *satipaṭṭhāna* context.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-203 "note")

We should probably think rather of mindfulness as leading to only one destination, namely, *nibbāna*. We should also note that it is not mindfulness alone that is referred to here, but the four establishings of mindfulness—that is to say, states in which one is mindful, energetic, and aware. Or, as the tradition understands, mindfulness accompanied by vigor (*viriya*) and wisdom (*paññā*).

To set against this are many passages where the relationship between *samādhi* and *paññā* or between calm and insight is stressed. The later commentarial tradition does accept that there were *arahat*s “liberated by wisdom” (*paññāvimutta*) who had not developed all or even any of the four *jhāna*s.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-204 "note") I turn now to examine this notion in detail.

### **Arahat*s Liberated by Wisdom in the List of the Seven Persons***

A list of seven persons (*puggala*s) is preserved in a number of places in the Pali Canon. The full list is found twice in the *Dīgha-nikāya*, twice in the *Majjhima-nikāya*, and once in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-205 "note") It is also referred to in *The Points of Controversy* (*Kathāvatthu*) and a number of times in *The Designation of Human Types* (*Puggalapaññatti*).[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-206 "note") It was part of the shared heritage of all or many Buddhist schools; essentially the same list is found in the Sarvāstivādin equivalents to the *Sampasādanīya-sutta* (D 28)[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-207 "note") and the *Saṅgīti-sutta* (D 33)[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-208 "note") as well as in the exegetical literature of the Vaibhāṣikas and Vaibhāṣika-influenced *ābhidharmika*s. It is also recorded in Sanskrit in the Mahāyāna literature of the Yogācāra school.

The significance of the list is made clear in one of the *sutta*s in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*. The set of the seven persons is referred to as those “who are worthy of veneration with offerings, worthy of hospitality, worthy of gifts, worthy of respectful greeting—an incomparable field in which merit grows for the whole world.”[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-209 "note") In other words, they are the noble community (*ariyasaṅgha*), and the list corresponds to the eight kinds of person (*purisapuggalā*) in the well-known chant for recollection of the *saṅgha*: those who have attained the path and the fruit of stream-entry, once return, never return, and *arahat*ship.

<!-- Page 83 -->

This list can be set out in tabular form, as in [Table 4](#tbl4).

### **Table 4: Methods of Attaining the Four Stages of the Path and Their Fruits**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| The one freed in both ways (*ubhatobhāgavimutto*) | The fruit of *arahat*ship (*arahattaphala*) |
| The one freed by wisdom (*paññāvimutto*) |
| The body witness (*kāyasakkhin*) | From the fruit of stream-entry (*sotāpattiphala*) to the path of *arahat*ship (*arahattamagga*) |
| The one who has attained to view (*diṭṭhippatto*) |
| The one freed by faith (*saddhāvimutto*) |
| The follower of the teachings (*dhammânusārin*) | The path of stream-entry (*sotāpattimagga*) |
| The follower by faith (*saddhânusārin*) |

We can note also that expanded versions of the list are also found*. The Designation of Human Types* (*Puggalapaññatti*) (Pp 10) has a list of nine persons, adding the Sammāsambuddha and the Paccekasambuddha, while a *sutta* in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* has ten, including also the “one who has entered the lineage” (*gotrabhū*)—that is, one who is on the verge of becoming an *ariya*. Two additional *Aṅguttara-nikāya sutta*s list the seven with two kinds of non-*ariya*: the person of beautiful nature who keeps the precepts and the person of evil nature who does not keep the precepts.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-210 "note") Sanskrit sources also include the list in even longer sets, such as the eighteen *śaikṣa*s[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-211 "note") or the twenty-eight persons.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-212 "note")

What we can notice about the above list is that it falls into three sections: a pair, a group of three, and another pair. The middle group of three is also dealt with separately in the *Samiddha-sutta* (AN I 118f.), where three *arahat*s indicate their preference as to which of these should be considered more splendid and more refined. The Buddha, however, indicates that any one of the group of three could be nearer to *arahat*ship than the other two; so an unequivocal answer cannot be given. What the *sutta* does show is a clear relationship between these three persons and three of the five faculties or lordships (*indriya*s), as in [Table 5](#tbl5).

### **Table 5: Three Kinds of Persons and Faculties**

<!-- Page 84 -->

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| The body witness (*kāyasakkhin*) | Concentration (*samādhi*) |
| The one attained to view (*diṭṭhippatto*) | Wisdom (*paññā*) |
| The one freed by faith (*saddhāvimutto*) | Faith (*saddhā*) |

The *Samiddha-sutta* does not indicate any such relationship between the faculties and the two pairs given before and after the set of three. In the case of the first pair, however, it is not difficult to infer that there is a relationship between the one who progresses by faith and the faith faculty, and likewise between the one who progresses by *dhamma* and the wisdom faculty. Yet, this is not stated in the *Kīṭāgiri-sutta* (M 70; MN I 477f.), where instead it is emphasized that both the one who progresses by faith and the one who progresses by *dhamma* have all the five faculties. Moreover, both here and in the *Tissabrahma-sutta* (AN IV 77f.) it is emphasized for each of the first five (who are not *arahat*s) that what they need to do is resort to suitable accommodation, frequent inspirational (*kalyāṇa*) friends, and “direct along together” (*samannānayamāno*) the five faculties.

On the surface, this might seem to contradict the indications of the *Samiddha-sutta* for the middle three, but the contradiction is only superficial. What is intended here is that all five faculties must be active and, in some degree, balanced to develop the *ariya* stages and, indeed, all forms of higher consciousness. In other words, if someone has a natural tendency toward wisdom, that may have harmful results for them if not balanced with the other faculties, especially faith. But if the other faculties are active, that same tendency toward wisdom may be the principal means for them to progress. This is expressed in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* by saying that the other four faculties follow after that (*tadanvayā*), or by statements such as that the other four faculties are developed, thoroughly developed on the basis of the faith faculty.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-213 "note")

The above-mentioned implied correspondence of the one who progresses by faith and the one who progresses by *dhamma* with the faith and the wisdom faculties respectively is explicit in *The Designation of Human Types* (*Puggalapaññatti*). Probably related to this is *The Guide* (*Nettipakaraṇa*)’s declaration that at the time of stream-entry, it is one of the craving type who becomes one who progresses by faith and a person of the views type who becomes one who progresses by *dhamma*.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-214 "note") But this leaves us with two questions. Why are there no *ariya*s whose attainment is principally due to the vigor (*viriya*) and mindfulness (*sati*) faculties? And why is there no one who enters stream-entry on the basis of a natural tendency toward concentration (*samādhi*)? As to the first question, one can only suggest that these two faculties, when strong, do not allow the mind to settle into the object of absorption and comprehension.

<!-- Page 85 -->

With the second question the issue seems clearer. The *Paṭisambhidāmagga* fills the gap by applying the label of body witness (*kāyasakkhin*) also to the stream-enterer who develops the path on the basis of insight into suffering and the concentration faculty. But this does not really explain why only two of those listed in [Table 5](#tbl5) are given in the list of seven in [Table 4](#tbl4). But I will turn now to the third and final component of the set of seven persons. As we shall see, only later sources give a reason[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-215 "note") for the absence of an *ariya* who attains stream-entry on the basis of the concentration faculty.

### ***The One Freed by Wisdom and the One Freed in Both Ways***

The final pair of persons is the “one freed by wisdom” (*paññāvimutta*) and the “one freed in both respects or in both ways” (*ubhatobhāgavimutta*). I will discuss the precise meaning of the latter term in due course. I have called this the final pair, taking the list in ascending order, as it were, but in the canonical texts it is always given in descending order, beginning with the one freed in both ways. The most detailed treatment of the seven persons found in a single place in the *sutta*s is in the *Kīṭāgiri-sutta* (M 70; MN I 473–481). Here we find that what distinguishes each of these two from the other five persons is that “seeing by means of one’s wisdom the corruptions (*āsava*s) are entirely destroyed.” In the case of all, or most, of the others, we are merely told that “seeing by means of one’s wisdom some corruptions are entirely destroyed.” That indicates that these two persons are *arahat*s, as is also made clear in various other passages. For example, in the *Tissabrahma-sutta* (AN IV 74–9), *deva*s declare of both of these, “As long as his body abides, for so long *deva*s and men will see him. After the breakup of the body *deva*s and men will not see him.” And this is strongly implied in the *Pavāraṇā-sutta* (SN I 190–92) when the Buddha divides the five hundred monks who are present into sixty with the threefold knowledge, sixty with the six kinds of higher knowledge (*abhiññā*), sixty who are freed in both ways, and the remainder who are freed by wisdom. It is explicitly stated that all the five hundred are free from blame as regards any bodily or verbal acts. The *arahat* Sāriputta too is described as “freed in both respects (*vimuttaṃ ubhatobhāge*)” in *The Verses of the Elder Monks* (*Theragāthā*) (Th 1176). In the case of the *paññāvimutta* this is also indicated in the *Susīma-sutta* (SN II 120–28), where monks declare themselves to be *paññāvimutta* in the context of the declaration of the knowledge (*aññā*) of *arahat*ship.

<!-- Page 86 -->

The difference between the “one freed by wisdom” and the “one freed in both respects or in both ways” is explained in the *Kīṭāgiri-sutta* as whether or not that person dwells experiencing with the body the peaceful freedoms that are formless and have passed beyond forms. The one freed in both ways does experience such freedoms; the one freed by wisdom does not and neither do any of the other five persons except for the body witness (*kāyasakkhin*), whose special characteristic is concentration (*samādhi*). This resembles in many ways the explanation at the climax of the *Mahānidāna-sutta* (D 15; DN II 68–71) where the same two kinds of *arahat* are distinguished partly on the basis of the eight freedoms (*vimokkha*s). The *arahat* who is freed by wisdom thoroughly understands the seven abidings of consciousness (*viññāṇaṭṭhiti*s) and the two spheres (*āyatana*s)—how they arise and pass away, what is attractive in them and what is inferior and the way of escape from them and so is completely (*anupādā*) freed.

By contrast, the *arahat* who is freed in both ways has complete mastery of the eight freedoms (*vimokkha*s)—such *arahat*s can enter and emerge from these meditative states in any order, wherever and whenever they wish, for as long as they wish. And, as a result of the destruction of the corruptions, they have realized for themselves through higher knowledge in this very life freedom of mind and freedom through wisdom without corruptions, and they have entered into that and abide in it. The list of the seven abidings of consciousness with the two spheres covers much of the same ground as the list of the eight freedoms: both sequences have at their core the four formless attainments. In the *Kīṭāgiri-sutta* (M 70) there is only mention of the peaceful formless freedoms, but that is simply another way of describing the same core sequence.

In the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* (AN IV 452f.), three successive *sutta*s treat the body witness, the one freed by wisdom, and the one freed in both respects. Here the treatment employs rather the sequence of the four *jhāna*s, followed by the four formless attainments and the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing. We learn that, for purposes of teaching (*pariyāyena*), even someone who has complete mastery of just the first *jhāna* can be considered a body witness. Strictly speaking, however, only one who both has such mastery all the way to the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing, and having seen that attainment by means of wisdom has thoroughly destroyed the corruptions (*āsava*s), is unequivocally a body witness. This, if it is not a textual error, would seem to suggest that only an *arahat* can be a body witness in the fullest degree.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-216 "note")

<!-- Page 87 -->

Similarly in the second *sutta*, for purposes of teaching, it is said that anyone who can enter and abide in the first *jhāna* and who understands it through wisdom, can be referred to as freed by wisdom. The same applies in the case of the following stages. Strictly speaking, however, only someone who can both enter and abide in the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing and having seen by means of their wisdom has thoroughly destroyed the corruptions and has also seen that attainment by means of wisdom is unequivocally one freed by wisdom. The one who is freed in both respects differs from this only in that, like the body witness, they can also abide contacting any one of these spheres with their body as they wish.

There is no indication here that one who is freed by wisdom would not have developed the full set of *jhāna*s and formless attainments. However, in the *Susīma-sutta* (SN II 119–128), a group of *arahat*s freed by wisdom are questioned by Susīma and explicitly state that (a) they have attained *arahat*ship, and (b) they have not developed any of the following:

1. The various kinds of psychic power (*iddhi*)
2. The divine ear element
3. The knowledge that encompasses the minds of others
4. The divine eye
5. The ability to dwell experiencing with the body the peaceful freedoms that are formless and have passed beyond forms

There is no suggestion here that they have *not* developed the four *jhāna*s, and indeed the fact that only the formless liberations are mentioned strongly implies that they indeed have developed the *jhāna*s. Nor can we assume that what is stated here applies to all those freed by wisdom. So there need be no contradiction with the indications given in the discourses of the *Dīgha-nikāya* and *Majjhima-nikāya*. What we must understand is that the *arahat* liberated by wisdom has experienced the formless attainments to a sufficient degree to fully understand them and thereby free the *arahat*’s mind from the very subtle defilements that can bind the mind to such levels of experience. What such *arahat*s have not done is free their minds from these defilements by developing thorough mastery of those states.

We have seen in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* that anyone who can enter and abide in the first *jhāna* and who understands it through wisdom can be referred to as freed by wisdom by way of exposition (*pariyāyena*). But such a one is not stated to have thoroughly destroyed the corruptions; so they are not an *arahat*. Only one who has experienced all the nine stages of the sequence from *jhāna*s to the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing (*saññā-vedayita-nirodha*) and has thoroughly destroyed the corruptions by means of wisdom is unequivocally (*nippariyāyena*) one liberated through wisdom.

<!-- Page 88 -->

Since the *abhidhamma* is the non-expository teaching of the Buddha—that is, the teaching given without regard to the specific needs of the audience and the teaching situation—it is not surprising that in the *abhidhamma* account, for both the one who is freed in both ways and the one freed by wisdom, only the last case is referred to: the one who has thoroughly destroyed the corruptions. But what differentiates the two kinds of *arahat* is that the first “dwells having contacted the eight freedoms with his body,” whereas the one freed by wisdom does not. We should not conclude from this that the *arahat* freed by wisdom has never experienced any of the eight freedoms at all—that is, has not developed any of the *jhāna*s or formless attainments. Rather the implication is that they have enough experience of these to have insight into their nature but have not developed mastery.

### ***The Eight Freedoms (*Vimokkha*s)***

What is striking is that in nearly all these cases there is a strong relationship between the list of the seven persons and the set of the eight freedoms. That indicates a context that belongs to the very earliest teaching of the Buddha—a time when he was basing a significant part of his teaching on the structures he had learned from his two earlier teachers. These include lists whose core was the four formless attainments or some of them. We have the set of the seven abidings of consciousness (*viññāṇaṭṭhiti*s) whose culmination is the sphere of owning nothing, taught by Āḷāra the Kālāma. A similar list culminating in the sphere of neither labeling nor absence of labeling (*nevasaññānāsaññāyatana*) must have been taught by Uddaka and his father Rāma. It is on this basis that the Buddha will have taught the eight freedoms, adding his own contribution of the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing (*saññā-vedayita-nirodha*).

Looked at in this way, the distinction between the *arahat* freed in both respects and the *arahat* freed by wisdom would be part of the earliest Buddhist teaching. Later, when the Buddha introduced his own system of four *jhāna*s and four formless attainments, linked to the system of the five aggregates, the pair could easily be adapted to that. But at this stage there is no indication that there could be an *arahat* freed by wisdom who had not developed the *jhāna*s at all.

<!-- Page 89 -->

It is important at this point to remind ourselves of a major development in the understanding of what is meant by *jhāna*. In general, in the *suttanta* teachings, no distinction is made between approach (*upacārajjhāna*) and absorption (*appanājjhāna*). Such a distinction is clearly intended in some form in the *Abhidhamma-piṭaka*, since the consciousness of the *jhāna*s is defined as form-level (*rūpāvacara*)—that is, directed toward a form (*rūpa*) or meditation object—and the object of the mind at that time is defined as a concept. But the notion of approach stages for each of the *jhāna*s and formless attainments is more clearly developed in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga*.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-217 "note")

Given this gradual shift toward a more precise understanding of what is meant by *jhāna*, it is perhaps not surprising to see that the *arahat* freed by wisdom comes to be understood as one who has not developed *jhāna* at all or only the first four *jhāna*s. But we need to be clear that when the *Paññāvimutta-sutta* describes the *arahat* freed by wisdom as having entered all of the *jhāna*s and formless attainments, it is saying exactly the same thing. It is just using a *suttanta* definition of *jhāna* rather than an *abhidhamma* one (AN IV 452–453). The account as we have it in the *sutta* sources can be set out diagrammatically, as in [Table 6](#tbl6).

### **Table 6: Nine Abidings**

|  |
| --- |
| 9. Cessation of labeling and feeling (*saññā-vedayita-nirodha*) |
| 8. Neither labeling nor absence of labeling |
| 7. Possessing nothing |
| 6. Boundless consciousness |
| 5. Boundless space |
| 4. Fourth *jhāna* |
| 3. Third *jhāna* |
| 2. Second *jhāna* |
| 1. First *jhāna* |

This presents the sequence in terms of the nine successive abidings (*anupubbavihāra*s), but earlier it might have been given in terms of the eight freedoms. What we have to understand is that there are two dimensions to the sequence. For convenience I will call them height and depth. An *arahat* must have experienced all or nearly all of these stages into order to be detached from all of these levels of experience—in other words, the *arahat* must have mastered the aspect of height. But not all *arahat*s enter deeply enough into these experiences to acquire mastery and fluidity in handling them. Only those who do so can become freed in both ways. For the later tradition, however, each of these stages (except perhaps the last) is divided into two: approach and absorption. This means that the path for the two kinds of *arahat* can be presented differently. The *arahat* freed in both ways would proceed through the four *jhāna*s (and similarly through the formless stages) as in [Table 7](#tbl7).

<!-- Page 90 -->

### **Table 7: Path of the *Arahat* Freed in Both Ways**

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fourth *jhāna* | 7. approach | 8. absorption |
| Third *jhāna* | 5. approach | 6. absorption |
| Second *jhāna* | 3. approach | 4. absorption |
| First *jhāna* | 1. approach | 2. absorption |

Here the aspect that I have called “depth” is separated out as absorption.

The *arahat* who is freed by wisdom proceeds a little differently, as in [Table 8](#tbl8).

### **Table 8: Path of the *Arahat* Freed by Wisdom**

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fourth *jhāna* | 4. approach | absorption |
| Third *jhāna* | 3. approach | absorption |
| Second *jhāna* | 2. approach | absorption |
| First *jhāna* | 1. approach | absorption |

This could be expressed as saying that such *arahat*s develop only the stage of access for each level, but it is better to suppose that they enter momentarily into absorption of, for example, the first *jhāna* and then pass into access of the second *jhāna* and so on.

What this means is that from the point of view of the *sutta*s, the *arahat* freed by wisdom passes through the sequence of the nine successive abidings from the first *jhāna* to the attainment of cessation. From the point of view of the *abhidhamma*-based commentarial tradition, however, this *arahat* does not develop the *jhāna*s and so on or only does so to a slight degree. What must be emphasized, however, is that these are simply two different ways of describing essentially the same process.

### ***The Discussion Recorded in the Old* Aṭṭhakathā**

The precise meaning of the term “freed in both ways” (*ubhatobhāgavimutta*) has been variously discussed at different points, but the earliest discussion is probably that recorded in the *abhidhamma* commentaries.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-218 "note") There the views are recorded of three elders named Cūḷanāga, Mahādhammarakkhita, and Cūḷābhaya, each of whom is given the title Tipiṭaka, presumably meaning that they had mastered the whole Pali Canon. These three elders appear to have lived in the late first century b.c.e., in the reign of King Kuṭakaṇṇatissa.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-219 "note") Cūḷanāga interpreted *ubhatobhāgavimutta* as meaning “freed twice” and explained this as referring to being freed by an attainment and being freed by the path, with the former being “freedom by suppression” (*vikkhambhana*) and the latter being “freedom by cutting off” (*samuccheda*). Mahādhammarakkhita, however, cited the *Question of Upasīva* in the *Pārāyaṇa*, where the Buddha recommended the practice of the sphere where one has nothing to Upasīva and referred to the sage as released from his mental body.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-220 "note") This is interpreted by \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*) as a situation where Upasīva was previously freed from the form body (*rūpakāya*) and later attains *arahat*ship. Then he is freed from both the form body and the mental body.[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-221 "note") On this basis Mahādhammarakkhita understands *ubhatobhāgavimutta* to mean that one is freed in both ways due to being thoroughly freed from both the form body and the mental body. The third elder, Cūḷābhaya, explained: one is *ubhatobhāgavimutta* because one is freed once by an attainment with the freedom by suppression and once by the path [of *arahat*ship] with the freedom by cutting off.

<!-- Page 91 -->

Also from an older commentary is the “question about the one who is freed both ways” (*ubhatobhāgavimutta*), recorded by Buddhaghosa in the commentary to “The Great Discourse on Cause,” the *Mahānidāna-sutta* (D 15). This apparently arose outside the Lohapāsāda in Anurādhapura and was definitively settled after a long while by another elder with the title of Tipiṭaka named Cūḷasumana. The issue here was whether there were five or six kinds of *arahat* freed in both ways. All agreed that someone who became an *arahat* after emerging from one of the four formless attainments or from the attainment of cessation would also be freed in both ways. Some argued that because of the similarity of the fourth *jhāna* to the formless attainments, someone who became an *arahat* after emerging from that would also be freed in both ways. Buddhaghosa goes on to cite the story of a pupil of Cūḷasumana who was told about the “question about the one who is freed both ways” by a *piṇḍapātika* monk in the Giri Monastery—that is, the Abhayagiri Monastery. The pupil declared, “When our teacher was explaining *dhamma* outside the Lohapāsāda, it was something never previously heard.” Asked about this, he quoted him as saying, “Although form-level (*rūpāvacara*) fourth *jhāna* has two [*jhāna*] factors[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-222 "note") and neutral feeling [like the formless *jhāna*s] and dispels defilements, it belongs with what is near to the defilements and occurs in a place where they spring up. For these defilements occur in five-constituent existence [existence involving the five aggregates (*khandha*s)] in dependence upon one of the objects such as the blue color and form-level *jhāna* does not transcend that object. Therefore only one who has reached *arahat*ship by dispelling the defilements through formless *jhāna* after turning completely away from form is freed in both ways.”[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-223 "note")

That position remains authoritative for the Mahāvihāra school in subsequent Pali literature. But it is not unchallenged. Dhammapāla, the *ṭīkā* writer, attributes the dissenting view here to the other two schools in Ceylon: the *Abhayagirivāsin*s and the teachers of the *Sārasamāsa*.[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-224 "note") This is probably anachronistic, but he does so because in their *Dīgha-nikāya* commentary, the former defined *ubhatobhāgavimutta* as meaning “freed in both ways due to concentration and insight” and, as he says, imagined that one is freed from the obstacles to concentration by means of form-level *jhāna*. That is reminiscent of the explanation in the commentary to *\*The Treasury of Abhidharma* (*Abhidharmakośabhāṣya*): “[The *arhat*] who obtains the attainment of cessation is freed in both respects by the powers of wisdom and concentration from the obstacles (*āvaraṇa*s) of the defilements and the obstacles to the freedoms.”[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-225 "note") This understanding seems fairly general in the northern sources.[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-226 "note")

<!-- Page 92 -->

## **The Later Tradition**
