The *abhidhamma* manuals and the *ṭīkā*s give a fairly consistent statement, following the position of the commentaries of the time of Buddhaghosa. I give here the summary presented in \**A Summary of the Truth* (*Saccasaṅkhepa*):

After reaching a gateway to liberation, that [intelligence] brings about the seven noble persons

and the various kinds of *jhāna* and other factors due to the basic [*jhāna*] and so on.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-227 "note") (348)

For if the emergence of a given yogin was due to impermanence,

[that *yogin*] will have a sharp faculty (*indriya*) of faith because of having plenty of commitment.[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-228 "note") (349)

If it were due to suffering or no-self, the [*yogin*s will have] respectively

sharp faculties of one-pointedness or intelligence because of having plenty of tranquility or knowledge.[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-229 "note") (350)

It has been explained that the yoke is wisdom if emergence is through not-self.

The yoke is faith [if emergence is through] the other [two].

This is because the inclination (*abhinivesa*) of [one with plenty of concentration] is like [that of one with plenty of faith].[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-230 "note") (351)

The two with sharp faith and calm will be ones who “journey through faith”

in the initial [stage and] ones who are “freed by faith” in the middle six cases.[[36]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-231 "note") (352)

The other is one who “journeys through *dhamma*” in the initial [stage], one who has “attained by view” in the remaining stages except the last.

In both cases they are “freed by wisdom” at the last and they may or may not possess [ordinary] *jhāna*.[[37]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-232 "note") (353)

For one whose faith is sharp in the final [stage], the condition of one “freed by faith” has been declared

in *The Path of Purification*[[38]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-233 "note") and for the median case [of one full of concentration] the condition of “body witness” in [all] eight [stages].[[39]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-234 "note") (354)

Both that and the condition of being one who has “attained by view” that was given in the *Mokkha-kathā*

<!-- Page 93 -->

[of *The Path of Discrimination* (*Paṭisambhidāmagga*)[[40]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-235 "note")] for the *arahat* with sharp wisdom are not in the *abhidhamma*.[[41]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-236 "note") (355)

All of these, if they are obtainers of the eight liberations, in the six middle [stages]

will be “body witnesses” and in the final [stage] “both ways liberated.”[[42]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-237 "note") (356)

## **A Possible History**

At an early stage in the promulgation of his teaching, the Buddha set out a list that divided those who had gained some degree of realization of his teaching into seven persons. That list was partly based on the list of eight freedoms—a description of the stages of the path produced by adding the cessation of labeling and feeling or experiencing (*saññā-vedayita-nirodha*) as a final stage after the attainments of the spheres where nothing is owned and the sphere of neither labeling nor absence of labeling, taught by the Buddha’s former teachers. The two kinds of *arahat* that are, as it were, the summit of the list are distinguished by the depth of their experience of the freedoms. Both have seen the corruptions (*āsava*s) with wisdom and completely destroyed them. One has full mastery of the eight freedoms and can contact them as a bodily experience—this one is called “freed in both respects or both ways.” The other does not have that full mastery of the last five freedoms—that one is called “freed by wisdom.” But there is nothing to suggest that the latter has not experienced them at all.

Later, the Buddha adopted a method of teaching based upon the four *jhāna*s and the formless attainments. Almost certainly the classification in terms of four *jhāna*s is the Buddha’s own invention; earlier teachers probably taught in terms of different types of concentration (*samādhi*). This new analysis was then easily adapted to the list of the seven persons.

At a later stage in the development of Buddhist teaching, a distinction is made between approach concentration and absorption concentration. From that perspective, entering deeply into *jhāna* is called absorption and from some points of view is considered as the only real *jhāna*. The two kinds of *arahat* in the list of the seven persons are then reinterpreted. The first remains as before: one who has mastered all the eight freedoms or all of the *jhāna*s and attainments. The second—the *arahat* freed by wisdom—is understood to have not attained the formless stages at all and may or may not have experienced the four form *jhāna*s. That one can then be what the commentaries call the *arahat* who is *sukkhavipassaka*, “one who [just] has insight.” No such idea is found in the *sutta* literature.

<!-- Page 94 -->

Although this may seem a major difference, we should not make too much of it. It may be no more than a different way of describing exactly the same facts.

# 5. *Samatha* and *Vipassanā*

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

Already in 1927 E. J. Thomas wrote the following:

> The *jhāna*s are only four stages in a much more extended scheme. It may of course be the case that they once formed the whole of the mystic process.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-238 "note")

Given that we do not know precisely what developments had already occurred before the time of the Buddha, and given that developments in teaching must have occurred during the long life span of the Buddha himself as well as afterward, it is difficult to say with any certainty what exactly was taught by the Buddha himself. I put forward my own views here and in the last chapter; others will have different opinions. No doubt, like any wise teacher, the Buddha would have wished his followers to develop his teachings in a creative and fruitful manner. The subsequent history of Buddhism is clear enough proof that this was indeed what happened. We can say that for most later forms of Buddhism, in India and elsewhere, the typical Buddhist approach is a synthetic one that seeks to combine differing approaches toward a higher ideal.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-239 "note")

Asked about calm (*samatha*) and insight (*vipassanā*) meditation, a well-known twentieth-century Thai meditation teacher, Ajahn Chah, commented at the end of his reply: “These days many people cling to the words. They call their practice *vipassanā*. *Samatha* is looked down on. Or they call their practice *samatha*. It is essential to do *samatha* before *vipassanā*, they say.”[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-240 "note")

This summarizes a debate that is frequently encountered in Thailand and, using a slightly different terminology, in Sri Lanka also. It is interesting to re-examine the use of these two terms in the earlier Pali literature, in order to see exactly how ancient this kind of usage is and whether there is any clear position on the matter in the classical texts.

Our main concern here is with the use of *samatha* as equivalent to *samādhi* (concentration) and of *vipassanā* as equivalent to *paññā* (wisdom). Not surprisingly, this generally falls into the context of descriptions of the Buddhist path. Sometimes we find the two terms as part of a sequence outlining the stages of the path in general. Or sometimes they occur in descriptions of the Fourth Noble Truth or its expansion in the grouping of seven lists known later as the thirty-seven factors contributing to awakening (*bodhi-pakkhiya-dhamma*s). To appreciate fully the usage of *samatha* and *vipassanā* in the *nikāya*s, it is therefore necessary to understand certain aspects of the standard accounts of the Buddhist path.

<!-- Page 95 -->

## **The Structure of the Path**

Among the most elaborate accounts of the path found in the Pali Canon are those found in the “Section on Moral Conduct” (*Sīlakkhandha*), the first third of the *Dīgha-nikāya*. It is perhaps more correct to speak of a single account, repeated with slight modifications in many of the *sutta*s. Since the main changes are to the way in which the path as a whole is structured, it seems to me that this is mainly a question of “ringing the changes” to prevent too rigid a view of the stages of the path. For the present purpose we need only take account of the arrangements that divide the path into three—notably the *Subha-sutta* (M 99), which divides the path into *sīla*, *samādhi*, and *paññā*, and the *Kassapa-sīhanāda-sutta* (D 8), which gives *sīla-sampadā*, *citta-sampadā*, and *paññā-sampadā*.

By the end of the period in which the *nikāya*s were composed, if not earlier, this three-fold structure had been applied to the stages of the *ariya* path.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-241 "note") The Buddhist path proper begins with the attainment of one of the four stages of sanctity: the stream-enterer who has seen *nibbāna* and has only seven more lives at maximum in this kind of existence; the once-returner who has further reduced greed and hatred; the never-returner who has only subtle attachment to meditational states together with a little pride, excitement, and ignorance; and the *arahat*, who has completed the Buddhist path.

In the present application, the stream-enterer has fulfilled moral conduct (the *sīla*s), the never-returner has mastered concentration (*samādhi*), while the *arahat* has mastered wisdom (*paññā*). This is only new as a specific structure. The stream-enterer is frequently seen in terms of perfecting the precepts, and thus morality or moral conduct—hence his non-rebirth in an inferior destiny such as would be the consequence of breaking the precepts. The never-returner does not return precisely because he has freed himself from attachment to the sense sphere—he is reborn in the Brahma realm. In this he is parallel to the *jhāna* attainer except that his achievement is permanent. The association of *arahat*ship with superior wisdom is, of course, obvious.

<!-- Page 96 -->

This might be better expressed by saying that all *ariya* disciples have mastered the precepts; the never-returner has mastered both moral conduct (*sīla*) and concentration (*samādhi*); and the *arahat* has mastered wisdom as well. This corresponds quite closely to the structure of the Buddhist cosmos. One is reborn as a *deva* through generosity and keeping the precepts, as a *brahma* through developing concentration, and in the Pure Abodes by developing wisdom. Quite logically, all *brahma*s are also *deva*s but not vice versa, while all those resident in the Pure Abodes are both *deva*s and *brahma*s. This may be termed the vertical structure of the path.

An alternative view becomes very important in the canonical *abhidhamma* texts. For this, the whole of the path is seen as arising together in unity at the moment of attainment. This we will call the horizontal structure. It is applied, for example, to the thirty-seven factors contributing to awakening (*bodhi-pakkhiya-dhamma*s) in relation to each of the four paths (*magga*s). On a lesser level it is applied to the five faculties (*indriya*) in relation to *jhāna*. For the Pali *abhidhamma*, all skillful states (and to some extent, unskillful ones) contain an incomplete version of the path.

Obviously these three approaches involve a measure of surface incompatibility. Any attempt to reconcile them would require a fairly sophisticated system. No doubt one could be devised. Perhaps more to the point is the type of function that is fulfilled by this sort of “over-determination.” It gives a sense of multiple dimensions to the Dhamma and a feeling of its intricate and harmonious balance. This, after all, is the teaching that is “beautiful in its beginning, beautiful in its middle, beautiful in its ending.”

## **How *Samatha* and *Vipassanā* Relate**

The main uses of the two terms *samatha* and *vipassanā* in the *nikāya* literature occur within these path structures. We find them, for example, in sequences setting out the vertical structure of the path. Some examples may make this clear. One passage runs,

> he should be one who performs fully in respect of precepts (*sīla*s), devoted to peace (*samatha*) of mind within, not having rejected *jhāna*, endowed with insight (*vipassanā*), one who increases empty houses.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-242 "note")

<!-- Page 97 -->

Here the succession is: *sīla* > *samatha* > *vipassanā* with *jhāna* apparently identified with *samatha* and “empty houses” following insight (*vipassanā*), perhaps seen as related to emptiness (*suññatā*).

Another passage refers to right view, when accompanied by five factors, as resulting in liberation of heart (*cetovimutti*) and having the advantage of resulting in liberation of heart, resulting in liberation of understanding (*paññāvimutti*) and having the advantage of resulting in liberation of understanding.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-243 "note") The five are in the sequence: moral conduct (*sīla*) > learning (*suta*) > discussion > *samatha* > *vipassanā*.

In numerous passages *samatha* and *vipassanā* are identified either with the eightfold path or with the fourth noble truth. Sometimes this is explicit. Sometimes it is intended by the use of some form of the causative of *bhavati*, “bringing into being,” or “practice” (*bhāvanā*).[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-244 "note") No doubt such a use is intended also in most of those cases in which the two occur with little explanation or context.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-245 "note") In a few passages there is some connection with either the *ariya* disciple or the transcendent (*lokuttara*) mind. Presumably this is because the stream-enterer has already mastered the precept and only has to develop concentration and wisdom.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-246 "note")

Identification of *samatha* and *vipassanā* with *samādhi* and *paññā* in the context of the horizontal structure of the path is less frequent in the major canonical *sutta* collections (*nikāya*s). In one verse passage, the five faculties (*indriya*s) are given as faith (*saddhā*), mindfulness (*sati*), strength (*viriya*), *samatha* (where one would expect concentration, *samādhi*), and *vipassanā* (where one would expect wisdom, *paññā*).[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-247 "note") Such an identification is standard in the *abhidhamma* texts where *samatha* is included in the register for *samādhi* and *vipassanā* is given for *paññā*.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-248 "note")

<!-- Page 98 -->

## **The Two Contrasted**

In a few places the two are differentiated more specifically. At AN I 61 we find that development of *samatha* leads to developing the mind or the heart (*citta*), which leads to the abandoning of desire (*rāga*) by means of liberation of heart (*cetovimutti*). Development of *vipassanā*, by contrast, leads to developing wisdom and then to the abandoning of ignorance and liberation of understanding. At AN 11 140 we learn that there is a time for hearing *dhamma*, a time for *dhamma* discussion, a time for *samatha*, and a time for *vipassanā*. If each of these is practiced from time to time, *arahat*ship will surely be reached—just as rain that falls on the mountains goes stage by stage to the ocean.

Several passages contrast the one who obtains peace of mind with the one who obtains insight into *dhamma* through higher wisdom.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-249 "note") Both are required. Whichever of the two is lacking should be developed. The individual who already obtains both should make effort (*yoga*) to obtain *arahat*ship. This last suggests that *The Designation of Human Types* (*Puggalapaññatti*) is correct in interpreting inner peace of mind as *jhāna* and *dhamma* insight through higher wisdom as the transcendent paths and fruits.

Perhaps more significant for later interpretation is Ānanda’s declaration (AN II 157):

> Sirs, whatever *bhikkhu* or *bhikkhuṇī* declares in my presence the attainment of *arahat*ship, does so in four ways or by one of the four. By which four?
>
> 1. Here, sirs, a *bhikkhu* brings into being (*bhāveti*) insight preceded by peace. As he is bringing into being insight preceded by peace, the path (*magga*) is born to him.
> 2. Again, sirs, a *bhikkhu* brings into being peace preceded by insight. As he is bringing into being peace preceded by insight, the path is born to him.
> 3. Again, sirs, a *bhikkhu* brings into being peace and insight yoked as a pair. As he is bringing into being peace and insight yoked as a pair, the path is born to him.
> 4. Again, sirs, the mind of a *bhikkhu* is gripped by *dhamma* excitement. On the occasion, sirs, when the mind stabilizes within, settles down, becomes one-pointed and enters concentration (*samādhiyati*), the path is born to him.

<!-- Page 99 -->

After each of these four alternatives we find

> he practices, brings into being, and makes much of that path. When he practices, brings into being, and makes much of that path, his fetters are abandoned, his latent tendencies are destroyed.

It is difficult to say how this discourse was originally intended, but later the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* quotes it in full and comments upon it.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-250 "note")

Significantly it is included in the *Yuganandha-vagga* of that work—the section concerned with transcendent *dhamma*s “yoked as a pair.” The *Paṭisambhidāmagga* emphasizes *ekābhisamaya*—a moment of enlightenment consisting of simultaneous realization of the four or sixteen aspects of the truths in a single moment. It lays great stress on the unity, harmony, and balance of the path at every level. Naturally, this emphasis affects its interpretation of this *sutta*, particularly regarding the third alternative.

In the first case, peace or calm (*samatha*) is explained as onepointedness of mind, non-distraction, concentration due to freedom from sensuality (*nekkhamma*). Insight, *vipassanā*, is seen as contemplating as impermanent, suffering, and without self the *dhamma*s arisen in that peaceful state. In the second way, *vipassanā* is contemplating as impermanent, suffering, and without self, while *samatha* is one-pointedness of mind, non-distraction, concentration based upon the relinquishing of the *dhamma*s arisen in that contemplation. More exactly, a state that has as its object such a relinquishing leads to concentration and peace. The *Aṅguttara* commentary explains this as the case of one who naturally obtains insight.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-251 "note") In this case, insight arises for a given individual and subsequently *jhāna* is developed and then leads to development of the path.

With the third alternative, the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* goes its own way. As the commentary points out, it concentrates upon the actual path moment itself as exemplifying the perfect unity of *samatha* and *vipassanā*. Of course, the *sutta* itself was really more concerned with the process by which that moment is reached. So the *Aṅguttara* commentary rightly interprets the third way as the case in which someone attains successive levels of calm, or *samatha* (i.e., *jhāna*), applying insight to each one before developing the next. It could also be understood as in some forms of Mahāyāna practice that seek to emulate transcendent consciousness as a means to attaining it.

<!-- Page 100 -->

The *Paṭisambhidāmagga* explanation is crucial in the fourth case—without it, the passage would not really be explicable. It explains that while paying attention to the five aggregates and so on as manifesting impermanence, etc., there arises one of ten *dhamma*s. These are then listed. This is the earliest occurrence of the list of the ten defilements of insight, which was mentioned in chapter 4 (here the order differs slightly from later versions). The list is formed from the usual materials descriptive of the path. Indeed, of the seven factors of awakening (*bojjhaṅga*s), only concentration is absent. This is not perhaps surprising, since concentration (*samādhi*) is the opposite of excitement (*uddhacca*); it is replaced by two terms of closely related usage: happiness (*sukha*) and commitment (*adhimokkha*). The two remaining terms are radiance (*obhāsa*) and *nikanti* (attachment to the prior state), first and last in the list respectively.

The point of the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* explanation is clear. States similar to those of awakening (*bodhi*) itself are reached, but become the cause of excitement. This obstructs the clarity and onward development of insight. The commentary interprets this fourth way as that of the pure insight, or “dry” insight, follower (*sukkhavipassaka*).[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-252 "note") This seems very plausible in view of the emphasis on the mind settling down and becoming concentrated that follows. What is probably originally intended is such cases as when the Buddha gives a talk and a listener’s mind is sufficiently aroused that they are able to reach some level of enlightenment thereafter.

## **Other Later Canonical and Paracanonical Works**

The inclusion of *samatha* and *vipassanā* in the *suttantika* couplets of *Buddhist Psychological Ethics* (*Dhammasaṅgaṇī*) perhaps indicates that they were already considered an important part of *suttanta* teaching. But even for the account of the different possible kinds of mental arising (*cittuppāda*) in strict *abhidhamma* terms, they are included among the basic categories of analysis alongside such things as the faculties, path factors, powers, and factors of awakening and they are presented as necessarily present in all higher states of consciousness—not only *jhāna*s but also enlightened states. For the canonical *abhidhamma* works they are presented in terms of the horizontal structure outlined above.

<!-- Page 101 -->

Yet it is perhaps only in the hermeneutic treatises (*pakaraṇanaya*s), on the methods of the paracanonical works—the older *Piṭaka Disclosure* (*Peṭakopadesa*) and the influential *The Guide* (*Nettipakaraṇa*)—that we find them playing a really major role.

In these works the path is considered from the standpoint of various methods (*naya*s). The first of these, the “auspicious turning to the right” (*nandiyāvatta*) method, views the way as composed of *samatha* and *vipassanā*, overcoming craving and ignorance respectively.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-253 "note") This is then the basis for an intricate set of relationships, potentially involving almost everything in Buddhist teaching that can be expressed in pairs. Indeed by this method almost anything in Buddhist teaching could be expressed as a pair! Some examples may be of interest.

*Samatha* is explained as the medicine for craving, bringing freedom from sickness by liberation of heart, while *vipassanā* is the medicine for the sickness of ignorance, bringing freedom from sickness by liberation of understanding.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-254 "note") One bringing into being *samatha* comprehends matter, abandons craving, and realizes liberation of the heart. One bringing into being *vipassanā* comprehends the immaterial, abandons ignorance, and realizes liberation of understanding. Here the functions associated with each of the four noble truths are being utilized: comprehending for the first, abandoning for the second, realizing for the third, and bringing into being for the fourth.

Or those whose character type is prone to views (*diṭṭhicarita*), due to the hindrance of ignorance, may incline to the extreme of practicing self-mortification or tend to the annihilationist view. One whose character type is prone to craving may incline to the extreme of practicing sensual enjoyment or tend to the eternalist view.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-255 "note") The former will bring into being *samatha* preceded by *vipassanā*, abandon ignorance, and attain liberation of understanding. The latter will bring into being *vipassanā* preceded by *samatha*, abandon craving and attain liberation of heart.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-256 "note") Many other such classifications and groupings are offered in these very detailed works.[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-257 "note")

<!-- Page 102 -->

## **The Commentarial Literature**

The terms *samatha* and *vipassanā* occur quite frequently in the commentarial (*aṭṭhakathā*) literature.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-258 "note") We will confine ourselves here to usages closely related to descriptions of the stages of the path.

An especially important passage is in Buddhaghosa’s commentary to the *Middle Length Discourses* (*Majjhima-nikāya*).[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-259 "note") This is introduced in a way that suggests it has been taken as a whole from earlier sources, almost certainly the old commentaries of the Mahāvihāra. No doubt this is true of the bulk of Buddhaghosa’s writings, but it is only in such cases as this that we can be fairly sure that we are dealing with an earlier stratum unmixed with later material. The subject of this passage is given as “the method of bringing into being” (*bhāvanā-naya*). It distinguishes just two alternatives: insight preceded by calm and calm preceded by insight. For the first we have,

> In regard to this someone, first arouses approach concentration or absorption concentration.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-260 "note") This is peace. He brings into being insight into that and into its conjoined states, which sees them as impermanent and so on. This is insight. So peace is first, afterward insight. Therefore it is referred to as bringing into being insight preceded by peace. As he is bringing into being insight preceded by peace, the path (*magga*) is born to him. He practices, brings into being, and makes much of that path. When he practices, brings into being, and makes much of that path, his fetters are abandoned, his latent tendencies are destroyed—in this way he brings into being insight preceded by peace.

Here *samatha* is quite explicitly explained as approach or absorption concentration. For the alternative case of calm preceded by insight, however, the calm is defined as “one-pointedness based upon the relinquishing of the *dhamma*s arisen in that contemplation”—that is, insight into impermanence, etc. is let go of and the mind settles into one-pointedness. In this second case there is no reference to approach or absorption concentration. Rather, concentration that arises after strong insight is seen as something separate. The passage concludes,

<!-- Page 103 -->

> But both for one who brings into being insight preceded by calm and for one who brings into being calm preceded by insight, at the moment of the transcendent path calm and insight are yoked as a pair.

One *sutta* in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* (II 155–56) distinguishes the person who reaches the final goal with effort (*saṅkhāraparinibbāyī*) from one who does so with ease (*asaṅkhāra*). Only the latter attains to the four *jhāna*s. Buddhaghosa’s commentary explains that the first individual is one who develops just insight (*sukkhavipassaka*).[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-261 "note") The person who reaches the goal with ease is explained as one whose vehicle is peace (*samatha-yānika*).[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-262 "note")

The term *yāna*—“vehicle” or “carriage”—generally refers to the eightfold path.[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-263 "note") This is further developed in the *Vibhaṅga Commentary* where the vehicle of insight (*vipassanā-yāna*) is equated with knowledge (*vijjā*) and the first two items of the eightfold path.[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-264 "note") The vehicle of calm is identified with conduct (*caraṇa*) and the remaining six factors of the path. The source is another *sutta* from the “The Section on Moral Conduct” (*Sīlakkhandha*), the *Ambaṭṭha-sutta* (D 3), which gives a description of the path in terms of the well-known phrase “endowed with knowledge and conduct.” According to Buddhaghosa, following *The Guide* (*Nettipakaraṇa*), the vehicle of calm overcomes the extreme of practicing self-mortification, while the vehicle of insight overcomes the extreme of practicing sensual enjoyment.[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-265 "note")

The precise position of Buddhaghosa is made clear in the *Visuddhimagga*, when the way to initiate the development of insight is described. One whose vehicle is calm begins by examining the contents of one’s mind after emerging from *jhāna*, especially the *jhāna* factors themselves. Having established that these are mind (*nāma*), one seeks the underlying support of mind—its “lair”—and finds it in the physical heart base (*rūpa*).[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-266 "note") This the meditator discovers to be supported by the four elements and the material phenomena derived from them. One establishes that they are form, *rūpa* (defined as “that which is afflicted” or capable of being damaged by contact with other *rūpa*).[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-267 "note")

<!-- Page 104 -->

Once the precise nature of mental and material phenomena is established, one is able to see that there is no entity or person or deity apart from that. In other words, one understands the no-self teaching and thereby becomes established in right view that sees things as they are. One thus avoids the two extremes of either affirming a soul not subject to destruction or affirming one subject to destruction, thereby falling into eternalism or annihilationism. This point is reached in a different way by one whose vehicle is purely insight (*suddha-vipassanā*). That one must commence with form or matter (*rūpa*). This is also possible as an alternative option for one whose vehicle is calm.

Two other passages from Buddhaghosa’s commentaries seem worth mentioning. Discussing the situation of someone with the delusion that they have reached some attainment, it is suggested that one who has purely obtained peace or purely obtained insight would believe that they were a stream-enterer, once-returner, or never-returner. One who had obtained both peace and insight would believe they were an *arahat*.[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-268 "note") Another passage discusses the difference between painful progress and pleasant progress (in suppressing the hindrances) as concerned with the degree of craving and whether the necessary work has been done for *samatha*. The subsequent difference between slow and rapid acquiring of direct knowledge is concerned with the strength of ignorance and whether the necessary work has been done for *vipassanā*.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-269 "note")

## **The Transcendent**

I have so far not mentioned the seventh purification, the experiential realization of fundamental doctrine—the four noble truths. At the moment when the path of stream-entry (or one of the higher paths) is aroused, a number of things are accomplished: defilements are abandoned (some temporarily, others permanently), conditioned things are relinquished as objects supporting the mind, *nibbāna* becomes the object of consciousness, the thirty-seven states connected with awakening become fixed (*vāsanā*) in the personal continuity, and the Four Noble Truths are comprehended by a single knowledge. All of this amounts in a sense to the last of these: “By making cessation its object, path knowledge reaches, sees, and pierces the Four Noble Truths.”[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-270 "note")

<!-- Page 105 -->

This is a highly sophisticated understanding of the four truths: with respect to the first truth one knows, “*dukkha* is the conditioned,” the second, “arising is the cause of the conditioned,” the third, “cessation is the unconditioned,” and the fourth, “path is the means to the unconditioned.” Each of these knowledges performs a particular function. The first truth is to be comprehended, the second abandoned, the third realized, and the fourth developed. According to Buddhaghosa, a single path of knowledge accomplishes all these four functions in a single moment. “Just as a lamp performs four functions simultaneously in a single moment—it burns the wick, dispels darkness, makes light appear, and uses up the oil.” This is quite a startling statement in *abhidhamma* terms and ought really to imply that the conditioned and the unconditioned are the same thing, although Buddhaghosa does not draw this conclusion. What it certainly does indicate is a state in which a knowledge arises that is permanently impressed in the mind and that involves an awareness of the nature of the phenomenal world, abandonment of mental defilements, direct experience of the unconditioned, and the structuring of consciousness in terms of the factors of the path and of awakening, and the like. This is potentially accessible at all times.

Compare this with the stream-enterer, who is particularly characterized by the absence of the three fetters of *sakkāyadiṭṭhi* (often translated as “personality belief”), of adherence to *sīla* and vows as a means to liberation, and of doubt. Elaborated, this means that the view of self is removed, attachment to the body is given up, ritualism is dropped, and certainty arising from seeing *nibbāna* is always present. Equally defilements in general are reduced and wrong actions of the more serious kinds will no longer occur.

## **A Corkscrew Analogy**

We can say that the goal of Buddhism is *bodhi*—awakening or enlightenment. But that awakening can be described as the perfect union of calm and insight—a union in which the two are coupled together in a harmonious balance that can never be wholly lost. From this point of view these two terms describe the horizontal structure of the path at its culmination. For Theravāda, only such a mental state can fully develop the mind and enable it to experience the unconditioned truth that is *nibbāna*.

<!-- Page 106 -->

From the point of view of the vertical structure of the path, *samatha* comes first and *vipassanā* comes afterward. So all forms of Buddhist practice begin with *samatha*, whether they call themselves *samatha* or *vipasssanā*, and equally all forms of Buddhist practice lead to the development of *vipassanā*, whether they call themselves *samatha* or *vipassanā*.

Not surprisingly, then, modern usages of these terms as applied to meditation vary considerably, as we shall see in the final chapter of this book. By way of conclusion I offer an approximate model of how all this relates. I call this “corkscrew Buddhism.”

The Buddhist path is not a ladder, whether we are speaking of the eightfold path or the simpler model of calm and insight. In ascending a ladder we go step by step, leaving the lower steps behind. This is not the case with the Buddhist way of bringing into being. If we start from the handle of an old-fashioned corkscrew, we can see this as a figure for the precepts and basic training. The corkscrew winds upward, as the path continually moves between calm and insight. This combines both the vertical structure of the path and the horizontal structure of the path. At the last it comes to a single point, centered in the middle. This corresponds to the union of calm and insight. And I may add that in order to use it, we have to turn it upside down. We might call this a kind of Zen Theravāda.

# 6. The *Sutta* on Mindfulness with In-and-Out Breathing

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

The oldest texts refer to a number of subjects of meditation.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-271 "note") In this chapter we turn to examine one specific method of meditation: meditation on the breath and one particular source for this method, the canonical *Ānāpānasati-sutta*, or “Discourse on Mindfulness with In-and-Out Breathing” (M 118).[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-272 "note") Within that discourse we find set out a sequence of sixteen stages of development in four tetrads. The sixteen stages are not only found here. Within the Pali Canon we find them included in another *Majjhima-nikāya* discourse, in a discourse in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*, and in the twenty discourses collected into the “Connected Discourses on Breathing” (*Ānāpānasaṃyutta*).[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-273 "note") A detailed commentary on them is included in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga.* They are also given in a story included in “The Great Section” (*Mahāvagga*) of the *Vinayapiṭaka*. They are similarly preserved in many sources available in Chinese and a few surviving Sanskrit versions.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-274 "note") The first four stages are also found in versions of the “Discourse on Breathing Mindfulness” section of the *Satipaṭṭhāna-sutta* (M 10), but it is likely that in that context they have been extracted from the earlier set of sixteen.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-275 "note") The final twelve stages would not fit comfortably into the analysis given in the *Satipaṭṭhāna-sutta*.

<!-- Page 107 -->

Florin Deleanu comments: “The sixteen bases represent a very old technique dating back, most probably, to the earliest days of Buddhism.”[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-276 "note") This seems very likely, as they show every sign of being an oral mnemonic composed in the early stages of teaching the method. There are some variations in the non-Pali sources. These will be referred to later, but the general pattern is rather consistent.

The sixteen stages are invariably preceded by a formula, specifying how and where one should prepare to meditate:

> In this teaching, monks [or other addressee], a monk goes to the forest or the root of a tree or an empty house and sits. He adopts a cross-legged posture, setting his body erect and establishing mindfulness in front.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-277 "note")

The details of the position of the legs are not specified in any canonical source, although we may suspect that something like the well-known lotus posture is intended.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-278 "note") Two late canonical texts do, however, specify what is meant by establishing mindfulness “in front” or “around the face.” According to them, it means “on the nose-tip” or “on the appearance of the face.”[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-279 "note") This suggests that breathing mindfulness is specifically envisaged in this formula, and indeed in the canonical literature it seems to occur only before the sixteen stages, or before the overcoming of the five hindrances, or before the formulas for the four *jhāna*s.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-280 "note") That would seem to imply that the normal method envisaged for developing the *jhāna*s is breathing mindfulness. In line with this is the emphasis in Buddhist tradition on the central role of breathing mindfulness in the awakening of all Buddhas.

Immediately prior to enumerating the sixteen stages, the *sutta* texts all have this sentence:

> **Mindful** he breathes in. Mindful he breathes out.

The first occurrence of the word translated as “mindful” is emphasized with the particle *va*; so I have put it in bold in this translation.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-281 "note") This sentence is erroneously interpreted by some as an initial stage in which one is not aware of or does not pay attention to the length of the breath. That seems to be a misunderstanding of the Pali, which gives a general description of breathing mindfulness and then lists the sixteen ways in which it is developed. This is also clear from the way in which breathing mindfulness is referred to in many later sources as sixteenfold.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-282 "note") It is probably simply a transmission error when this is listed at the start as if it is a part of the stages, as in a few non-Pali sources.

<!-- Page 108 -->

Significantly, there does not appear to be anywhere in the canonical literature where meditation on the normal breath is recommended. Rather, it should always be made long or short.

## **The First Tetrad**

The first four stages are

> 1. Either while breathing in long one understands, “I am breathing in long.” Or while breathing out long one understands, “I am breathing out long.”
> 2. Either while breathing in short one understands, “I am breathing in short.” Or while breathing out short one understands, “I am breathing out short.”
> 3. One trains with the idea, “I will breathe in experiencing the whole body.” One trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing the whole body.”
> 4. One trains with the idea, “I will breathe in tranquilizing the bodily activator.” One trains with the idea, “I will breathe out tranquilizing the bodily activator.”

There is some uncertainty in the tradition as to which word means “breathe in” and which means “breathe out,” but since the formula specifies that either breathing in or breathing out is acceptable as a starting point, this has little importance. Some sources influenced by the Sarvāstivādin *abhidharma* tradition reverse the order of the first two stages so that a short breath precedes a long breath. (See below.) But all known *sūtra* and *vinaya* texts preserved in Sanskrit and Chinese seem to retain the order as first short then long as in Pali.

That a deliberate process of making the breath long or short is envisaged is clear from the simile that compares the first two stages to a wood-turner’s knowing when he makes a long cut and knowing when he makes a short cut.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-283 "note")

In the version given above, we have “understands” for the first two stages and then “trains” for the next two (as well as for the following stages). This is the normal practice in the Pali texts. However, when the case concerns the post-awakening practice of the Buddha, the notion of “training” does not sit well. So we find that here “understands” is used throughout.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-284 "note") Not surprisingly in the texts influenced by Sarvāstivādin *abhidharma* this is used for all the stages, since *abhidharma* aims to give a universally applicable description (*nippariyāyadesanā*). Uniquely, the Chinese translation of *The Connected Discourses* (*Saṃyuktāgama*) employs “trains” with all sixteen stages, but this is probably an error arising from an overly abbreviated manuscript.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-285 "note")

<!-- Page 109 -->

No further information is given as to how to practice the first two stages, but one may suspect that from an early date some kind of counting was employed in the initial two stages.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-286 "note") Although counting the breath is not mentioned in the canonical literature, it is widespread in Buddhist traditions in the early centuries c.e. as part of a complementary list of four, six, or eight stages of practice, beginning always with counting. Buddhaghosa refers to the list of eight as the “ways of bringing to mind” (Vism 278–87/*The Path of Purification*, VIII 189). Deleanu suggests that lists of this type may go back to the second century b.c.e., and sees it as an auxiliary exercise “devised by the Abhidharma masters.”[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-287 "note") But there is nothing particularly *abhidharm*ic about it, and it may equally have been part of a much older oral tradition of teaching.

### ***The Complementary List According to Buddhaghosa***

1. Counting
2. Following
3. Touching
4. Fixing
5. Investigating the characteristics
6. Turning away
7. Purity
8. Looking back on these [seven].

The third of the sixteen stages is given in the Pali sources as “experiencing the whole body.” This must originally have been meant very simply and literally. As meditation on the breath continues, the meditator becomes aware of more subtle effects of the breath in a wider and wider area of the body. There is, of course, no concept of any kind of “gas” or “oxygen” in ancient India; what is meant by “breath” is a force that moves different parts of the body and generates various contact sensations. In essence, then, “experiencing the whole body” in the sixteen stages is the same thing as the second stage in the Buddhagosa’s complementary list referred to above, following.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-288 "note")

Buddhaghosa interprets this as referring to the breath body and explains it as making known (that is, fully clear) the beginning, middle, and end of each breath. He explains that for some meditators, only one of these three may be initially seen clearly; this has to be rectified so that all three are clear. Later, however, when discussing the following stage of the complementary list, he suggests that the beginning, middle, and end should not be followed in and out of the body, or to and from the navel, heart, and nose-tip. He cites a passage from the *Paṭisambhidāmagga*:

<!-- Page 110 -->

> Body and mind are disquieted and disturbed and troubled when one follows with mindfulness the beginning, middle, and end of the in breath with a mind that is distracted within. Body and mind are disquieted and disturbed and troubled when one follows with mindfulness the beginning, middle, and end of the out breath with a mind that is distracted without.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-289 "note")

It seems clear from the context, however, that the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* intends to warn against letting the mind be distracted within or without, not against following the breath as such. It is treated alongside other examples of letting the mind be distracted from the immediate object, such as desiring the out breath while being mindful of the in breath and so on.

We may compare the description of the second item of the complementary list in *\*The Treasury of Abhidharma* (*Abhidharmakośa*):

> What is called “following” follows the movement of the in breaths and out breaths without controlling [where they go] so as to know how far they go in or out and whether they pervade the whole body or occur in a single place. One follows them as they go in sequentially entering the neck, heart, navel, loins, thighs, lower legs as far as the feet. [One follows them] as they go out the distance of a span or a fathom. Others say that [one follows them] as far as the circle of winds and the Vairambha winds. That is not appropriate because it would be bringing to mind an element [that is, wind element and not breathing mindfulness].[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-290 "note")

The final stage in the first tetrad of breathing mindfulness refers to tranquilizing bodily activities or constructions (*kāyasaṅkhāra*s). This can be interpreted in two ways. *Saṅkhāra* can just mean “activity,” in which case this fourth stage simply refers to a gradual process of stilling all bodily activity or movement. The simile of the gong given in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* could be applied to this:

> Just as when a gong has been struck, first of all gross sounds occur. Because the appearance of the gross sounds has been well grasped, well brought to mind and well learned, even when the gross sound has ceased, still afterward subtle sounds occur. Because the appearance of the subtle sounds has been well grasped, well brought to mind and well learned, even when the subtle sound has ceased, still afterward the mind occurs because it has the appearance of the subtle sounds as its object. In the very same way first of all gross in-and-out breaths occur. Because the appearance of the gross in and out breaths has been well grasped, well brought to mind and well learned, even when the gross in and out breaths have ceased, still afterward subtle in and out breaths occur. Because the appearance of the subtle in and out breaths has been well grasped, well brought to mind and well learned, even when the subtle in and out breaths have ceased, still afterward the mind does not become distracted because it has the appearance (*nimitta*) of the subtle in and out breaths as its object.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-291 "note")

<!-- Page 111 -->

This simile describes the subtler and subtler appearance of the breath as breathing mindfulness is practiced and could easily have originally been applied to tranquilizing *kāyasaṅkhāra* in the sense of stilling all bodily activity. However, this is not how the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* understands it. Instead, it understands *saṅkhāra* in the sense of “activator” or “constructor.” So stilling the *kāyasaṅkhāra* means stilling what enables the body to act. It refers here to the first item in a list of three kinds of *saṅkhāra* in which the bodily activator is explained as in breaths and out breaths, the verbal activator is thinking and exploring, and the mental activator is feeling and labeling—the point being that breathing enables the body to function; when there is no breathing the body cannot perform any action. Thinking and exploring in their technical sense necessarily precede any kind of deliberate verbal speech. The two aggregates of feeling and labeling underlie the mental activities that continue to take place in the *jhāna*s and formless attainments. The special attainment that lies beyond the formless attainments is referred to as the cessation of labeling and feeling (*vedayita*).

This notion of the three activators is found in two canonical discourses.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-292 "note") In addition, the expression “with the bodily activator tranquilized” occurs in the list of the ten “noble livings” (*ariyavāsa*s) applied to the fourth *jhāna*.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-293 "note") It is also found in one other discourse.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-294 "note") Here it refers to the stopping of breathing in the fourth *jhāna*.

In terms of the complementary lists, the northern sources generally seem to have had “fixing” (*sthapanā*) as the third and final item in the *śamatha* portion of the list. Given the close relation of forms of the root STHĀ to definitions of *samādhi*, this seems to naturally correspond to the fourth stage in the sixteenfold list. \**The Treasury of Abhidharma* (*Abhidharmakośa*) indicates that “fixing” is when one sees [the in and out breaths] as fixed (*sthitān*) at the nose-tip [or at the other locations], as far as at the big toe, like a thread within a gem.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-295 "note") The *Vimuttimagga* and the *Visuddhimagga*, however, divide this stage into two: touching (*phusanā*) and “fixing” or “settling” (*ṭhapanā*). The motivation behind this seems to be a wish to emphasize the importance of the location where the contact of the breath is felt, either at the nose-tip or on the upper lip—at the nose-tip for those with large noses and on the upper lip for those with small noses.[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-296 "note")

<!-- Page 112 -->

### ***Abhidharma Interpretation***

In some Sarvāstivādin *abhidharma* circles, an attempt seems to have been made to formulate the sixteen stages in more precise *abhidharma* terms. The starting point for this was probably the fourth stage. As we have it in the list, this simply refers to stilling the bodily activities, understood as in-and-out breathing. We may suppose that all of the first four stages continue as the practice progresses, and certainly in the case of the fourth, the process of tranquilization continues as the remaining twelve stages are developed. But the followers of the *abhidharma*, the *ābhidharmika*s, seem to have relied on the *suttanta* claim that in the fourth *jhāna* breathing was fully tranquilized to argue that in the highest sense—that is, in *abhidharma* terms—the fourth stage refers specifically to the fourth *jhāna*.

What naturally followed from this was an identification of the preceding three stages with the three previous *jhāna*s. The third stage, “experiencing the whole body,” might naturally seem to link with the third *jhāna*, given that that is described as drenching, flooding, filling, and suffusing “this very body” with happiness undisturbed by joy. But there was rather more of a problem with linking the first two stages to the first two *jhāna*s. It was felt that long breathing is associated with a more peaceful state and short breathing with less peaceful states. So it was hard for them to envisage long breathing as connected to the first *jhāna* and short breathing as belonging to the second *jhāna*, always envisaged as more subtle than the first. There was perhaps some confusion here between two different situations. While engaged in strenuous activity, the breath is naturally shorter. When resting after such activity, the breath can become long and slow, even rather peaceful. The *ābhidharmika*s took this as their model. However, in the meditative context, when the breath becomes more subtle, it tends to be shorter, even very short, almost imperceptible.[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-297 "note")

The solution adopted was to reverse the order of the first two stages in the sixteenfold list and have short breathing precede long. This sequence is found in Vaibhāṣika works, notably the Chinese translations of *\*The Great Explanation* (*Mahāvibhāṣā*) and Sa*ṅ*ghabhadra’s \**Conformity with Correct Principle* (*Nyāyānusāra*). However, the usual order seems to have been well established and the change seems to have met resistance. Three texts translated or composed by Kumārajīva and others at the beginning of the fourth century c.e. resolve the issue by combining the two stages into a single stage of “observing breathing as long or short.” It is perhaps worth noting that something similar is done by Buddhaghosa in the *Visuddhimagga*, with this verse after the second stage:

<!-- Page 113 -->

> Long and short, in breath and also out breath four kinds occur at the nose-tip for a bhikkhu.[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-298 "note")

It seems likely that this is a quotation; so possibly something similar was known also to Kumārajīva.

But most later sources retain the order of long followed by short. These include almost all non-Sarvāstivādin sources, including \**The Stage of the Disciple* (*Śrāvakabhūmi*). Given that the materials preserved in Chinese represent a strong influence from Sarvāstivādin sources and other schools of the northwest of the Indian subcontinent and preserve relatively little of such major schools as the Sāmitīyas, Mahīśāsakas, and the different varieties of Mahāsa*ṃ*ghika, this is striking.

Not surprisingly, the identification of the first four stages with the first four *jhāna*s has also not been widely accepted. Buddhaghosa sees the first tetrad as the explanation for the beginner (*ādikammika*). The following three tetrads are seen as setting out contemplation of feeling, mind, and *dhamma* for one who has attained *jhāna*.[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-299 "note") This follows the link between the four establishings of mindfulness and the four tetrads that is already made in the canonical sources.[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-300 "note") This understanding is shared by most non-Pali sources also.

We should note that in this kind of understanding the sixteen stages are more like modes. They do not constitute a simple sequence in which one progresses. The shift from an original description of successive stages to a more complex interpretation is probably associated with the rise of *abhidhamma/abhidharma*.

## **The Second Tetrad**

The second set of four stages is

> 1. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in experiencing joy.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing joy.”
> 2. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in experiencing happiness.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing happiness.”
> 3. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in experiencing the mental activator(s).” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing the mental activator(s).”
> 4. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in tranquilizing the mental activator(s)” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out tranquilizing the mental activator(s).”[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-301 "note")

<!-- Page 114 -->

There can be very little doubt that in the *sutta* sources the first two stages in this tetrad are intended to refer to the *jhāna*s. What was probably originally meant by the first one is either the first two *jhāna*s or more probably just the second *jhāna*. However, the *sutta*s do not distinguish between approach and absorption concentration. So later sources had to reinterpret slightly. They understand that what is meant here is the experience of joy in the near *jhāna*, absorption, and post-*jhāna* states. This includes the development of insight on the basis of the *jhāna* factors. So this and the subsequent stages in this and the following tetrad are seen as developing both calm and insight.

Such an analysis is already found in the *Vimuttimagga*, which relates the first three stages in this tetrad to the second, third, and fourth *jhāna*s, but also points to the experiencing of joy in the first two *jhāna*s, of happiness in the first three *jhāna*s, and of the mental activator in all four *jhāna*s.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-302 "note") A similar position to the latter is found in Vaibhāṣika texts and in \**The Stage of the Disciple* (*Śrāvakabhūmi*), although they exclude the fourth *jhāna* on the grounds that breathing has stopped there, hence there can be no practice of mindfulness of in-and-out breathing at that time. For the Pali sources, however, the practice of breathing mindfulness continues in the fourth *jhāna*, taking the *nimitta* produced by the breath as its object.

With the third stage, “experiencing the mental activator(s),” the same issue arises as with the third stage in the first tetrad—what is meant by *saṅkhāra* here? But in this case it does not seem possible to take it in a general sense as “experiencing mental activity” because it would be difficult to distinguish that from the first stage in the third tetrad, “experiencing the mind.” So we have to understand mental activator(s) here as referring to feeling and labeling. This fits very well with the classification of this tetrad as concerned with mindfulness of feeling. This interpretation is followed by the Pali sources and also by Harivarman and Sa*ṅ*ghabhadra.[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-303 "note") This stage must originally in the *sutta* context have referred to the fourth *jhāna*, which is essentially a state in which feeling has been mastered and the mind can remain in balance with neutral feeling.

<!-- Page 115 -->

It follows that the fourth stage is moving toward formless meditation in some way. Just as the form *jhāna*s concern making feeling subtle, so the formless attainments concern making labeling (*saññā*) subtle. Probably what was meant here originally was the sphere of boundless space. However, “stilling the mental activator” in the fullest sense could only refer to the attainment of cessation. This is spelled out in the commentary to *The Discourse Giving the Analysis of Topics* (*Arthaviniścayasūtra*).[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-304 "note") Probably for this reason some sources understand *cittasaṃskāra* as referring to labeling and will (*cetanā*).[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-305 "note") The meditation manual found in Central Asia understands the difference between “experiencing the mental activator(s)” and “stilling the mental activator(s)” as concerning an absence of purposeful mental activity in the latter.[[36]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-306 "note") This seems to reflect a similar understanding.

## **The Third Tetrad**

The third set of four stages is

> 1. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in experiencing mind. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing mind.”
> 2. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in gladdening the mind.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out experiencing gladdening the mind.”
> 3. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in concentrating the mind.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out concentrating the mind.”
> 4. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in freeing the mind.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out freeing the mind.”

Following the same logic as before, the original *suttanta* understanding must be that the first stage refers to the sphere of boundless consciousness. If so, the second stage should relate to the sphere where one has nothing, or where nothing is owned. Probably the rationale here is the necessity of arousing a positive state of mind before adopting this sphere as the object of attention. The third stage would then correspond to the last formless attainment, “neither labeling nor absence of labeling.” This is the deepest level of formless concentration. The fourth could then be some kind of turn toward insight meditation.

<!-- Page 116 -->

No later source seems to interpret the third tetrad in this way, however. That may partly be because formless meditation was less practiced in the later period. What we do find is the kind of *abhidhamma*-based analysis we saw in the previous tetrad. So “experiencing mind” is understood by the *Visuddhimagga* in terms of experiencing mind in the four *jhāna*s—that is, on recollecting the *jhāna*s. “Gladdening the mind” is explained as gladdening the mind either at the moment of entering one of the first two *jhāna*s due to the associated joy or at the moment of insight arising after emerging from that *jhāna* and examining the way in which that associated joy is subject to destruction. Similarly, “concentrating the mind” is the act of properly setting the mind on the object in one of the *jhāna*s. Or it is the momentary one-pointedness of mind that arises through penetrating (one of) the three signs, after emerging from that *jhāna* and examining the way in which the mind that is associated with *jhāna* is subject to destruction. Finally, “freeing the mind” is the successive freeing of the mind from the hindrances and various *jhāna* factors as one progresses through the *jhāna*s. Or it is the freeing of the mind that arises at the moment of insight when a specific insight knowledge arises and frees the mind from the corresponding mental distortion—for example, contemplation of impermanence releases the mind from the notion of permanence and so on.

By contrast, in Harivarman’s \**The True Attainment Treatise* (*Satyasiddhiśāstra*) and \**The Stage of the Disciple* (*Śrāvakabhūmi*) we see explanations that are not based upon *abhidharma* analysis. They interpret “gladdening” and “concentrating” as overcoming sluggishness and excitement respectively. For the former, “freeing the mind” concerns establishing equanimity, while the *Śrāvakabhūmi* simply sees it as being purged from the hindrances. For the first stage, the *Śrāvakabhūmi* simply interprets it in terms of basic mindfulness of *citta*, while Harivarman refers to overcoming the savoring of feeling.

Such explanations follow naturally from the wording, but they do not seem to fit well with the overall structure of the sixteen stages. See [Table 9](#tbl9).

<!-- Page 117 -->

### **Table 9: Mindfulness through In-and-Out Breathing**

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | Body | Feelings | Mind |
| 1 | long breath | joy | mind |
| 2 | short breath | happiness | gladdening mind |
| 3 | whole body | mental activators | concentrating mind |
| 4 | stilling bodily activators | stilling mental activators | freeing mind |

Taking just the first twelve stages, there seems to be a significant parallelism. The link between the tetrads and the four establishings of mindfulness is almost universal and certainly ancient. The first stage in each set of four sets up contact with the area in question. Long breaths are a particularly visible and noticeable form of bodily manifestation. Joy is a relatively gross form of energization that suffuses the body with pleasant feeling. Compare the simile for the third *jhāna* of lotuses suffused from top to bottom with cool water. Experiencing mind is making contact with mind in its pure form as “boundless consciousness.” Short breaths are a somewhat more subtle form of bodily manifestation, as the happiness of the third *jhāna* is a very subtle form of pleasant feeling. The parallel is less clear in the case of mind, but it is still connected with a move to something mental that is more subtle.

With the third stage in each tetrad the shift is again to something more subtle. With the body we have a kind of whole-body awareness of the breath throughout the body. With feelings the focus is now on experiencing the most subtle kind of feeling, one that is very quiet and pervasive. The image is that of a person whose whole body, including the head, is covered by a white cloth. With the mind, concentration is a process of centering and unifying. That culminates in the transcending of the polarities involved in “neither labeling nor absence of labeling.”

The fourth stage in each tetrad is a process of gradual stilling so as to let go of that kind of experience and open out to something new. In the first case, the breathing becomes ever more subtle so that awareness of the body becomes ever more marginal. In the second case, the various feelings and the labeling activity that concerns itself with multiplicity are gradually discarded in the experience of boundless space. In the third case, the activity of the mind is being minimized so as to let go of all deliberate or purposive activity.

<!-- Page 118 -->

What is probably meant here is something like the concentration of mind that is signless (*animittacetosamādhi*) that is developed after the formless attainments.[[37]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-307 "note") In effect the fourth stage in each tetrad is transitional. On the one hand the first *jhāna* in mindfulness with in-and-out breathing represents the culmination of mindfulness of the body—it is fully aware of and lets go of the sensory world, but is still near to speech and vocalization. Sound is its thorn. On the other hand, it arouses a new kind of joy and happiness that brings it toward the realm of mindfulness of feeling. Similarly, the first formless attainment can be seen as the culmination of mindfulness of feeling —it retains the same feeling and object as the fourth *jhāna* but opens out toward the realm of subtle consciousness. The transition between the third and fourth tetrads is not just from mindfulness of the mind to another object, but rather to a mindfulness of *dhamma* and *dhamma*s that encompasses body, feeling, and mind together. At this point the turn to let go of deliberate directing of the mind on the one hand takes the practice to an even deeper level of mindfulness of *citta*, and on the other hand opens it to an awareness of whatever arises, which prepares the way for insight.

## **The Fourth Tetrad**

The third set of four stages in the Pali sources is

> 1. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in contemplating impermanence.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out contemplating impermanence.”
> 2. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in contemplating dispassion.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out contemplating dispassion.”
> 3. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in contemplating cessation.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out contemplating cessation.”
> 4. He trains with the idea, “I will breathe in contemplating relinquishing.” He trains with the idea, “I will breathe out contemplating relinquishing.”[[38]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-308 "note")

This is also the sequence in *\*The Treatise on the Abhidharma of Śāriputra* (*Śāriputrābhidharmaśāstra*) and *The Discourse Giving the Analysis of Topics* (*Arthaviniścayasūtra*),[[39]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-309 "note") but most other non-Meditations Pali sources follow the Sarvāstivādin tradition and insert “contemplating abandoning” (*prahāṇānudarśin*) as the second stage and omit the final stage, thus retaining a set of four. The exception is a group of texts translated by Kumārajīva that seem to adopt both alternatives in a set of five.[[40]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-310 "note")

<!-- Page 119 -->

The terms used here are part of often longer sets of terms that occur in a number of different ways in the canonical texts, both in Pali and in Sanskrit sources. It is often unclear whether they refer to different forms of insight experience or are simply near synonyms. As here, however, such sequences almost always begin with impermanence and so clearly concern insight or wisdom in some form. Relinquishing (*paṭinissagga*) is usually last, either because it is intended to refer to the final relinquishing of all attachment (*upadhi*) by the *arahat* or because of the rule of “waxing syllables” in oral literature—or both of these.

We may then suppose that an earlier *suttanta* explanation may have been to equate the contemplation of dispassion with stream-entry. The Pali commentaries preserve a tradition of identifying this contemplation with the path of vision (*dassanamagga*).[[41]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-311 "note") One might then identify the third stage with the path of bringing into being (*bhāvanāmagga*).

Later interpreters offer more sophisticated accounts. For the *Visuddhimagga* and the *Vimuttimagga*, this fourth tetrad concerns only insight. This ought to mean that they do not understand the final stage as completely fulfilling the path. But Buddhaghosa explicitly states that the final three stages can be understood both as insight and as the experience of enlightenment in the path (*magga*). Buddhaghosa would presumably agree that in the latter case, calm and insight are both involved. The commentators have to offer an interpretation suitable for someone like the Buddha because of the account in a discourse found in the *Saṃyutta-nikāya* and a corresponding Chinese version[[42]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-312 "note") in which the Buddha spends three months practicing the sixteen stages of breathing mindfulness and recommends it both for the trainee (*sekha*) and also for the *arahat* “for happy abiding in the here and now and for mindfulness and clear comprehension.”[[43]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-313 "note")

Mahānāma’s commentary on the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* offers an interpretation of the four in terms of insight alone. The first stage is simply contemplation of impermanence. The second is understood as contemplation of disenchantment (*nibbidā*), while the third is explained as contemplation of breaking up (*bhaṅga*)—that is, strong insight. The fourth is interpreted as insight leading to emergence, or equanimity about activities or constructions (*saṅkhāra*s) and conformity knowledge, the stages preceding membership (*gotrabhū*) and the path.[[44]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-314 "note") Mahānāma also gives the same explanations as Buddhaghosa elsewhere; he understands the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* context as referring specifically to developing insight.

<!-- Page 120 -->

## **Abhidharma Interpretation**

The notion of the three elements, with the element of abandoning, the element of dispassion, and the element of cessation, seems to derive from a Sarvāstivādin *sūtra* source.[[45]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-315 "note") There they are applied to permanent liberation and defined as abandoning passion, abandoning other defilements, and abandoning the basis for clinging (*sopādāna vastu*).[[46]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-316 "note") They are considered identical to the unconstructed (*asaṃskṛta*) element. *\*The Century of Noble Deeds* (*Avadānaśataka*) defines the three as the abandoning of, dispassion toward, and cessation of all constructions (*saṃskāra*s).[[47]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-317 "note")

A number of alternative explanations of the last four stages are given in \**The Great Explanation* (*Mahāvibhāṣā*).[[48]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-318 "note") These can be tabulated, as in [Table 10](#tbl10).

### **Table 10: *Mahāvibhāṣā* Stages of Purification**

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_129_Image_0001.jpg)

*\*The Stage of the Disciple* (*Śrāvakabhūmi*) explains the three elements as follows. The element of abandoning is the consequence of abandoning all *saṃskāra*s that are abandoned by [the path of] vision. The element of dispassion is the consequence of abandoning all activities or constructions (*saṃskāra*s) that are abandoned by [the path of] bringing to being. The element of cessation is due to the cessation of all attachment (*upadhi*).[[49]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-319 "note")

### ***The Fourth Tetrad in the Pali Sources and the Remainder of the Discourse***

If we follow the pattern envisaged so far, then the final tetrad should originally have been concerned with the development of insight. In the *suttanta* literature we do not find the kind of structured account of insight that we find for the four *jhāna*s or the formless attainments. That seems to be something that is only fully developed later, notably in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga.* Earlier, what is perhaps envisaged is a more spontaneous process. Still, we can generally say that some kind of *dhamma* investigation leads to insight into one or more of the three signs: impermanence, suffering, and no-self. That is generally presented as the beginning of the development of insight proper and is appropriate for the first stage of the last tetrad.

<!-- Page 121 -->

What then follows is some kind of re-evaluation of the phenomenal world that sees it as unattractive and a consequential disenchantment. That brings about a state free from attachment. This is what we have here in the second stage of the fourth tetrad: contemplating dispassion. With the third stage we have full immersion into insight—that is, a state that closely parallels the noble (*ariya*) stages to follow, but remains worldly (*lokiya*).[[50]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-320 "note") We should then expect that, like the final stages in the first three tetrads, the fourth stage represents the breakthrough to a higher level. In this case that must be stream-entry.

The *Discourse on Mindfulness with In-and-Out Breathing* continues by indicating that just as the sixteen stages have developed the four foundations of mindfulness, so the latter develop the seven factors of awakening (*sambojjhaṅga*s). This must be intended to represent the transcendent practice of the *ariya*s. Indeed, some sources restrict the *(sam)bojjhaṅga*s to the *lokuttara* level. That, for example, is the usual position of the *Dhammasaṅgaṇī* and the *Vibhaṅga*. If so, we might expect the last section, on freedom by wisdom (*vijjāvimutti*), to be concerned with *arahat*ship. However, since this chapter is concerned with the sixteen stages, we will not address this final portion of the *sutta* in detail here.

## **Interpretations and Divergences**

The divergences in the list of the sixteen stages can be understood as follows: the stages, or at least the first twelve stages, were originally as we still find them in the Theravādin and other sources. They in essence simply described the path to *jhāna* in the first tetrad, the path to the formless attainments in the second tetrad, the path to the development of insight in the third tetrad, and the path to stream-entry in the last tetrad. Early followers of *abhidharma*, the *ābhidharmika*s, gave an alternative *abhidharma* interpretation, interpreting the fourth stage not as describing the process of calming the breath but instead as the state where the breath is completely tranquilized: the fourth *jhāna*. It was then natural to envisage the next two tetrads using an *abhidharma*-style analysis. The first three stages were then understood as, properly speaking, corresponding to the first three *jhāna*s—probably intending full attainment rather than neighborhood concentration (*sāmantaka*). Such an *ābhidharmika* interpretation of the first four stages was not adopted into the traditions of the Vibhajyavādin schools such as the Dharmaguptakas or the Theriyas. This may well have been because they had a rather developed exegesis of breathing mindfulness that does not seem to have been taken over into Sarvāstivādin literature. The Pali tradition at least developed its own *abhidhamma*-based exegesis, probably at a fairly early date.

<!-- Page 122 -->

Although the *abhidhamma/abhidharma*-based developments were probably not original, they represent a natural and inevitable growth in sophistication, and they add much to the depth and richness of later understanding of breathing mindfulness. Such notions may well have had their origins in the later years of the Buddha’s long teaching career.

With the last four stages we have alternative sets of four terms in at least these two traditions. Various sequences of insight knowledges are actually found in early sources; so it may well be that this variation is quite old and did not originally imply any difference of understanding. The last four stages are given an *abhidhamma/abhidharma*-based interpretation in both the Theriya and the Sarvāstivādin literature. This allows them to be interpreted as four stages leading to stream-entry or as the four paths or (for an *arahat*) as aspects of fruition-attainment.

The versions connected with Kumārajīva seem to represent an attempt at synthesis of the two main traditions, either by Kumārajīva himself or by the Mahāyānist sources on which he drew.

# 7. A Lost Meditation Tradition from the Silk Road

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

## **The Eastern Silk Roads**

What is commonly known as the “Silk Road” refers to a complex of trade routes that, from at least the second century b.c.e. and probably much earlier, directly linked the civilizations of the Iranians and Hellenistic Greeks with those of China and the Indian subcontinent and indirectly created secondary trading connections with Europe and Africa to the one side and Southeast Asia on the other.
