In the present context we may note that from around the first century b.c.e., if not somewhat earlier, Buddhism began to spread along the Silk Road from Afghanistan northward into Bactria and Sogdiana and eastward along the trade routes both north and south of the Taklimakan desert, an area in southwest Xinjiang region, China, bounded by the Kunlun mountains, the Pamir mountains, the Tian Shan range, and the Gobi desert. We do not really know when this process began—there is no reason to suppose that the first Buddhist travelers along this route would have left any literary, historical, or archaeological record. We know about the process only after it has gathered sufficient momentum to attract the support of wealthy donors and kings. By then it may already have been in decline.

<!-- Page 123 -->

What is in any case certain is that during the middle centuries of the first millennium c.e., Buddhist monasteries and shrines were to be found scattered along the eastern Silk Road routes. These constituted what must have been an important cultural unifying force linking these territories with areas to the south in present-day northern Afghanistan, northern Pakistan, and north-western India, where Buddhist centers had been established from around the third century b.c.e. Buddhist monasticism remained a significant force in many parts of this large region at least until the coming of Islam, and in some parts down to the fourteenth century.

The later history of Buddhism in Central Asia does not concern us now. It is enough to note that when European and Japanese interest in the antiquities of this area began to develop in the nineteenth century, the region contained numerous sites of great interest. The dry climate preserved both art historical and textual antiquities in large numbers from a period significantly more than a millennium ago. This included much non-Buddhist material too—Nestorian Christian, Manichaean Gnostic, and Zoroastrian, to name but three. But our interest is with the spectacular discoveries of objects of Buddhist origin, and in particular with one area and with one specific text from that area.

## **The Background to the Discovery of the Manuscript**

Awareness of Central Asia as a possible source of well-preserved antiquities developed gradually over the last decades of the nineteenth century. The successful journeys of Sven Hedin (starting in the 1890s), in what was then known as Eastern Turkestan, resulted in a series of expeditions to the area mounted or funded by learned academies, governments, and wealthy individuals. In the West, those associated with the name of Sir Aurel Stein are especially well known, but at least seven countries sent out such missions and their acquisitions are stored in more than a dozen museums and libraries around the world. Our concern today is with the results of the expeditions sent out from Berlin and particularly with the third expedition, which worked in the Kučā (Kucha) region.

This four-man expedition was under the charge of Albert Grünwedel and Albert von Le Coq (1860–1930),[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-321 "note") although the library or bookstore in which our manuscript was found was the discovery of Theodore Bartus. Bartus, a native of Lassan on the river Oder in western Pomerania, had been a sailor and qualified pilot on a sailing vessel for many years and then a landholder in Australia until he lost his savings in a bank crash. He then took up employment with the Berlin Ethnological Museum (Berliner Museum für Völkerkunder) and was the only person to take part in all four of the German (Prussian) “Turfan” expeditions. His practical skills were in many ways the mainstay of these ventures.

<!-- Page 124 -->

Early in 1906, while the team was working in Kïzïl (36 km to the northwest of the town of Kučā), Bartus came across a library in which various birch bark manuscripts were found and in particular a small room with some stone bookshelves covered with a dense layer of rather decayed manuscript material. It was from here that the main part of our meditation manual was retrieved.

## **The Manuscript Material**

Because of the state of the main manuscript and the unfamiliarity of its contents, it was not until 1964 that an edition and German translation of the text was published by Dieter Schlingloff (with a facsimile volume following two years later). Since no title for the work is known, he referred to it as the *Yogalehrbuch*, and it is now usually referred to by that name even in English. In this chapter I shall simply call it the *Meditation Manual*.

The principal manuscript is on birch bark and dates from the seventh century.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-322 "note") For his edition Professor Schlingloff also made use of three fragments from paper manuscripts of similar date. Two of these were found together with the birch bark manuscript and one came from a location slightly farther east at Šorčuq (Shorchuk), near what is now Karasahr in Xinjiang, China. Working with this material Schlingloff was able to restore what he estimated to be about 42 percent of the text. Because the *Meditation Manual* applies a similar methodology to various topics, we can probably form a sufficient idea of the bulk of its contents.

A small amount of additional material has surfaced since 1966. In particular, we can note some further pieces of a paper manuscript preserved in the Pelliot collection in Paris, dating from a slightly later period. These appear to have come from nearby Duldur-aqur and have been edited by Nobuyoshi Yamabe.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-323 "note") The Hoernle collection of the British Library also contains at least five small fragments, which have been variously edited by Jens-Uwe Hartmann and by Seishi Karashima and Klaus Wille.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-324 "note") The provenance is certainly somewhere in the Kučā area, on the northern rim of the Taklimakan desert. The *Meditation Manual* has recently been reprinted with additional material by Hartmann and others.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-325 "note")

<!-- Page 125 -->

It seems, then, that we have definite evidence of the existence of at least four copies, and possibly more, of the *Meditation Manual* in the Kučā region around the seventh and eighth centuries. This is important because it suggests that the text had at least some currency in Central Asia, although we have no Indian reference to its methods and no Chinese translation appears to have existed or, if one was made, it has been lost.

This means that we must work out by inference from other materials found in the same area and by analysis of the contents of the *Manual* exactly how it fits into the history of Buddhism in general and of Buddhist meditation in particular. Schlingloff himself suggested that, like other works found in the same location, it must have been a work of the Sarvāstivādin school; in that case, it would derive from their centers in Kashmir (and the region around Mathurā). He also felt that it showed no definite signs of Mahāyāna Buddhist doctrine. Others see a more definite Mahāyāna element and a possible origin in Central Asia itself. It is, however, unlikely that they would have had sufficient knowledge of Sanskrit in this area to compose such a text without prior Indian models.

It has also been pointed out that the presence of a number of copies of a text of this kind—one closely connected to the actual practice of meditation—probably evidences actual living practice of meditation among the monks living here. This is particularly clear at Kïzïl, an old Silk Road town in what is now Baicheng County, Aksu Prefecture, Xinjiang, China. Here, a honeycomb of “cell” dwellings in a cave suggest that the place was used by a number of monks for meditation. We may suppose that cave dwellings of this kind typically begin as the habitation of relatively ascetic practice-oriented monks. In India, we might perhaps speak of forest dwellers. Any such center is liable to acquire a reputation for sanctity and eventually become a resort for pilgrims and those seeking respite from the troubles of ordinary life.

Once you have pilgrims and a wider range of visitors, you need to have something to show them, and you must create opportunities for them to earn merit by donations on both a small scale and a large scale. Not surprisingly, we see the creation of shrines, sculpture, and paintings both on perishable materials and on those of a more durable kind.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-326 "note") In India, and for that matter in most other locations, wood and textiles and the like usually do not survive, whereas here in Central Asia some of it does prove amazingly long-lasting under desert conditions. This undoubtedly tells us much that we would otherwise not know about the appearance of many Buddhist centers elsewhere at this time. Of particular interest in this case are the paintings. The *Meditation Manual* has a strong emphasis on visualization, and it does not seem too far-fetched to suppose that there may be some connection between the content of the paintings and many of the elements that occur in the visualizations.

<!-- Page 126 -->

We may then suppose that the *Meditation Manual* represents a tradition of practice, most probably originating in Kashmir or a nearby area—one that had spread along the more northern of the two main routes between China and India. This locates us spatially, but I have not so far addressed the exact date. The heyday of the artwork surviving at Kïzïl is probably from the fourth to the seventh century.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-327 "note") It is important to recall that the date of the manuscript is not the same as the date of the work it contains. That is likely to be quite a bit earlier than the seventh century. Nothing in it would seem to rule out a date before the fourth century c.e., and it may well be some centuries older than that. For this we need to look at the contents of the *Meditation Manual* and its place in the general development of Buddhist meditation.

## **The Structure of the *Meditation Manual***

The basic structure of the *Meditation Manual* consists of some fifteen exercises that can usefully be listed under five headings, as in [Table 11](#tbl11).[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-328 "note") It has to be noted that we have neither the precise beginning of the work nor its conclusion; so we do not know for certain whether there might have been one or two more topics of recollection at the end. There could even have been some entirely additional subjects, but that seems improbable. Although we can view this as a list of fifteen distinct methods of meditation, as we shall see, it seems more likely that they should be grouped under five headings.

### **Table 11: The Structure of the *Meditation Manual***

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| Exercise | To counteract |
| 1. Ugliness (*aśubha*) |  |
| 2. Mindfulness through in-and-out breathing |  |
| 3. Analytic meditations |  |
| Elements (*dhātu*s) |  |
| Aggregates (*skandha*s) | The view [that there exists] a self (*ātmadṛṣṭi*) |
| Spheres (*āyatana*s) | Thirst for existence and objects of enjoyment (*bhavabhoga-tṛṣṇā*) |
| Conditioned origination (*pratītya-samutpāda*) | Delusion (*moha*) 136R2 Ignorance (*ajñāna*) 139R4 |
| 4. Immeasurables |  |
| Loving-kindness (*maitrī*) | Anger (*krodha*) 139R4 Ill will (*vyāpāda*) 147R1 |
| Compassion (*karuṇā*) | Due to the removal of another’s happiness and welfare (*para-sukha-hitāpanayana-kāraṇa*) 147R1 The cause … 147R2 Cruelty (*vihiṃsā*) 152R6 Delighting in another’s suffering (*para-duḥkha-prīti*) 152R6/153V1 |
| Gladness (*muditā*) | Envy and boredom (*īrṣyārati*) 152R6 Not delighting in another’s happiness (*para-sukhâprīti*) 153V1 Envy and boredom (*īrṣyārati*) 155V3 |
| Equanimity (*upekṣā*) | Sensual passion and ill will (*kāmarāga-vyāpāda*) 155V3 |
| 5. Recollections |  |
| Buddha |  |
| Dharma |  |
| Sa*ṅ*gha |  |
| *Śīla* |  |

<!-- Page 127 -->

It is clear that the text originally listed what specific mental obstacles each of these fifteen exercises is designed to counteract, but those from the beginning and end are mostly missing at present. From other sources it is not difficult to guess what the first two would have been, since meditation on the unpleasant is commonly given as counteracting some type of greed or attachment, while breathing mindfulness is frequently referred to as the cure for discursive thinking. Very little has been written in English about the *Meditation Manual*, but one notable exception is an article by David Ruegg, published in 1967.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-329 "note") In it he refers to earlier work by Paul Demiéville and points out the existence of a number of sources in Chinese that mention “Five Gates.” Most but not all loosely correspond to the five headings given here. Ruegg sees a possible connection between these lists and the specific influence of the famous early fifth-century translator Kumārajīva, who was himself a native of Kučā.

This may be so, but let us note here that such lists are much older and likely to have been shared by most, if not all, Buddhist schools. Already such notions are found in canonical texts that belong to earlier strata of the Pali Canon. For example, in the *Itivuttaka* we read this:

> Dwell contemplating the unpleasant/ugly in the body, monks, and let breathing mindfulness be well established by you within, and around the face; dwell contemplating impermanence in all constructed things. The underlying tendency toward attachment (*rāga*) that is found in pleasing things is abandoned by those who dwell contemplating the unpleasant/ugly in the body. When breathing mindfulness is well established within, and around the face, external inclinations toward thinking that are associated with distress do not occur. For those who dwell contemplating impermanence in all constructed things, ignorance is abandoned and knowledge arises.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-330 "note")

Here in a text that probably predates the arrival of Buddhism in Central Asia, we already have the first two or three of our five headings, the meditations on the uglinesses/unpleasantnesses and the breath. In actuality, meditation on impermanence is closely linked to the exercises I have grouped under the third heading: conditioned origination.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-331 "note")

<!-- Page 128 -->

Even closer, in the *Udāna* we have this:

> The unpleasant/ugly should be practiced in order to abandon attachment. Loving-kindness should be practiced in order to abandon ill will. Breathing mindfulness should be practiced to cut off thinking. The recognition of impermanence should be practiced in order to root out pride in what one is; for, Meghiya, one who recognizes impermanence firmly establishes recognition of no-self. One who recognizes no-self achieves the rooting out of pride in what one is*—nibbāna* in this very life.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-332 "note")

This passage is also found in the *Aṅguttara-nikāya* (A IV 358, 353).

In the *Udāna* and the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*, we have the first of the immeasurables, or divine abidings (*brahmavihāra*s), in second place. We should understand that the practice of loving-kindness leads automatically into the practice of compassion, joy in the joys of others, and equanimity; so the single mention of loving-kindness automatically implies the full set of the four immeasurables. The negative qualities that each of them counteract are also found in canonical Pali texts.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-333 "note") Indeed, one equally early discourse in the *Majjhima-nikāya* (addressed to the Buddha’s son Rāhula) adds both the other three immeasurables and the four qualities they counteract.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-334 "note")

What is notably absent in all these sources is the set of exercises placed under our fifth heading: the recollections, starting with the recollection of the Buddha (*buddhānusmṛti*). That is all the more surprising when we note that both lists of ten and six recollections are found in Pali canonical works and in parallel texts in Chinese. The full list of ten recollections could not be used here because breathing mindfulness (which is one of the ten recollections) has already been included as the second exercise. We should not be surprised that some later lists give only the first recollection—recollection of the Buddha. This would be seen as leading naturally into the others.

It may be that the recollection of the Buddha represents a somewhat later element in the older literature, but in any case, it certainly precedes both the development of Mahāyāna literature and the likely introduction of Buddhism to Central Asia. Its addition to lists of meditation practices related to negative qualities that they counteract is found only in one of the latest texts of the Pali Canon, \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*; Nidd I 360; 453; Nidd II).[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-335 "note") This is best presented as a table. The context is a description of the way in which the Buddha adjusts his teaching for individuals of six different character types. Here we can ignore the third stage, which is not a type of meditation exercise in the usual sense. What remains are teachings in a set of five topics, which reasonably correspond to the five headings of our *Meditation Manual*. This can be seen in [Table 12](#tbl12).[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-336 "note")

<!-- Page 129 -->

### **Table 12: *The Exposition* (*Niddesa*) Analysis of Temperament and Teaching**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| Exercise given by the Buddha | For the temperament (*carita*) |
| He gives a talk on the topic of ugliness/unpleasantness (*asubhakathṃ katheti*) | The temperament based in passion (*rāgacarita*) |
| He explains the cultivation of loving-kindness (*mettābhāvanaṃ ācikkhati*) | The temperament based in hatred (*dosacarita*) |
| He gets him to stay near teachers, so that he can ask questions at the right time, listen to *dhamma* at the right time, and have discussion about *dhamma* (*uddese paripucchāya kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya garusaṃvāse niveseti*) | The temperament based in delusion (*mohacarita*) |
| He explains mindfulness through in-and-out breathing (*ānāpānasatiṃ ācikkhati*) | The temperament based in thinking (*vitakkacarita*) |
| He explains signs that arouse confidence: the Buddha and his noble awakening, the Dhamma and the noble law, the Saṇgha who have entered upon a noble way, and the precepts (*sīlāni*). (*pasādanīyaṃ nimittaṃ ācikkhati buddhasubodhiṃ dhammasudhammataṃ saṇghasuppaṭipattiṃ sīlāni ca*) | The temperament based in faith (*saddhācarita*) |
| He explains the signs that arouse insight into the self: the mark of impermanence, the mark of suffering, and the mark of non-self. (*attano…ācikkhati vipassanānimittaṃ aniccākāraṃ dukkhākāraṃ anattākāraṃ*) | The temperament based in knowledge (*ñāṇacarita*) |

I infer from all this that the general outline of the *Meditation Manual* follows a traditional pattern that is likely to be earlier than the date of the *Meditation Manual* itself and almost certainly precedes the creation of Mahāyāna literature in the form in which we know it. The intention of the author or authors of this *Manual* itself is certainly to present the fifteen topics as a logical progression through which the meditator gradually advances.

I do not intend to suggest that the contents of the *Manual* are necessarily of much earlier origin. The *Meditation Manual* does contain occasional references to elements or stages of the Buddhist path that are known to us mainly from Sarvāstivādin sources and may belong to the early centuries c.e. That said, we have little knowledge of other schools in Kashmir and neighboring areas at this time and do not know how far such ideas were shared among different schools. I turn now to the actual content of our *Meditation Manual.*

<!-- Page 130 -->

## **The Method of the *Meditation Manual***

What we find is a rather elaborate visualization procedure as a kind of technique that can be applied to each of the fifteen types of meditative exercise. In using the term “visualization,” I do not distinguish between an exercise for the purpose of seeing something and the spontaneous occurrence of something seen that is subsequently described. In practice it is often not possible to distinguish between these two possibilities.

A great deal of fairly vivid imagery is used. Many of the elements of this imagery occur regularly in early Indian art—Buddhist and non-Buddhist. These are some examples: vases used to sprinkle nectar or pour other liquids; lion thrones; *makara*s;[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-337 "note") *vajra* thrones;[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-338 "note") caves; standards (*dhvaja*s); parasols; elephants; lions; *haṃsa*s;[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-339 "note") lotus flowers and ponds; wish-granting jewels; jewel trees; jewel flowers; jeweled wheels; jeweled palaces; crystal, beryl, diamond, and other precious stones; conch shells; pearls; shrine halls (*kūṭāgāra*s); the four colors (blue, yellow, red, and white); pools and showers of oil; streams of flowers; jewels; foam, bubbles, and so on; moon and sun disks; *nakṣatra*s.[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-340 "note")

Nor is this imagery static. It is regularly extended in space both within the body and outward from various locations on the body, horizontally to the seas and continents of traditional Buddhist cosmology and beyond, and vertically to various lower and higher realms. It is also extended backward in time to the six past Buddhas and into the future with Maitreya, the Buddha-to-come. The visions are also peopled with deities and divine beings from the same worldview, notably Brahma and Indra, *preta*s,[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-341 "note") heavenly nymphs, and *garuḍa*s.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-342 "note") We find, too, visions containing numerous bodhisattvas and Buddhas, although no others (apart from past Buddhas and Maitreya) are given names.

Some of the imagery is more unusual. Let us note the sending out of streams made up of imagined portions of the body: bones, flesh, bodily organs, and various unattractive liquids. Or, on the more pleasing side, milk trees and milk pillars, streams, seas, and pools of milk. Particularly notable is the frequent occurrence of visual images, referred to as mental images or signs (*nimitta*s), which are explained as representations of particular qualities.

<!-- Page 131 -->

Yamabe has drawn attention to the resemblances between much of this imagery and that which occurs in a number of texts found in Chinese from the fifth century c.e.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-343 "note") Some of these are believed to be Central Asian creations from Turfan, an area near Ürümqi (Urumchi) in what is now the Uygur Autonomous region of Xinjiang, China. This associates our *Meditation Manual* with Mahāyāna, although this work lacks many of the most typical features of the developed Mahāyāna. I have mentioned the absence of large numbers of named Buddhas and bodhisattvas. We can also note the complete absence of the philosophical side of Mahāyāna—neither the emptiness teachings of the Perfection of Wisdom literature nor the mentalism of the Yogācāra school. The presence of an aspiration to become a Buddha is of course not unique to the Mahāyāna. A similar position to Yamabe is taken in a more detailed recent study by Sven Bretfeld.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-344 "note")

I find myself in disagreement. It seems to me that the world of the *Meditation Manual* is rather that of the art of Bharhut and Sāñcī and of the latest texts in the Pali Canon. It seems to represent the common Buddhist tradition around the beginning of the Christian era or a little earlier—a tradition from which both later Mahāyāna and the Southern Buddhist tradition develop in different ways. I would see the *Meditation Manual* then as continuing an older tradition of meditation. This might either be because it was preserved or written among followers of a tradition that rejected the newer Mahāyāna developments or because the core of the text dates back to an earlier period.

## **Visualization in the Pali Canon**

There is quite a large body of material in the early Pali texts related to the visual, both in specifically meditational contexts and in the use of imagery related to light and color. Probably the strongest indication of this lies in an account of the path that is found quite frequently as an alternative to the sequence that proceeds from the four *jhāna*s to the four formless attainments, discussed in chapter 1. In this alternative sequence, after developing the four *jhāna*s, the road continues with the six higher knowledges (*abhiññā*s), culminating in the destruction of the *āsava*s—that is, in *arahat*ship. The first of these higher knowledges is the various kinds of achievement or potency (*iddhi*). *Iddhi* is mentioned more than sixty times in the Pali Canon. It seems to involve a kind of visionary experience, resembling the kind of thing sometimes described in accounts of so-called astral projection and the like: walking on water, flying through the air, even touching and stroking the moon and sun in their majesty. Later on in the list of the higher knowledges we have such things as divine vision and divine hearing.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-345 "note")

<!-- Page 132 -->

There is not space here to survey all of the material related to visualization thoroughly; so I will confine myself to mentioning a few of the more relevant examples. In the *Upakkilesa-sutta* we find specific mention of the need to see both light and visual forms in meditation (MN 128; MN III 152–162).[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-346 "note") This alone would be sufficient to show that some visual element was present in meditation from a relatively early date.

Related symbolism in the Pali tradition includes such things as the use of the analogy of the directions to structure the universe of social relations or to establish a sense of protection by non-human beings. We also find examples of meditational use of directions, notably to spread loving-kindness and similar qualities to living beings in the different directions. The directions and the closely related number four, as well as sets of colors or precious materials, are used to enhance visualization practices. Under this heading we can include some materials related to the traditional Indian cosmology of the central mountain and four continents. Animals are also used in this context.

Perhaps the oldest example of this kind of use is to be found in the *Mahāsudassana-sutta* of the *Dīgha-nikāya* (D 17; DN II 169–198). Here we have many sets of four and seven as part of the descriptions of the city and palace of the *cakkavattin* king or Universal Monarch named Very Fair to Behold (Mahāsudassana).[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-347 "note") The first set of four gives the gates of the city as made of gold, silver, beryl (*veḷuriya*), and crystal. The seven walls of the city are made of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, ruby, emerald, and every kind of jewel. Similarly, the lotus ponds constructed around the city have tiles and stairways either of gold, silver, beryl, or crystal, while the balustrades of the stairways have pillars of the same material but crossbars and copings of an opposing material (paired as gold/silver or beryl/crystal).

The description of the monarch’s palace of Dhamma, constructed for him by the god Vissakamma, follows similar lines. The tiles, the 84,000 pillars, the seats, the twenty-four stairways, the 84,000 chambers,[[28]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-348 "note") and the trees inscribed on the doorways are all of the same four materials. The lotus pond, also named Dhamma, that the king had constructed in front of the palace is similarly described. These descriptions are the literary source of important elements in the descriptions of wondrous pure lands found in many Mahāyāna *sūtra*s, most notably the *Array of the Happy Land Sūtra* (*Sukhāvatīvyūha-sūtra*). The link between such descriptions and contemplating the idealized form of the Buddha as a meditation topic is well known.

<!-- Page 133 -->

The forebears of the southern tradition do not go quite as far as visualizing pure lands as a way of contemplating the Buddha, but there are some sources that are clearly moving in that direction. One *sutta* in the *Saṃyutta-nikāya* describes how the Buddha manifests himself above a Brahma deity, seated cross-legged in the air and meditating upon the fire element—that is, manifesting flames, as the commentary explains.[[29]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-349 "note") Four of the great *arahat* disciples then do likewise, taking up positions to the east, south, west, and north successively. This foreshadows much material in the Mahāyāna sources. The *Paṭisambhidāmagga*, a later canonical text, refers explicitly to suffusing a small or large space with blue or yellow or red or white.[[30]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-350 "note")

One Pali text, included in the Southern Buddhist Canon although certainly one of the last texts to be included there, does go somewhat further. This is a *Khuddaka-nikāya* text known as \**The Harvest of Deeds* (*Apadāna*). This text has not yet been translated and has not been much studied until recently.[[31]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-351 "note") Its first chapter gives a visualization practice, commencing with the mental construction of a palace that is subsequently peopled with buddhas and *paccekabuddha*s to whom offerings are made. It is notable that the description of the palace has a number of features in common with the palace of the Universal Monarch Very Fair to Behold, most notably in its stories of beryl, gold, and coral.

A later chapter gives a virtual apotheosis of the Buddha’s former wife Yasodharā in which, just before her death, she transforms herself into a birdlike shape after the fashion of the universe—that is, the parts of her body represent the four continents.[[32]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-352 "note")

## **The Practical Application**

One of the striking features of the meditation method outlined in the *Yogalehrbuch* is the use of locations on the body, as in YL 79:

> Should the [practitioner of yoga] practice in-and-out breathing further, the world and the body (*āśraya*) appear to be made out of rock crystal. Then from [the practitioner’s] head [a tree of] jewels permeates innumerable world systems and abides. On this tree, among the densely foliaged branches appear Buddhas, teaching *dharma*.

<!-- Page 134 -->

We find this in YL 132:

> Next a stream of milk goes forth from the hollow place below the midpoint between the eyebrows. It breaks through the circle of earth and stills all the suffering of the beings in the [four] Descents. It breaks through as far as the circle of wind, returns, and enters in at the navel. It goes out through the head, goes as far as the supreme (*aghaniṣṭha*) gods, and returns. Surrounded by sense-sphere, form-sphere, and formless-sphere gods, it enters again through the head.

And YL 137 has this:

> The practitioner thinks, “I have lifted these [beings] out of the mud of great wretchedness,” and becomes strongly compassionate toward them. Out of all the bodily openings come forth space-like silk cloths [and they abide enveloping all beings].

In total, we meet eleven such locations:

1. Head
2. Forehead
3. Between the eyebrows
4. Cheeks
5. Tip of the nose
6. Throat
7. Shoulders
8. Fingers of both hands
9. Between the big toe and the adjacent toe (*makara*)
10. Navel
11. All bodily orifices

To these could be added the head, the heart, and the body.

Another feature of this method, as was already illustrated in the examples cited, is constant movement alternating with a return to stillness, often in the navel. Sometimes the movement extends outward to the boundary mountains of the world system (*cakravāḍa*). Sometimes it moves downward to the circle of winds and upward to the highest of the *deva*s who possess some kind of physical body—the supreme gods, the *aghaniṣṭha* (Pali *akaniṭṭha*) *deva*s.[[33]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-353 "note") Sometimes a tree or a standard arises from the heart or elsewhere and rises up through the head to extend from the highest heavens to the lowest levels below the earth.

Again, the practitioner or a visualized entity sometimes experiences being sunk in pools of oil or milk or lotus pools. This is reminiscent of the similes for the *jhāna*s in the *sutta*s. With this goes a tendency to move between the repulsive and the non-repulsive. We can compare this with the idea expressed in the *Tikaṇḍakī-sutta* of the *Aṅguttara-nikāya*:

> It is good, monks, from time to time to abide recognizing the repulsive in the unrepulsive. It is good, monks, from time to time to abide recognizing the unrepulsive in the repulsive. It is good, monks, from time to time to abide recognizing the repulsive in that which is both unrepulsive and repulsive. It is good, monks, from time to time to abide recognizing the unrepulsive in that which is both repulsive and unrepulsive. It is good, monks, from time to time to abide equanimous, mindful, and aware, turning away from both the repulsive and the unrepulsive.[[34]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-354 "note")

<!-- Page 135 -->

The purpose for this given in the *sutta* is to overcome desire for the desirable, aversion for what arouses aversion, and so on. Our Sanskrit text follows a similar sequence, starting with the ugliness (*aśubha*) practice.

A high point of the visualization sequences is often some kind of sprinkling (*abhiṣeka*), which acts as a sign of success in the meditative exercise. The practitioner is showered or sprinkled with streams of milk or oil or gems or different colors or bodily liquids.

We then find various descriptions of mental images, objects, or signs (*nimitta*s), images that arise naturally in the mind when concentration on a particular meditation object has developed.[[35]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-355 "note") The use of *nimitta*s or visual representations of qualities and Buddhist teachings is shown here:

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| young boy | mind, heart, consciousness (*citta*) |
| conch shell/eye sockets | space element |
| monkey/spotted antelope, torch, etc. | consciousness element |
| beryl woman man | mindfulness (*smṛti*) clear comprehension (*samprajanya*) |
| golden woman dressed in white | compassion (*karuṇā*) |
| shining, golden, and joyful woman | joy in the joy of others (*muditā*) |
| man and woman dressed in white | self-respect (*hrī*) and regard for consequences (*avatrāpya*) |
| eight Buddha images on lion thrones | eightfold path |
| woman with lamp in hand | knowledge |

## **Elements Suggestive of Mahāyāna**

It is only as we near the end of the *Meditation Manual* that we begin to find elements that are suggestive of Mahāyāna Buddhism: the caution of the meditator at entering the city of *nirvāṇa*, on the grounds that one should turn rather to compassion for the “ocean” of beings who still remain suffering, is framed in terms we come to associate with the Mahāyāna movement. It is not clear, however, whether this text can be considered to be a Mahāyānist work as opposed to a composition from a period before the Mahāyāna has fully developed.

We find this, for example, in YL 171 (in the following passage, text that has been lost is denoted by …):

> (e) A white-colored woman with [dressed in] white [appears] as an embodiment of [great compassion] in the body of the Lord, [and says, “Accomplished is] what was to be done, fulfilled is the promise; completed is the training to be done, peaceful is *nirvāṇa*.”
>
> (f) Then the Lord [utters] this verse:
>
> [Impermanent] are constructions,
>
> subject to the law of arising and passing away:
>
> [they arise and disappear;
>
> their] cessation is happiness.
>
> When he has spoken thus, he enters the city of *nirvāṇa* as if enveloped by masses of space-like clouds and goes to peacefulness like a lamp, [no more] coveting [abodes?]. The disciples afterward enter the city of *nirvāṇa* [at the time of the] *parinirvāṇa* and [come in the same way] to peacefulness.
>
> (g) ([The yoga practitioner, however, who follows after them] is held back by the guardian of the gate [who says,] “If you …; then you will also enter here.” The whole [sea of living beings] appears … as well as those living in the Descents, who are bound by fetters and those who are tormented by various sufferings. [These] … and they say, “Save us, O you who are full of compassion; let it not behoove you [to go into the state of full enlightenment].” In [the practitioner’s] heart [arises] the above-mentioned embodiment [of great compassion]. [She] takes hold [of the practitioner] by the hands [and says], “Whither would you wish to go, leaving the suffering in their predicament?” Equipoise becomes far from the practitioner; compassion enters the practitioner. With both arms the practitioner embraces [the whole sea of living beings] and [here there is a lacuna, but shortly afterward we learn that] [the practitioner’s body arises] radiant with the marks and minor marks.

<!-- Page 136 -->

This passage comes at the end of the section on equanimity (*upekṣā*) and seems partly to come from a desire to emphasize the difference between equanimity and hardheartedness.

# 8. The *Porāṇa* Meditation Tradition

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

Now we will turn to a type of meditation practice that has only recently reached the full awareness of scholars. It belongs to a tradition that is variously referred to as Tantric Theravāda, Esoteric Southern Buddhism, or as *Borān Yogāvacara* or *Borān Kammaṭṭhāna*. I am calling it here the *Porāṇa* tradition—the Tradition of the Elders. It seems to represent a form of Theravāda Buddhism that was formerly normative at least in the Buddhist areas of present-day Thailand, Laos, and Cambodia—perhaps more widely.

## **First Discoveries**

In 1896 the Pali Text Society in London published an edition of an unusual text on Buddhist meditation: the *Yogāvacara’s Manual of Indian Mysticism as Practised by Buddhists*.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-356 "note") It was based upon a copy of a single manuscript located in Teldeniya, near Kandy in Ceylon, by Anagārika Dharmapāla. Dharmapāla took it to London on his way to the 1893 Parliament of Religions in Chicago and gave it to T. W. Rhys Davids to edit. Dharmapāla claimed that the practice of *jhāna* had quite died out in Ceylon at this time, but we should perhaps take this as the rhetoric of reformers, since at least one monk who had been initiated into the technique and practiced it was alive in Ceylon as late as 1900.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-357 "note") And since the method was being taught as an esoteric one, there is no way of knowing to what extent it continued to be practiced after this date. The work is written in mixed Pali and Sinhaḷa and was translated as *Manual of a Mysti*c by F. L. Woodward on the basis of a “rough translation” by Suriyagoda Suma*ṅ*gala.

The text as received by Rhys Davids had neither title nor authorship, hence the titles made up by the editors for the text and its translation. However, Sinhalese scholars recognized a link to the eighteenth-century contacts with Siam that resulted in the formation of the Siam Nikāya, currently the largest branch of the Buddhist monastic order on the island. Indeed, Sinhalese records refer to the creation of a forest meditation center at this time for two monks and the instruction of some twenty-four disciples in a method of meditation that is almost certainly the one recorded in the *Yogāvacara’s Manual.*

<!-- Page 137 -->

Little research was done on this text during the period between the wars; it is only referred to sporadically. However, the discovery of the manuscript played an important role in the modern revival of meditation practice in Ceylon.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-358 "note") A version of the same text was eventually published in a collection in Ceylon under the title of “Book of Insight” (*Vidarśanāpota*), together with another text from a similar tradition, \**Seeing the Path to Freedom* (*Vimuttimaggadassana*).[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-359 "note") But the *Yogāvacara’s Manual* remained little studied until the French scholar François Bizot came upon a living tradition of this kind of meditative practice in Cambodia in the 1960s and 1970s. Bizot’s research is extremely important for the understanding of this and related meditation practices.

## **The Method of the *Yogāvacara’s Manual***

The method utilized in the *Yogāvacara’s Manual* is presented in Ceylonese tradition as an esoteric one, requiring initiation by a teacher, and that accords well with present-day practice in Cambodia. It has a strong ritual element, as is the case for all traditional forms of Theravāda Buddhism. It uses the Pali word *arahaṃ* as a mantra together with breathing mindfulness in the initial stages, then continues to apply a very repetitive sequence successively to various other meditation topics in an abbreviated form. The list of topics organized under twelve headings is in [Table 13](#tbl13).

### **Table 13: The Method of the *Yogāvacara’s Manual***[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-360 "note")

|  |
| --- |
| 1. Joy—five kinds of joy |
| 2. Six pairs |
| 3. Happiness of body and mind (*kāyacittasukha*) |
| 4. Mindfulness through in-and-out breathing |
| 5. The devices (*kasiṇa*) |
| 6. Ugliness/unpleasantness (*aśubha*) |
| 7. Mindfulness of body (*kāyagatāsati*) |
| 8. The recollections (*anussati*s) |
| 9. The formless meditations (*arūpavacara*s) |
| 10. The divine abidings (*brahmavihāra*s) |
| 11. Insight knowledges (*dasañāṇa*s, the ten knowledges) path and fruit of stream-enterer, once-returner, non-returner, and *arahat*, plus *nibbāna* |

The bulk of this material—namely, sections four to ten—consists of the usual subjects of meditation. Much of this material we met in the *Meditation Manual* from Central Asia discussed in the last chapter. Indeed, this can be understood as a slightly divergent version of the thirty-eight or forty subjects of meditation presented in the classical commentarial literature from Ceylon, themselves essentially a systematization of material in late canonical texts such as the *\*The Exposition* (*Niddesa*). In itself this is largely a matter of practices that were probably shared with all or most early Buddhist schools. However, the first two sections are distinctive teachings of the Pali tradition. No other Buddhist school appears to divide *pīti*, “joy,” into five kinds in the manner of the first section.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-361 "note") Similarly, the set of the six pairs is part of the basic teaching of the Pali *abhidhamma*. No other surviving branch of *abhidharma* has this explicit sequence, although explanations of the meaning of *praśrabdhi* in Sanskrit sources sometimes cover similar ground.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-362 "note") The third section seems to follow on from the first two. The whole work culminates in the final section, with a version of the sequence of insight knowledges and the set of the nine transcendent *dhamma*s: path and fruit of stream-entry to *arahat*ship, plus *nibbāna* itself.

<!-- Page 138 -->

Since the same method is applied in each section (and the text is therefore very abbreviated), it is sufficient to look at just the first section to understand the basic method. It begins with standard devotional practice: the various Pali chants that invoke the Buddha (*namo tassa* … three times) and going for refuge to the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Sa*ṅ*gha as a lifelong commitment. These are interspersed with the verses in praise of the three (*iti pi so* …). Then follows a verse declaring that, as a result of paying respect to the Triple Gem, great merit has been gained and by its power all obstacles (*antarāya*s) have been destroyed. Then the *Mettā-sutta* is chanted. After this are verses again giving homage to the Buddha and expressing the aspiration that by the power of that truth, all beings will be happy and the merit gained will benefit the hearer. Then comes an aspiration based on that merit for the destruction of inferior states, progress toward *nibbāna*, and protection against the five Māras.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-363 "note") The Buddha, the Dhamma, the Paccekasambuddha, and the Sa*ṅ*gha are again invoked, and the aspiration is made that by their power Māra will get no opportunity.

Now the text turns toward the specific practice. This starts with the invocation of the Buddha three times (*namo tassa* …) and then a formula of confession is repeated three times in Pali “for the sake of future restraint.” The aspirant commits to striving to fulfill the instructions of the omniscient Gotama by means of worship in the form of meditation practice. The practitioner then requests the acquired sign and the semblance sign, the way of approach (*upacāra*), the way of absorption (*appanā*), and momentary joy among the five joys in the inner chamber. Then a longer-term aspiration is made for the nine transcendent states in due sequence. Next the practitioner returns to the immediate aspiration for the momentary joy to arise while sitting in meditation practice and be experienced with the visual sense, in the sense of touch, and in the mind.

One then requests the qualities of the Buddha, the Dhamma, the Sa*ṅ*gha, the Teacher, and the course of practice (*kammaṭṭhāna*). (An extended version of the three refuges along these lines occurs sporadically in Theravāda Buddhism from the twelfth century onward.) The practitioner promises to dedicates this life to such practice, saying, “may it be a condition for *nibbāna*.”

<!-- Page 139 -->

Now what we would think of as the actual meditation practice begins. One sits cross-legged and upright in the meditation posture, establishing mindfulness of the breathing. One understands that one is breathing in a long breath, breathing out a long breath, breathing in a short breath, or breathing out a short breath. The method seems to be to focus eye consciousness at the tip of the nose while setting the mind on awareness of the breath in the heart and using either *arahaṃ* or *sammā arahaṃ* as an initial mantra to settle the mind.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-364 "note") Such a combination of the visual and a mantra with the traditional objects of meditation seems to be typical of the techniques of these meditative methods.

What follows is a description of the result of the practice. The practice of each of the five kinds of joy is preceded by the same invocation, requests, and aspirations as appropriate. In each case, the mental object (*nimitta*) is first experienced at the tip of the nose, then moved initially to the heart and subsequently to the navel. After successfully developing the five kinds of joy and the associated mental objects, we have a very complicated series of exercises to develop mastery. Some of this is not very clear, but Bizot provides explanations from present-day practitioners in Cambodia.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-365 "note") Subsequently, the various mental objects and elements are positioned at locations between the navel and the heart or in some kind of *maṇḍala*-like arrangement at the center of the body.

The same method is used in each section with slight variations. The order in which the elements appear varies depending on the particular objects of meditation. Also, the locations at which the various *nimitta*s are placed differs in the final section on the paths and fruits. Now they are mostly positioned at various places in the head. This use of various places in the body resembles that found in the *Meditation Manual*/*Yogalehrbuch* discussed in the last chapter. Also comparable to the practices in that work is a section in these works from Ceylon where, in the practice of breathing mindfulness, after the arising of the space element, the *nimitta* of the breath is extended outward from the nostril in the ten directions.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-366 "note") This involves seeing Mount Meru and the surrounding mountain ranges and seas, extending the vision upward to the six *deva* (sense-sphere) heavens, the sixteen *brahma* (form) realms, and beyond and downward to the land, sea, and atmosphere below.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-367 "note") Despite these common features, it does not seem likely that there is any direct connection between the methods derived in the manuscript from Central Asia and that from Ceylon. Similarities simply arise from a common background in ancient Buddhism.

<!-- Page 140 -->

## **The Picture as It Emerges after Bizot’s Research**

The *Yogāvacara’s Manual* seemed rather unusual, if not aberrant, in the early twentieth century, and scholarship concerning it did not advance much during this period, although a few related texts were noted in catalogues and collections. It was François Bizot’s discovery of related traditions in Cambodia and the subsequent assembling and publication of relevant texts that gradually led to the formation of a new picture. Far from being unique, this material belongs to a tradition Bizot initially identified as that of the *anciens Mahānikāya*—a tradition in Cambodia actively resisting reformist tendencies. In the Cambodian context it is sometimes referred to as the *Borān* tradition. As research progressed, it became clear that material from this tradition, although marginalized by reforms from the eighteenth century onward, is to be found scattered across Southeast Asia.

Initially, some scholars suggested that the texts Bizot collected might be quite recent in date, but as research has progressed, the dating has gradually been pushed back. Inscriptional evidence that pushed the dating of some of this material back to the seventeenth century has been located by Bizot and his associates. More recently, research by Kate Crosby and others at the School of Oriental and African Studies in London has found evidence from the sixteenth century.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-368 "note") And it may well be older.

What is at least clear is that we are not dealing simply with one or two idiosyncratic texts, or even with a special tradition local to Cambodia. Rather, what is involved here is a whole approach to Theravāda Buddhism that was once widespread across Southeast Asia. There is no agreed name for this form of Theravāda at present. Heinz Bechert adopted the name “Tantric Theravāda,” which has been followed by some. I have preferred the name Esoteric Southern Buddhism in order to avoid various value judgments and historical assumptions, which seem to me to be premature at this stage. Here I have adopted the name *Porāṇa* tradition, using the Pali word for “ancient” as this seems more appropriate for something that is pan-Theravādin.

<!-- Page 141 -->

My concern in this context is not with the *Porāṇa* tradition as a whole. This certainly embraces a broad field of loosely interconnected practices, covering such areas as ritual and monastic dress, to name only two. Rather, it lies more specifically in what we would call meditation practice. Nor would I wish to suggest that meditation practice in the *Porāṇa* tradition was homogenous. Rather, I would suppose that we are dealing with a rich diversity of practices linked together by common themes and shared ideas. How much of this diversity still survives is not entirely clear, given the tendency for it to continue in esoteric or at least discreet forms.

The origin of this tradition is also far from clear. Kate Crosby surveys the different theories that have been advanced to date:

* Mon influence
* Sri Lankan Abhayagirivihāra
* (Mūla)-sarvāstivāda
* Hindu influence
* Mahāyāna-Theravāda syncretism
* Theravāda that is exclusively un-Sri Lankan
* Re-emergence of pre-Buddhist practices

There are problems with all of these. Influences of the older Mon tradition upon the generally later known forms of Thai and Burmese Buddhism are generally probable.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-369 "note") We may also suspect that the *Porāṇa* tradition was formerly current among the Mon, but I am not at present aware of any conclusive proof of this. We have no evidence so far to suggest that this tradition is as old as the first millennium c.e., but if it is, then it could derive from the forms of Southeast Asian Theravāda current then among the Mon and the Pyu and in Arakan. Possible, but no proof to date.

Bizot has argued for connections with the heterodox traditions of Theravāda that were once important in Ceylon—the Abhayagiri and Jetavana schools. He based this on a passage in the *Vimuttimagga*, a work stemming from South Asia but extant now only in Chinese and partially in Tibetan. Kate Crosby has shown that the connection to the *Vimuttimagga* is quite tenuous; so even if that is a work of the Abhayagiri school, it has no bearing on the origins of the *Porāṇa* tradition. Similarly, Ole Pind has shown a parallel between a story in one esoteric work, the *Saddavimalā*, and a similar story in the *(Mūla)-sarvāstivādin Vinaya*.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-370 "note") Influence of that school on Southeast Asian Buddhism in general is well attested, for example, by murals in Pagán and by such works as the *\*An Account of the World* (*Lokapaññatti*) and the cult of Upagupta.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-371 "note") This may either represent influences from Pāla Bengal or could be transmitted from Ceylon. There is no way of knowing if the example found in the *Saddavimalā* is just another example of this general influence or due to a specific transmission of influences to the *Porāṇa* tradition.

<!-- Page 142 -->

Hindu or brahmanical influences have been postulated. Similarly, Mahāyānist influences can be suggested, but there is no trace of any influence from the Mantrayāna or Tantric variety of Mahāyāna Buddhism. In both cases some shared ideas are found and there are undoubtedly cases of direct influence on the Theravāda in general, but brahmanical influences go back to the beginnings of Buddhism and some apparently Mahāyānist ideas and practices derive from the forms of Buddhism current at the time of the rise of the Mahāyāna. We cannot at present show any conclusive influence either from Brahmanism or from Mahāyāna Buddhism on the specific development of the *Porāṇa* tradition. Similar problems arise in the case of postulated pre-Buddhist influences, either from ancient India or from the indigenous cultures of Southeast Asia. There are undoubtedly some influences of all these kinds, but it is not clear that any one of them was a dominant factor in the development of the *Porāṇa* tradition.

Bizot sees the *Porāṇa* tradition as in competition with textually based traditions from Ceylon. But it is far from clear that this was actually so during the period after the twelfth century when the reorganized Ceylon tradition spread across Southeast Asia. We know little about the kind of meditation practiced at that time and so cannot rule out the possibility that some or all of the elements that form the *Porāṇa* tradition came originally from the island.

## **General Features of the *Porāḷa* Tradition**

In a paper published in the volume *Indian Insights*, I referred to the Porāṇa tradition as a type of mysticism, which I called “tantro-kabbalistic.”[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-372 "note") By this I meant a form of mysticism that utilizes a rather elaborate map of correspondences between the human body, the cosmos, and some kind of higher reality or knowledge. In the process, it draws on the full resources of the widely dispersed traditions of magic and the occult—letter, sound, and number symbolism together with the use of structured patterns of shape or gesture. Often these are applied in ritual. Typically, this is linked with the spiritual practice of one of the major religions in a manner that integrates the system of correspondences with a model of the spiritual path and with various modes of spiritual practice.

<!-- Page 143 -->

There are a number of features apart from meditative practice that are characteristic of Esoteric Southern Buddhism:

1. Correspondences with the body
2. Correspondence with the cosmos
3. Correspondence with higher knowledge
4. Letter and number symbolism
5. Ritual
6. Application in daily life

A much longer list has since been given by Kate Crosby under eleven headings.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-373 "note") These include the creation of a Buddha within; the use of sacred language and of heart syllables combined with mantras and yantras; understanding sacred language as a kind of creative principle; esoteric interpretations of known words, objects, and myths; and the necessity of prior initiation for a ritual or practice. Also important is the spiritual recreation of fetal development as a ritual and meditative practice, and the use of the *abhidhamma* categories. Noticeably, this kind of practice is identified as *samathavipassanā*—that is, combining calm and insight in some way; it is typically not restricted to monks. Lay practitioners, including women, are not unusual and may even be teachers to monks.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-374 "note")

Most of these features were already identified by Bizot in his first work on the *Poṛāṇa* tradition, published in French under a title that we can render into English as the “Five-Branched Fig Tree.”[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-375 "note") The text presented there was translated from Cambodian. A different text from northern Thailand containing many of these correspondences has more recently been translated into English by Donald Swearer.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-376 "note") An exciting new development in the study of this material is the identification of the manuscript of a Sinhalese translation of the Fig Tree text in the Neville collection of the British Library.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-377 "note") An account of this discovery, first postulated by Andrew Skilton, has now been published in an article in the *Journal of Indian Philosophy* by Kate Crosby and her associates. The translation was made for one of the wives of the eighteenth-century Kandyan king Kīrti Śrī Rājasi*ṃ*ha in the mid-eighteenth century by one of the Thai monks sent from Ayutthaya by the Siamese king in order to teach meditation. This confirms what had already been suggested by scholars on other grounds. Rather than being a somewhat marginal and heterodox tradition, as it seemingly became in the twentieth century, in the eighteenth century the *Porāṇa* tradition was accepted and utilized at a senior level in the Thai monastic tradition.

<!-- Page 144 -->

## **The Meditative Methods of the *Porāḷa* Tradition**

We turn now to the specific meditation methods of the *Porāṇa* tradition. There is every reason to think that these were very diverse, as I have already suggested. I will simply give now a few examples where we have textual evidence. In the text from Cambodia translated into French by Bizot in his *le Chemin de Laṅkā* (*The Laṅkā Path*)[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-378 "note") we find the following request:

> I, teacher, request to study the holy *kammaṭṭhāna*s, which one speaks of as the *samatha kammaṭṭhāna* and the *vipassanā kammaṭṭhāna.* These holy *kammaṭṭhāna*s are the treasure of the holy Buddha who is the holy Self-Awakened One. The holy *kammaṭṭhāna*s are the treasures that fulfill the holy noble disciples.
>
> His noble sons aspire to study the *kammaṭṭhāna*s of *samatha* and *vipassanā* so as to destroy the five aggregates (*khandha*s), which are the groups of materiality, feelings, perceptions, mental formations, and consciousness; and with these abandoned, to enter the city of *Nibbāna*, as have all the past Masters who have destroyed the *āsava*s.

After referring to making offerings and a statement of the obstacles to be overcome, we have the following fairly orthodox aspiration:

> We wish to practice each of the holy *samatha kammaṭṭhāna*s, from the holy minor joy (*khuddakapīti*) to begin with, to the holy *kammaṭṭhāna* of Ugliness (*aśubha*) to finish. May we experience [meditation] both with and without signs. And further, let us be able to perceive the holy acquired sign and the holy semblance sign. May they appear before us day and night, without falling away. May we obtain, equally, the holy approach concentration and the holy absorption concentration in every detail without interruption. This is not all. We wish to practice the insight wisdom [that leads to] the holy knowledge of omniscience from the holy purification of precept to the holy knowledge of conformity to finish. May we be able to obtain the mindfulness, precepts, concentration, and wisdom of the Supreme Omniscient One and fix our mind on the knowledge of the three characteristics of existence so as to grasp clearly that material and mental phenomena are impermanent, suffering, and not subject to our control (*anattā*), and to feel that inner stirring [that incites] to release from the circle that is without end and without beginning. May we obtain the Paths, the Fruits, and *Nibbāna*, according to our wish, and bring about development and success in each of these things.

<!-- Page 145 -->

In another passage we read the following:

> Here also, I practice worship in the form of meditation practice in accordance with the teaching of the holy omniscient Gotama so as to grasp in the sphere of the virtues of the holy Buddha the five holy kinds of joy, the six holy pairs, the holy happiness, and holy approach concentration.

Compare the initial structure of the *Yogāvacara’s Manual*. Similar exercises to develop mastery to those found in that manual are also mentioned. Later we have the following:

> I request the meditation practice of the holy mindfulness of breathing to obtain the acquired sign and semblance signs in the sphere of mindfulness of breathing. May the holy Buddha be my refuge. May the holy Dhamma be my refuge. May from now on, the holy Noble monks, from the revered Mahā Aññāta Kauṇḍañña-thera, down to the conventional order of monks be my refuge. May the revered holy Teacher, who teaches the holy *kammaṭṭhāna* be my refuge. May the holy *kammaṭṭhāna* be my refuge.
>
> Behold! Here also, I practice worship in the form of meditation practice in accordance with the teaching of the holy omniscient Gotama in order that the acquired sign in the sphere of the holy mindfulness of breathing is born in me by virtue of the words that I pronounce: *arahaṃ sammāsambuddho bhagavā*! [three times]; *buddho*! [three times].

That this is not merely a ritual is clear from the following:

> I practice the preparatory stage, reciting: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 a hundred times, a thousand times.

Thus far the content of *The Laṅkā Path* is clearly related to the texts found in Ceylon. There follows, however, a long second section consisting of a kind of dialogue between the teacher and pupil. It has no parallel in the texts from Ceylon so far identified. It does not give a systematic account of the meditative practice envisaged, but there is much interesting information. Notably, the practice begins by reciting *arahaṃ* with the breathing until a white light appears. Amusingly, when initially asked, the pupil says that he cannot see anything, but he is told to persevere. Eventually a white light does arise and is taken behind the nose and the syllable NA appears. The meditator sees a path that descends to the back of the mouth. Now a blue light is seen, and the syllable MO appears. Then the path downward is perceived, and the meditator descends to the neck where a yellow light arises and the syllable BU appears. Next he descends to the chest where a red light arises and then the syllable DDHĀ. Continuing to the center of the body behind the navel, a white light arises and the syllable YA.

<!-- Page 146 -->

Subsequently a peg the size of a mustard seed is located in the navel area (or just above?) where all five lights arise. (The first is a pearly white, while the last is given variously as jasmine white or crystal.) This special location is assimilated to the seat of the Buddha under the Bodhi tree at enlightenment and is envisaged as the seat of the sphere of consciousness. Luminous spheres of the four elements are distinguished. Then there is reference to a crystal sphere identified with the Dhamma that appears a little above the navel. A link is made between breathing in and out and the location of the peg. Emphasis is placed upon the desirability of falling in love with the meditation practice.

At a later stage the practice seems to move from repetition of the three syllables of *arahaṃ* to reciting simply the syllable A and utilizing some type of more forceful and controlled breathing.

At intervals the text introduces various correspondences. The five syllables are linked not only to the various colors and elements but also to the letters of the alphabet and the parts of the body, as well as to the five Buddhas of the present eon (*bhaddakappa*). This is part of the more elaborate system found in the *Five-Branched Fig Tree* and elsewhere. The correspondences relate the letters of the alphabet to the different parts of the body and to the different syllables of the chants in honor of the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Sa*ṅ*gha. The heart syllables of the names of texts of the Pali Canon are similarly given correspondences of various kinds—for example, the heart syllables of the seven books of the *Abhidhamma-piṭaka* are seen as generating the stages of development of the embryo in the womb. All of this is done in a rather fluid way, as is typical of correspondence systems in magical lore and related spiritual systems. Various kinds of manipulation of numbers and letters seem to be involved.

The dialogue section of *The Laṅkā Path* also includes what is explicitly stated to be another exercise, based on external objects. This involves visualization to see a river and its banks. Then across the river the meditator sees Mount Sumeru and the Cūḷamaṇi *stūpa* on its summit. They are made to practice to distinguish a canoe or a bridge. They see themselves crossing as a monk and worshipping the *stūpa*. Then they return and recollect the experience so as not to forget it. Questioned, they indicate that they see the four kinds of relic deposited in the *stūpa*.

<!-- Page 147 -->

Bizot describes the continuation of this kind of visualization practice in Cambodia in the 1960s.[[24]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-379 "note") Following the massive destruction in Cambodia under the Khmer Rouge in the 1970s that entailed the deaths of many monks and the loss of libraries, there has been some revival of traditional forms of practice and it is likely that this form of meditation practice is, to some extent, continuing.[[25]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-380 "note")

## **The Fig Tree Allegory**

One theme that is associated with various practices is the story of the two children of the *citta*: Cittakumāra and Cittakumārī—the Prince and Princess of the Heart, if you like. Following the version still current in Cambodia, the two children engage in a quest to find the five-branched fig tree:

The tree grows as high as is *nibbāna*.

Its roots of crystal

number twenty-six.

Set with diamonds is the precious trunk

that unfolds beyond

the four continents.

The first branches,

like six marvelous stars,

subdivide into nine smaller branches

whose thick foliage

very rarely

withers.

The upper branches,

which number sixteen,

with leaves that are

close-set

and evergreen,

carry fruit that

last long before they fall.

The succeeding branches

—there are nine most beautiful

—are very thick [with foliage]

of leaves, some old, some new,

which, when their time has come,

fall one by one.

From the midst of the branching,

which separates many times

in countless forkings,

come forth four healing branches,

a permanent refuge

that protects all beings.

These are the four requisites (*paccaya*)

of humanity today,

the constant source of protection

that spreads good fortune,

happiness, and success.

They bear leaves, flowers, and fruit

that ripen and fall

in the network of the world,

and transform [on reaching] the ground

to bring about the birth of fig trees

that possess five branches.

An *indriya* [bird] keeps guard

upon each of these five branches

so as to devour human beings.

Such is the search for the crystal spheres, the fruits, and flowers of the fig tree.[[26]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-381 "note")

The details of this are not all clear and indeed seem to vary quite fluidly in different sources. The versions from Lanna in the north of Thailand and Laos seem to generally have only a single individual, the Princess of the Heart, engaged in the quest for the crystal spheres. In the newly discovered version from Ceylon, the Princess of the Heart appears even to suffer rape by Māra before finally gaining the precious gem(s) that take the place of the crystal spheres in the Sinhalese version.

<!-- Page 148 -->

But to show how these identifications operate, let me quote another of Bizot’s texts:

> ## ***The Two Children of the Heart***
>
> *Citta* is our heart; *cetasika* our phenomenal mind (*viññāṇa*). One is called Princess of the Heart, the other Prince of the Heart. These are the two Children of the Heart.
>
> ## ***The Land of the Rose-Apple Tree***
>
> It is located to the south of holy Mount Sumeru. Around Mount Sumeru is situated first Mount Giri, then Mount Vulture Peak, and lastly the seven ramparts of Mount Sevenfold Ring.
>
> ## ***The Fig Tree That Grows up in the Land of the Rose-Apple Tree***
>
> This fig tree has five branches and as many very fierce *indriya* birds to guard each branch. Four crystal spheres are placed within the figs. The person who wishes to take these crystal spheres must slay the five *indriya* birds. Then he will be able to take hold of them. In order to slay them, fashion the staff of a bow with the bone of a hare, the string with its hairs and the arrows with its vessels. When the *indriya* birds are dead, reflect in order to obtain the four holy crystal spheres. If one doesn’t come there, one should have oneself instructed by a person of wisdom.
>
> ## ***Mount Sumeru***
>
> In the five aggregates of our bodily form, our head is Mount Sumeru; our chest Mount Giri; our pelvis Mount Vulture Peak; the two knees, the two ankles, and the two soles the seven stages of Mount Sevenfold Ring, the ramparts of Mount Meru. The four lakes situated at the foot of Mount Sumeru are the four elements in our bodily form.
>
> ## ***The Fig Tree***
>
> The five aggregates of our form are called the fig tree. That which grows up in the land of the rose-apple tree is the two Children of the Heart who are reborn in the maternal womb.
>
> ## ***The Three Worlds***
>
> That is to say the three circles of rebirth—are possessions, wives, and children. This is what is called the three attachments in the circle of rebirths.
>
> ## ***The Fruits of the Fig Tree***
>
> The figs are our body. The four crystal spheres are the four elements. The holy place of work/path of practice (*Kammaṭṭhāna*) is found in our body. The person who does not venerate and does not practice this holy *Kammaṭṭhāna* does not distinguish good from ill and commits serious faults because he has contempt for his own body. The person who venerates and practices this holy *Kammaṭṭhāna* considers his own body as a place of establishing (*ṭṭhāna*) for which he dare not have contempt. Such a one will attain the paths and fruits that will lead him to heavenly good and to good of *nibbāna*.
>
> ## ***The Five Indriya Birds***
>
> The five *indriya* birds are called *cakkhindriya*—the creature in the eyes; *sotindriya*—the creature in the ears; *ghānindriya*—the creature in the nose; *jivhindriya*—the creature in the tongue; *kāyindriya*—the creature in the body. This is what is called the five *indriya* birds.
>
> To take a hare bone by way of bow staff is to use the heart in order to reflect. To take the hairs of the hare by way of string is to use discrimination in order to awaken the presence of remembrance. To take the vessels of the hare by way of arrow is to use wisdom in order to pierce through ill (*pāpa*).[[27]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-382 "note")

<!-- Page 149 -->

The meditations discussed here may have been part of the normal tradition of Buddhist meditation in Southeast Asia before the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. We will now move to the new forms of insight-oriented meditation, which have emerged mainly from Burma and spread widely around the world.

# 9. The Flourishing of Insight Meditation in Modern Times

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

In his 1990 doctoral thesis concerning meditation practice in Burma, Gustaaf Houtman writes, “Since the foundation of the first known meditation centre by the Min’gun’ Hsa-ya-daw in 1914, today there are at least two dozen distinct nationally renowned [*vipassanā* meditation] methods which operate many hundreds of centres, many thousands of teachers, and hundreds of thousands of practitioners.”[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-383 "note") It is important to note that this figure does not include many other distinct methods taught in single centers or temples. Nor does it include the practice of *samatha* meditation in various forms, which I will return to in the next chapter.

Insight meditation practice has in relatively recent years spread from Burma to other Southern Buddhist countries and even far outside the traditional environment of this form of Buddhism. Today centers and teachers for the practice of insight meditation are to be found in many countries in Asia, Australasia, Europe, and the Americas. Almost all of these derive ultimately from Burma, although they are not all of the same branch of Burmese meditation.

Until recently this outreach was largely dominated by two major teaching traditions: that of Mahāsi Sayadaw (1904–1982) and that of the pupils of U Ba Khin (1899–1971). Of the two, the tradition founded by Mahāsi Sayadaw is much more influential within Burma itself and probably somewhat more influential internationally. Its immediate origins as a major force lie within the governing circle in Burma in the period after independence in the 1950s. Prime Minister U Nu and his associates were engaged in sponsoring Buddhist activities of various kinds, notably the famous Sixth Council and the associated edition of the Buddhist Canon and its exegetical literature. This group was mostly themselves interested in meditation practice and decided to establish a very large meditation center near Rangoon. They then selected Mahāsi Sayadaw to teach at the center. He was already an established figure who had set up a number of smaller centers of his own, but the new location provided him with a platform for his teaching activities at a center to which foreigners coming to learn meditation in Burma were most often directed. The Mahāsi was not himself seen as a political figure; so the center survived and even flourished (after some vicissitudes) following the fall of U Nu and the eventual establishment of military rule under General Ne Win. It remains the place to which foreigners are most often encouraged to go. The main emphasis of the Mahāsi approach for beginners is on body mindfulness and a very intensive course of practice. Mahāsi Sayadaw was a pupil of Mingun Sayadaw (1869–1954), who set up the first modern-style meditation center in Burma. We shall return to him shortly.

<!-- Page 150 -->

Our other major figure is U Ba Khin (1899–1971). He was a layperson and taught mainly by a layperson, who was himself a disciple of Ledi Sayadaw (1846–1923)—an important figure in the early development of insight meditation. U Ba Khin was one of the first Burmese to be employed in the Accountancy Office of the Burmese colonial government in the 1930s and gradually rose through the ranks there. He was director of the Accounts and Auditors Department during the Japanese occupation of Burma in World War Two.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-384 "note") When after the Burma Campaign the British recovered control of Burma in the closing stages of World War Two, he became deputy accountant general. After independence in 1948 he became the first accountant general of the Union of Burma. While not a member of the political group associated with U Nu, he would have been well known to them and at times taught meditation to U Nu.

U Ba Khin initially taught in his government department, but eventually established his own center in Rangoon. After his death, that center was continued by his Burmese disciples. Before his death, however, U Ba Khin had taught a number of foreign pupils and encouraged them to teach in their own countries. Probably the most famous of these is S. N. Goenka, who was successful in setting up numerous centers, especially in South Asia but also around the world. Western pupils of U Ba Khin included Robert Hover, John Earl Coleman, Ruth Denison, and others. Some of these have gone on to establish centers stemming originally from this tradition. They vary somewhat as to how far they have retained a close connection with the mother center in Burma. In some cases they have developed rather independently.

## **The Historical Roots of Insight Meditation**

There has been some discussion among scholars as to exactly how old the insight meditation tradition is. We should distinguish carefully here what we mean by meditation and who exactly we are referring to. First of all, it is necessary to distinguish between monks and laypeople. It is often claimed that meditation among laypeople in what used to be called Ceylon and is now called Sri Lanka is a relatively new phenomenon of the postwar period. Indeed, meditation centers serving middle-class lay practitioners are a recent development. Relatively few village people seem to take up meditation before they reach a more advanced age. It may however be a mistake to assume that this custom is merely formalistic or ineffective.

<!-- Page 151 -->

Some also claim that lay practice in youth in Southeast Asia is a recent phenomenon, although it is certainly considerably older than in Ceylon.
