I have doubts about this, however, as the strong tradition of spending a period in the *saṅgha* will have led to a certain number of individuals continuing to meditate after disrobing. There is no doubt that the vast majority of meditators would have been monks before relatively recent times. In any case, lay meditation seems to have been a normative part of the *Porāṇa* tradition discussed in the last chapter.

For monks, there has always been some practice of meditation, at least in Southeast Asia. In Sri Lanka it is usually held that relatively few monks meditate. This seems to me to be a slight overstatement of the case and depends very much on what one means by “meditation.” Nevertheless, for Ceylon the impression is that the meditation tradition was largely moribund at some point in the past and has been in part reintroduced from elsewhere. The same claim that few monks meditate is also made for Burma and Thailand. Here I find it flatly unbelievable. My own experience is that meditation is widely practiced and well known in the *saṅgha*, although certainly not universal. In his thesis, Houtman expresses some surprise at Spiro’s relative unawareness of the meditation movement.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-385 "note") I think all this tells us is that if an anthropologist is not a meditator and is plainly uninterested in meditation, most meditators will say little on the subject to him.

Above all, it is essential to attempt to ask questions in terms that are actually meaningful to the meditation tradition. This is where what we actually mean by “meditation” becomes crucial. In modern English usage, as was suggested in chapter 3, the word “meditation” seems to refer to methods or techniques of repetitive exercise for developing some kind of mental state or understanding. As we have seen, this is very far from covering the full range of meaning of Buddhist *bhāvanā*. In other words, such monastic activities as studying or teaching the Dhamma as well as chanting *sutta*s or repetition of songs, chants, and verses (*gāthā*) may equally be forms of *bhāvanā*. This is certainly the position of the commentaries (*aṭṭhakathā*) and was probably that of traditional Theravāda Buddhism. Many Asian meditators today would still have some such understanding. In this view of the matter, *bhāvanā* is very widely practiced indeed, both by virtually all monks and by most of the more committed laity.

<!-- Page 152 -->

For some varieties of insight meditation, such activities as chanting and repetition of traditional formulas are either not meditation at all or only an inferior form of meditation and that only when they are in a very orthodox form. Note then that for such monks or lay followers, there is relatively little meditation in present-day Buddhism—by definition. There is also a tendency in some quarters to suppose that meditation practice requires intensive practice over long periods. My own understanding, however, is that there is a great deal of individual variation. What matters is not how much you do, but how well you do it. So for some people, half an hour a day may be quite sufficient to develop their practice to a significant degree. For others, it can only be developed in an intensive context that puts aside all other activities. For still others, what is needed is some combination of the two. All this means that it is unwise to make hasty judgments as to whether monks meditate or not.

Early reports by travelers of various kinds refer to the practice of “recitation” both by monks and laypeople. This tended to be understood in Christian terms as some kind of devotional prayer, and indeed in Buddhism the line between devotion and meditation can sometimes be very fine. Probably, however, the best way of understanding the information we have is to suppose that *Porāṇa* meditation traditions were widely practiced at some point in the past, both by monks and laypeople. More recently, monastic reforms have tended to stress the importance of study and scholarship with some consequential diminution of interest in meditation in the monastic traditions.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-386 "note") In modernizing circles, some meditative traditions were perhaps seen as populist and superstitious and “real” meditation came to be seen as something for monks. However, we should not assume that this was always the case. Indeed, general religious studies would lead us to expect that the level of various kinds of religious practice will vary over time. We might then suppose that in the past there would have been periods of widespread meditative activity interspersed with periods when such activity was more of a minority interest.

<!-- Page 153 -->

Looking at the development of the meditation movement that grew up over the course of the twentieth century, we find that meditation centers as opposed to monasteries were a new development. The first of these was founded by Mahāsi Sayadaw’s teacher, Mingun Sayadaw, in 1911. Many others followed. This signaled a wider extension of insight meditation practice among lay followers that gradually increased over time. However, this was not the origin of lay practice of insight meditation. That can certainly be traced back to Ledi Sayadaw from the latter part of the nineteenth century. All of these monks were extremely knowledgeable in scriptural studies and their meditative teachings were firmly based on the *abhidhamma* and the Pali commentaries. Ledi Sayadaw was originally linked to the circles of distinguished monks associated with court circles in Mandalay. After the conquest of Upper Burma by the British in 1885, however, he founded a forest monastery and began to emphasize the importance of insight meditation for laypeople.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-387 "note")

As we have seen, insight as such is an integral part of Buddhist meditation from an early date. It is unclear whether the option of insight meditation without much prior practice of calm meditation is envisaged in the canonical texts. It certainly is acknowledged in the commentarial writings of the school of Buddhaghosa in the fourth or fifth century c.e., but there it seems to be a less preferred, but possible, alternative. It is also unclear whether such a practice precedes the time of Ledi Sayadaw. More probably the practice of insight meditation as a separate method is a revival based at least in part upon the texts. It can be seen as a Buddhist response to the problems posed by foreign rule and modernization.

There is some evidence for the practice of meditation in Burma before Ledi Sayadaw’s time.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-388 "note") A monk named Waya-zaw-ta started a meditation movement that thrived in the 1720s and ‘30s, but it was suppressed after he died. Although referred to as *vipassanā*, it is not clear whether it was insight meditation in the sense with which we are concerned here. In 1754, Taung-lei-lon Sayadaw wrote the earliest example we have of a vernacular meditation manual. What is certain is that there was a significant revival of interest in meditation during the reign of King Mindon (r. 1853–1878) both in court circles and with some forest-dwelling monks. It is not clear whether any of this was insight meditation as such.

<!-- Page 154 -->

## **Insight Meditation in the Twentieth Century**

### ***Mahāsi Sayadaw***

The school of Mahāsi Sayadaw teaches one of the primary and most influential methods of insight meditation today. In Burma alone there were 332 meditation centers belonging to this tradition in 1994.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-389 "note")

The method of practice of this school is highly intensive, involving the maintenance of mindfulness and clear comprehension over long periods of time, ideally with very little sleep—eighteen to twenty hours of continuous meditation is normally recommended with just four hours of sleep. Usually sitting meditation and walking practice are alternated. The walking practice involves the systematic breaking down into named stages of the process of movement. Initially, each of these stages is noted mentally and at the same time all external distractions or internal wanderings of the mind are similarly noted. It is recommended that the walking practice (and any other necessary activities) should be carried out as slowly as possible.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-390 "note") This slowing down is sometimes criticized by devotees of other schools of insight and does not seem to be authorized in the older texts, although the differentiation of movement into stages is found. The objective is to establish mindfulness, initially concentrating on the first foundation of mindfulness—mindfulness of the body. This is sometimes taught by focusing on the contact of the breath at the nose. That was the method of the Mahāsi’s teacher and is still sometimes used, but the most distinctive element in the practice of this school is undoubtedly its technique of watching the rise and fall of the abdomen. This was an innovation introduced by the Mahāsi himself and has sometimes met criticism. Nonetheless it is now widely practiced. Here too mental noting is employed: “rising,” “falling.” It is usually stressed that the noting should always be mental, never out loud.

Mahāsi Sayadaw emphasizes that all that is required to practice insight meditation is the weakest of the three degrees of concentration—that is, *khaṇikasamādhi*, or momentary concentration. It is doubtful how far this position is supported by the commentarial literature, which seems more often to envisage approach meditation for insight and full absorption to be developed before or at least simultaneously with the path. In practice, however, this does not seem to make all that much difference to many of the methods taught.

<!-- Page 155 -->

Mahāsi Sayadaw summarizes the basic method as follows:[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-391 "note")

> It has been emphasized during this brief outline of the training that you must contemplate on each mental occurrence good or bad, on each bodily movement large or small, on every sensation [bodily or mental feeling] pleasant or unpleasant, and so on. If, during the course of training, occasions arise when there is nothing special to contemplate upon, be fully occupied with attention to the rising and falling of the abdomen. When you have to attend to any kind of activity that necessitates walking, then, in complete awareness, each step should be briefly noted as walking, walking, or left, right. But when you are taking a walking exercise contemplate each step in three sections, up, forward, down. The student who thus dedicates himself to the training during day and night, will be able in not too long a time to develop concentration to the initial stage of the fourth degree of Insight … and onward to higher stages of Insight Meditation.

Thus far the approach does not seem out of line with the older literature. What is more surprising is the identification in this school of a *vipassanā jhāna*. The Mahāsi considers a stage where constant noting is successfully established as equivalent to the first *jhāna*. He goes on to say,[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-392 "note")

> As the meditator progresses, the actions and movements will present themselves spontaneously for noting. The meditator has passed the stage where he has to make an effort to note them and has reached the stage of insight called *udayabbayañāṇa.* The initial application *(vitakka)* and sustained application *(vicāra)* are absent, and joy *(pīti)* and happiness *(sukha)* abound with a further strengthening of concentration. Therefore the earlier part of this *udayabbayañāṇa* stage of insight is equivalent to the second *jhāna.*

He goes on to suggest that the advanced stage of rise and fall knowledge—where happiness and concentration become stronger—is equivalent to the third *jhāna*, and the further stages of insight knowledge—where equanimity strengthens—are equivalent to the fourth *jhāna*. This suggestion is not made in the older literature. I do not know whether it originates with the Mahāsi himself, but it is in part surprising. The general parallel is clear, but the suggestion that *vitakka* and *vicãra* are absent in rise and fall knowledge does not seem to accord with the earlier literature for which these two mental factors accompany all consciousnesses with faith or wisdom. Perhaps, however, the Mahāsi means that they do not occur in as strong a form as before.

<!-- Page 156 -->

As we have seen, this and many other approaches to insight meditation emphasize the dropping of all discursive and conceptual activity in favor of a simple, non-judgmental observation. The aim is for the observation and recognition of mental and physical phenomena to lead naturally to the ability to perceive and be continuously mindful of a process of constant change. This kind of direct awareness is seen as a type of knowledge that is quite distinct from discursive cognition.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-393 "note") Often the knowledge that arises from insight is contrasted with the knowledge that comes from calm practice. The latter is understood as based upon names and conventions, whereas the knowledge that comes from insight is understood to be a direct apprehension of how things really are.

Now let us look at some of the better-known figures and traditions in Burma and Thailand and some of their salient variations.

### ***Sunlun Sayadaw***

Although the Mahāsi approach may appear quite strict, some other Burmese approaches may seem even stricter. By way of contrast, we can look at the method of Sunlun Sayadaw (1878–1952). Whereas the Mahāsi was one of the most distinguished scholar monks in Burma, Sunlun Sayadaw did not have much aptitude for scholarship and gave up a desk job to work in the fields and raise a family. He took up meditation as a layperson, only ordaining at the age of forty-four. However, he was soon widely regarded as an *arahat*. In 1976, 143 meditation centers in Burma were following his tradition. His teachings stressed the value of rigorousness. Whereas in the Mahāsi method, sittings of up to an hour alternate with periods of walking meditation and the sitting posture should be comfortable and can even be on a chair, for Sunlun Sayadaw the posture should be cross-legged and sitting should be maintained for many hours without moving.

This contrast is partly because although the Sunlun method starts by observing the contact of the breath at the nose, it soon turns to focus on awareness of the three kinds of feeling: pleasant, painful, and neutral. The aim is to sit through painful feeling however long it takes and however painful it is. As Sunlun Sayadaw is supposed to have said, “If cramped don’t move, if itchy don’t scratch, if fatigued don’t rest.”[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-394 "note") A cross-legged posture with both legs flat on the ground tends to be used and the right hand is clasped in the left hand to make it easier to apply forceful effort. An unusual feature of this approach is the use of a forceful, rough type of breathing rather than simple observation as is more common in insight meditation. In theoretical terms we can say that the focus here is on the second foundation of mindfulness: mindfulness of feeling, together with the application of vigorous effort.

<!-- Page 157 -->

### ***Mohnyin Sayadaw***

The great majority of insight meditation teachers in the Southern Buddhist countries have some kind of base in the theoretical teachings of the *abhidhamma*, usually founded especially on the manual of Anuruddha, written around the eleventh century—the *Abhidhammatthasaṅgaha*, known in its English translation as *A Comprehensive Manual of Abhidhamma*.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-395 "note") Some, especially in Burma, would have a broader knowledge based on a wider range of *abhidhamma* literature, but that would not usually be taught to beginners. There are some schools of insight meditation, however, that do stress a foundation in *abhidhamma* theory as a necessary beginning of practice, leading into meditation as such. Such schools can be seen as working from the fourth foundation of mindfulness—mindfulness of the Dhamma and *dhamma*s, or awareness of how the material and the mental function together in accordance with law.

An example of this approach is in the teachings of Mohnyin Sayadaw (1873–1964). His teachings can be seen as developing the teachings of Ledi Sayadaw in a particular direction. Only after acquiring a knowledge of basic *abhidhamma* ideas would a meditator start to practice mindfulness. Particular stress would then be laid on observing postures by which is meant the four modes of activity: walking, standing, sitting, and lying down. Mindfulness should be developed whether moving slowly or rapidly and one should be equally aware of mental phenomena. In effect, the approach stresses mindfulness in all activities rather than during limited or defined periods.

### ***Ajahn Naeb***

Similar methods seem to have been introduced into Thailand before World War Two. One teacher who did so was Ajahn Naeb (1897–1983).[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-396 "note") She was a Thai nun who founded a number of meditation centers. Here too the stress is on a prior understanding of *abhidhamma* teachings, followed by “natural practice” focusing initially on the four postures and then becoming aware also of mental phenomena. This is because artificially slowing down or breaking up the process of movement into stages or spreading the sense of impermanence through the body is considered to be “meditating with desire,” something that will not give good results. It is also stressed that mindfulness should be accompanied by clear comprehension (*sampajañña*)—too much mindfulness can be unhelpful. The use of *abhidhamma* in this school amounts to a process of re-educating the understanding by means of a kind of systematic questioning. One could compare this to some forms of philosophical dialectic, although adherents would certainly reject the comparison.

<!-- Page 158 -->

### ***Mogok Sayadaw***

Another development originating from the teachings of Ledi Sayadaw is the teaching of Mogok Sayadaw (1899–1962). This also stresses a prior knowledge of theory, but in this case the theory in question is the lists connected with the senses and their objects, the five aggregates, and above all dependent origination. So this is mainly utilizing teachings from *suttanta* rather than *abhidhamma* teachings. These are used as a basis to develop the second and third foundations of mindfulness—mindfulness of feeling and especially mindfulness of the mental state (*citta*). These are usually practiced after some basic *samatha* in the form of mindfulness of breathing, ideally to the stage of approach.

### ***U Ba Khin***

U Ba Khin taught a method based upon initial practice of breathing mindfulness followed by a technique that he seems to have introduced, sometimes referred to as “the *anicca* sweep.” Like other teachers in the lineage of Ledi Sayadaw, he regarded a basic level of concentration and some theoretical knowledge as desirable prerequisites. He writes,

> Before entering upon the practice of *Vipassanā* Meditation, that is, after *Samādhi* has been developed to a proper level, a student should acquaint himself with the theoretical knowledge of mental and material properties, i.e. of *Rūpa* and *Nāma*.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-397 "note")

He himself taught both breathing mindfulness and sometimes other methods of preliminary concentration, but a typical meditation course in this tradition usually begins with breathing mindfulness as a concentration practice and after a while turns to activate awareness of impermanence throughout the body by going through the body from head to foot, noting sensations.

Pupils presenting this method outside Burma seem to lay much less stress on the theoretical knowledge, but this may be an illusion. It is perhaps more of a teaching strategy with the aim of gradually introducing the theoretical foundations over time. In the case of S. N. Goenka, it is generally presented as a universal and non-denominational practice and specifically Buddhist elements are relatively low-key but nonetheless present.

### ***The Outreach of Insight Meditation***

<!-- Page 159 -->

As has been suggested, the six examples given here are simply a selection from a much wider variety of approaches, especially in the monastic *saṅgha*. To this can be added the teachings of numerous individuals and groups outside the traditional Southern Buddhist countries who have set up or branched out into their own approach. Some of these teach exclusively or mainly insight meditation in some form. Others are more eclectic. It would be quite impossible to examine all this variety without devoting a whole volume to the subject, but there are a few specific tendencies worth noting here.

One such tendency is a move to combine meditation with some form of psychotherapy. This seems to be particularly the case in the United States but is also found elsewhere. Another would be the adoption by some Mahāyāna teachers of methods derived, at least in part, from Theravāda sources. An example of this would be Thích Nhát Hạnh*.* That is perhaps not entirely surprising in a teacher from Vietnam, since that country is the historic frontier between Southern Buddhism and Eastern Buddhism. But we see it also in some other Mahāyāna teachers, including those from Northern Buddhist traditions. This can be a matter of substituting the familiar Theravāda practices for other Śrāvakayāna methods from their own background. Equally it can be part of a wish to bring together different Buddhist traditions in a new world situation. Many western Buddhists are also attracted to the idea of a unified Buddhism as a kind of world movement.

A striking example of the tendency to adapt ideas originating from insight meditation and some forms of Zen practice is the Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction program developed by Jon Kabat-Zinn. This has led to methods that have been adopted by something like two hundred medical centers. These ideas have been further developed by Mark Williams and colleagues in Oxford, where there is a Mindfulness Centre and a masters degree program within the Department of Psychiatry in Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-398 "note")

From this survey we can see how the spread of the modern insight tradition in the twentieth century was largely the work of the pupils of two specific teachers, Mahāsi Sayadaw and U Ba Khin. The move to encourage insight meditation practice among the laity seems to originate with Ledi Sayadaw after the end of the Burmese kingdom. Similarly, the move to adopt insight practice after only a minimal amount of *samatha* comes to the fore at this time and may not have much prior history. The movement flourished in the twentieth century with a considerable variety of different approaches in the Buddhist countries, many of which remain little known.

<!-- Page 160 -->

# 10. The Varieties of *Samatha* and *Samatha*-*Vipassanā* Today

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

As we have seen, when we look at the classical literature of Theravāda we can form a fairly precise understanding of what is meant by *samatha*. The same is not quite true for the usage in the Southern Buddhist countries today in actual practice. In theory, the meaning in the Pali texts is shared by all, but the usage I have adopted here is more normal in Thailand and surrounding areas. The Burmese tend to use the term slightly differently in actual practice. There, as we have seen, many schools of insight meditation are current, but in addition to that, there is a great deal of concentration-oriented meditation. Much of the latter seems to be associated either with esotericism of some kind or with the development of psychic powers and is often especially linked to developing mental contact with some kind of non-human being.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-399 "note") There are a large number of occult associations with varying ideas and practices. In essence, *samatha* practice—whether one of the classical *kammaṭṭhāna*s, mantric practice, or such things as alchemy—is seen as facilitating almost any kind of magical practice: constructing magic diagrams (yantras), healing, long life, fortune-telling, communicating with non-human beings, magical protection, even magical harming. In this understanding, a spell needs concentration to work.

All of these things tend to be associated with *samatha* meditation in the popular mind and are often looked down upon by insight meditators, especially those with strong reformist or modernist orientations. In themselves, however, they are not *samatha* meditation in the sense meant here and are in any case not well known to Western investigators. In the latter part of the Ne Win era, some forms of *samatha* were actively suppressed. Houtman writes about this in his 1990 thesis:

> While the military Government tolerated WM “Wipathana Meditation,” [a term for *vipassanā* meditation] practice … it was not pleased with the unorthodox concentration meditator whose aspirations to power introduce an element of political instability. Many concentration meditation practitioners, both laymen and monks, have been arrested over the last few years, and the powers derived from concentration meditation—such as those of flight and transformation—are censored from Burmese films. This policy extends to stories about ghosts, witchcraft and thrillers, which are thought to confuse the general public, along with stories about aspirant weik-za.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-400 "note")

<!-- Page 161 -->

This extended to restrictions on foreigners:

> Apart from people entering the country in the capacity of government diplomats, or aid officials, only WM allows foreigners to get into Burma for a period of more than the one week standard tourist visa. Indeed, I was allowed to visit any WM centre I chose, but not to visit areas famous for the practice of unorthodox concentration.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-401 "note")

We may note, however, that the military are not always a united force in this and other regards. Some of the military have developed strong connections with particular concentration teachers.

How far all of this represents something that is outside the Buddhist path as such has been and is debated. It is not clear whether some of the concentration traditions in Burma represent something like a survival of traditions related to those we saw in chapter 8, influenced too, perhaps, by the new insight movements. In any case, there are certainly teachers of much more textually based approaches. A good example is Pa Auk Sayadaw (1934–).

### ***Pa Auk Tawya Sayadaw***

The approach of this teacher has become influential outside Burma. We are told that in 2007 there were more than 130 foreign monks, nuns, and lay practitioners residing at Pa Auk Forest Monastery, the Sayadaw’s main center in Moulmein in the Mon state in southeastern Burma. Pa Auk Sayadaw has personally led retreats in a number of countries, including the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Sri Lanka, South Korea, China, and throughout the Chinese diaspora in Asia. So his teachings are becoming widely known. I do not think the practice of this school would be referred to as *samatha* meditation in the Burmese context. This is partly because the Sayadaw also teaches the bare insight method to those who prefer that. Nevertheless, the preferred approach is to develop at least the four *jhāna*s and possibly also formless meditation before developing insight.

Normally, those meditating with the Sayadaw begin either with breathing mindfulness or with meditation on the four elements in the body, but with mastery of those, they would move on to various other *kammaṭṭhāna*s. As the Sayadaw puts it,

> There are forty subjects of Samatha meditation, and a person can develop any of these to attain concentration.
>
> For those who cannot decide which meditation subject to choose they should start with mindfulness-of-breathing. Most people are successful in meditation by using either mindfulness-of-breathing or the four-elements meditation.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-402 "note")

<!-- Page 162 -->

The understanding here is that if someone has mastery of one *samatha* meditation subject, it is rather easy to move on to another. His followers are encouraged to develop skill across a wide range of *kammaṭṭhāna*s before turning to insight practice. The Sayadaw emphasizes that *samatha* meditation is a “strong support” cause (*upanissayapaccaya*), a stimulus condition for the development of insight.[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-403 "note") Pa Auk Sayadaw began his training in theory studies before turning to meditation under various teachers and then meditating in the forest for a while. So, not surprisingly, his approach is well grounded in *abhidhamma* theory. Indeed he has written a 3,600-page book in Burmese that demonstrates his strong rootedness in *abhidhamma* theory.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-404 "note")

## **Meditation in Sri Lanka**

Sri Lanka too saw a revival of interest in meditation in the 1950s, leading to an increased interest in meditation both in the *saṅgha* and later extending to the laity. A number of forest centers were set up around this time, although a few are older.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-405 "note") Until recently, the lay-oriented centers established then were largely focused on insight meditation, but many forest centers (mainly but not exclusively visited by monks) do not practice exclusively insight meditation. In particular, some of these centers teach *kasiṇabhāvanā*—meditation on colors and the qualities of the four elements.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-406 "note")

Widespread among individual monks are two practices: the development of loving-kindness (to oneself and usually to all sentient beings) and mindfulness of in-and-out breathing.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-407 "note") The first of these is strictly a form of *samatha*, or calm meditation, although it is not unusual for it to be practiced in conjunction with insight meditation or as a balancing adjunct to other methods. Equally it may be (and often is) adopted as the main form of meditation. As to the second, many different techniques for working with the breath are current, but breathing mindfulness differs crucially from the other methods in that it can be used to develop insight or calm or both together. All these kinds of practice, as found in Sri Lanka today, seem to be partly individual creations from the literature and partly something transmitted through the network of individual connections within the Buddhist *saṅgha*. It is likely, however, that there was some continuity from earlier meditation practice at the time of the formation of the Siam and later the Rāmañña fraternities.

<!-- Page 163 -->

## **Meditation in the Mahānikāya**

Turning now to Thailand, which is certainly the major center for *samatha* meditation of various kinds, we can divide the meditation methods in accordance with the two main fraternities (*nikāya*) of the Thai *saṅgha*: the Mahānikāya—which is really a collection of various originally diverse local traditions that have been grouped together and to some extent organized and centralized from Bangkok—and the Dhammayuttika (Thai: Thammayut) *nikāya*.

Currently, insight meditation plays an important role in the Mahānikāya, but that is a relatively recent development. Influenced by developments in Burma under U Nu, in the 1950s Phimontam, who was then the abbot of the leading Mahānikāya monastery and Sa*ṅ*gha Minister of the Interior, promoted the introduction of insight meditation in the Mahāsi tradition. Believing that the introduction of monastic examinations had had a harmful effect on the practice of meditation among study monks, he promoted as abbots only those who had attended *vipassanā* courses. This resulted in the closure of many traditional centers for various kinds of *samatha* meditation. His policies produced a reaction and he was imprisoned as a supposed communist for a while, eventually recovering his position. Since that time, a modified version of the Mahāsi system has remained very influential in many Mahānikāya monasteries. As was mentioned in the last chapter, other schools of insight meditation also exist in the Mahānikāya, such as that of Ajahn Naeb (discussed in chapter 9) and Ajahn Dhammadharo. Yet *samatha* of various kinds has remained widely practiced.

### ***Sot Chandassaro, Abbot of Wat Paknam***

A major influence in the spread of meditation in Bangkok was the abbot of Wat Paknam, Sot Chandassaro (1885–1959), usually known by his royal title, Mongkol Thepmuni. He had pioneered the teaching of meditation both to study monks and laypeople in the Bangkok area even before Phimontam’s introduction of insight meditation. The method that he taught has been carried on and developed by his monk successors, so that it is now taught in various other locations. After his death, one of his pupils, a nun named Khun Yāy, continued the method and taught it to a group of disciples who went on to become monks and found a new monastery—Wat Dhammakāya near Don Muang Airport. This monastery continued to teach a version of the same method and also pioneered a number of innovative ways of presenting Buddhism that had considerable success, especially among young university students. The movement, although sometimes controversial, has prospered and claims branches in some fourteen countries worldwide.

<!-- Page 164 -->

The method taught by the abbot of Wat Paknam is distinctive. It starts with concentrating at the nostril to generate a bright clear sphere (first position) and then moves that sphere through various centers in the body. This is accompanied by the mantra *sammā arahaṃ*. Eventually the focus is placed in a sphere in the seventh position at the center of the body, two fingers breadth above the navel. Concentrating within that sphere generates a sphere containing spheres of the elements. Thereafter concentrating at the center of each successive sphere will produce a more subtle sphere and then eventually various kinds of subtle body: human, divine, belonging to the *brahma* level, and then a series of what are called *dhamma* bodies (*dhammakāya*s). In each case, one concentrates on a sphere at the center of the new bodily form to generate a still more subtle form. From the first *dhammakāya* form on is understood to be *vipassanā*.

It will be quite obvious that there is some connection between this practice and the *Porāṇa* meditation practices referred to in chapter 8. In the literature from Wat Paknam, the abbot is presented as having found a long lost path to *arahat*ship. However, it has become clear over the last decade that in his youth the abbot had studied with various teachers and constructed his system in part from earlier practices. Referring to earlier writings in Thai, the 1999 thesis of Mettanando Bhikkhu identified a major source for part of his methods in the meditation teachings of Suk Kai Theuan (1733–1822).[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-408 "note")

### ***Suk Kai Theuan***

Suk was initially invited from a monastery in Ayutthaya to teach meditation in Bangkok by the first king of the present Chakri dynasty, Rāma I, and was eventually appointed to the position of Sa*ṅ*gharāja by the next king, Rāma II. His influence and prestige were obviously very great at this time, and he probably represents an influential meditation tradition from the late Ayutthaya period. King Mongkut (Rāma IV) later restored the *stūpas* at Wat Ratchasittharam as a memorial to the fact that “two kings had studied in the same monastery school under the Supreme Patriarch (Suk) when he was yet still the meditation master Phra Yannasangwon residing at Wat Ratchasittharam.”[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-409 "note")

<!-- Page 165 -->

Mettanando and also Catherine Newell, in her 2008 School of Oriental and African Studies thesis, give detailed information on this system. The latter recounts how, during fieldwork in 2005–6, she visited Wat Ratchasittharam and found that Suk’s method is still being taught in that monastery.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-410 "note") The texts associated with this method make it very clear that it is closely related to the *Porāṇa* tradition discussed in chapter 8. Mettanando indicates that the manuscript used by Suk contained, for example,

> a description of the five grades of joy (*pīti*) identified with the five cosmic Buddhas of the eon, the five physical elements, i.e., earth, fire, water, wind and ether, each of which is directly related to the object of perception, the five senses and the five Khmer letters of the sacred mantra “namo buddhāya.”[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-411 "note")

Particularly notable is the system of the nine bases taught by Sot Candassaro. This has obvious similarities with the Wat Paknam method, although there are major differences. In particular, Suk’s method involves ascending upward through the nine bases rather than descending, and there are also many instructions for using the centers in conjunction with healing.

### ***Ajahn Buddhadāsa***

In some ways at the opposite extreme from this kind of traditional practice lies the approach of Ajahn Buddhadāsa (1906–1993). Buddhadāsa Bhikkhu is a very well-known figure in twentieth-century Thai Buddhism, and there is a considerable literature about his life and ideas.[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-412 "note") These focus especially upon his modernism. He rejected much traditional ritual and accepted some of the results of historical scholarship, rejecting, for example, the authenticity of the *abhidhamma* literature as the word of the Buddha and seeing its influence as often rather harmful. In his teaching he also had a tendency at times toward a kind of universalism and ultimatism, claiming that at the highest level there is no such thing as “religion.” We can note also that he was to some extent influenced by ideas and art deriving from Zen Buddhism and certainly utilized that in his teaching.

<!-- Page 166 -->

Buddhadāsa employs a rather fluid way of presenting Buddhist teachings, employing metaphorical interpretations of much Buddhist teaching and even sometimes of non-Buddhist teachings. In this regard his approach can be seen as, in part, concerned with offering a less rigid and more open approach to Buddhist ideas. It is not entirely easy to classify his teaching of meditation in terms of calm and insight. Indeed, he tends to stress the need to combine the two:

> *Samatha-vipassanā* is one thing, not two separate things. If they were two things, we would have to do two things and that would be too slow. When tranquility and insight are united as one thing, there is only a single thing to do. Both *samatha* and *vipassanā* are developed at one and the same time.[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-413 "note")

A little later on, he writes this:

> We can describe this as simultaneously seeing with tranquility (*samatha*), seeing as object and fixing the mind upon it, and seeing with insight (*vipassanā*), seeing the characteristic, conditions, and truth of the thing.

Despite this emphasis on the unity of the two, we can say that his approach emerges from the Thai tradition of *samatha* practice and is distinct from the kinds of insight meditation that were discussed in the last chapter. When it comes to the actual teaching of meditation, his main method was the practice of breathing mindfulness, following the sixteen stages.

His position is made quite clear when he declares this concerning breathing mindfulness:

> Altogether it contains sixteen steps or objects which are to be practised. It seems, however, that some people do not like this and complain that it is too much for them. Regarding this point, I insist that the Buddha never taught anything more than necessary or less than complete. That is, this 16-Step *samādhibhāvanā* [concentration practice] is neither too much nor too little. If you are patient enough to do all sixteen steps, you will have the complete system. If you are unable to do it, there is still a condensed version which is adequate for lazy people.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-414 "note")

By a condensed version he means one can practice only the first and last tetrads of breathing mindfulness, in effect omitting serious development of the *jhāna*s. So for him, the bare insight method is a possible but inferior method of practice.

<!-- Page 167 -->

### ***Ajahn Chah***

Among the Thai schools, the most well known to Western practitioners is certainly the *samādhi* tradition of northeast Thailand. This approach, also referred to as the Forest Tradition, is particularly current in the Dhammayuttika Nikāya. I will turn to that shortly.[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-415 "note") Its origins lie in the earlier traditions of wandering monks that probably predate the formation of the Dhammayuttika fraternity, and there remains a significant tradition of such practice in the Mahānikāya.

The most renowned teacher of that ilk is probably Ajahn Chah (1918–1992). Ajahn Chah’s disciples today claim more than three hundred branch monasteries in Thailand and around the world.[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-416 "note") His approach is best described as the inculcation of a lifestyle both by example and by direct teaching. He did not insist on his disciples adopting his own meditative practice; some well-known members of his lineage are insight practitioners (such as Ajahn Sumedho). The basic method taught at Ajahn Chah’s monastery was mindfulness of breathing. He states,

> In this monastery we concentrate our attention on the tip of the nose and develop awareness of the in-breath and out-breath with the mantra word “Buddho.” If the meditator wishes to use another word, or simply be mindful of the air moving in and out, this is also fine.[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-417 "note")

The approach is basically to see morality, concentration, and wisdom as an integrated practice of the eightfold path. So both calm and insight are valued. Ajahn Chah does not regard absorption as necessary in the beginning, but recognizes its value for the more experienced meditator who will not get attached to concentration experiences. He teaches the development of insight partly through the maintenance of mindfulness both in ordinary life and in formal practice, but mainly through perceiving the five aggregates as impermanent, suffering, and no-self.

The flavor of his teachings is simple and down to earth. They are not based on the study of theory and indeed Ajahn Chah discouraged this somewhat in the Thai context. He sometimes emphasizes that the way in which things are actually experienced is not the same as the descriptions in the books. His collected talks evidence a rather broad knowledge of actual practice.

<!-- Page 168 -->

## ***Samatha* Meditation in the Dhammayuttika Nikāya**

Although I refer to the meditative traditions in the smaller but influential Dhammayuttika fraternity as *samatha* meditation in opposition to insight meditation, this is not terminology that is especially used by them. Insight meditation traditions deriving from Burma are not found among them, but they do combine insight with the practice of concentration in various ways. In general, we can distinguish two main approaches to meditation among them. The first is closely related to study and the modernist ideas of this fraternity, initially founded by King Mongkut during his earlier life as a monk, but only established as a separate fraternity in the reign of his son King Chulalongkorn (r. 1868–1910). This approach to meditation is based especially at the head temple of this fraternity in Bangkok, Wat Boworn, and perhaps goes back in part to Mongkut himself. As an illustration of this method, we can consider the Saṅgharāja of Thailand and former abbot of Wat Boworn, Somdet Phra Ñāṇasaṃvara (1913–2013).

### ***Somdet Phra Ñāṇasaṃvara***

Ñāṇasaṃvara did not present himself as primarily a meditation teacher, but he taught meditation classes (especially for foreigners) at Wat Boworn and produced booklets on the subject. I believe he is representative of the central Dhammayut way of presenting meditation. Both calm and insight are advocated in the form of the foundations of mindfulness and mindfulness of breathing. He writes,

> Working for calm is the first step because the mind needs relief from the defilements which bind and envelop it. One can then practise for insight as the tranquil mind is balanced and free from the bias of the defilements. Whatever is then investigated can be seen clearly for what it really is, and this is where insight begins to develop.[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-418 "note")

This kind of approach, which keeps relatively closely to the textual tradition, is widespread among scholar monks and tends to be taught in temples that do not have a link to a wider meditative tradition and do not have a specific practice of their own. Something similar is found in many Mahānikāya monasteries also.

Although the Dhammayuttika Nikāya had some ongoing meditative practice, it was not much stressed among scholar monks before the 1950s. However, with the introduction of Burmese insight meditation in the Mahānikāya in the 1950s and the associated lay turn toward interest in meditation, the response of the leaders of the Dhammayut was to turn not to Burmese-style insight but rather to promote interest in a meditative tradition of their own: the wandering monks of the northeastern Forest Tradition.

<!-- Page 169 -->

### ***Ajahn Mun Bhūridatta***

Most of the famous meditation teachers of the Forest Tradition in the latter part of the twentieth century were disciples of Ajahn Mun Bhūridatta (1870–1949). He joined the new, growing Dhammayut order under the influence of his teacher Ajahn Sao (1861–1942). They were attracted by the strict discipline of the Dhammayuts and the practice orientation of the local abbots of that tradition in the northeast. It is clear, however, that the Forest Tradition had a long prior history in that area and, although less well known, has continued also in the Mahānikāya.

What is striking about the disciples of Ajahn Mun is a considerable diversity of approach. This suggests a considerable broad-mindedness on his part and an underlying richness to the tradition he embodied. He himself remained a wanderer all his life, building up small communities of disciples in many different locations. But in the changed circumstances of deforestation in Thailand and the distrust in homeless monks emanating from some circles in Bangkok, most of his pupils eventually settled down in particular locations.

Given the hagiographic nature of his biography, it is difficult to be quite clear as to Mun’s teaching. He certainly taught meditation on the thirty-two parts of the body and the use of the mantra “Buddho” with mindfulness of breathing. It is likely that he encouraged a wide variety of practices for more experienced pupils and to suit different individuals.

We should also note that the major emphasis of the Forest Tradition, whether Mahānikāya or Dhammayuttika Nikāya, is on the undertaking of the thirteen purification practices (*dhutaṅga*s), the optional additional practices that monks may undertake—such things as sleeping in the open air, eating only once a day, and the like. Within that we should expect some variety of meditative practice.

### ***Ajahn Mahā Boowa***

One of the most well-known disciples of Ajahn Mun was Ajahn Mahā Boowa (1913–2011). He was especially famous for the severity of his teaching and for the authorship of Ajahn Mun’s biography. He had passed the monastic examinations in Pali and *dhamma* before turning to forest practice with Ajahn Mun. His writings are full of detailed information relating both to the practice of concentration and to the development of wisdom.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-419 "note") A special emphasis of his is on the practice of “mindfulness and wisdom” (*sati*-*paññā*).

<!-- Page 170 -->

### ***Ajahn Lee Dhammadharo***

Ajahn Lee Dhammadhāro (1906–1961) was responsible for establishing centers of the Forest Tradition in eastern and central Thailand. He has been described as “perhaps the most famous disciple of Mun to become involved in amulets and relics.”[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-420 "note") In fact, he is one of the more well-known teachers of the Forest Tradition in the West, largely due to the work of Ṭhānissaro Bhikkhu—a Western pupil of a leading pupil of Ajahn Lee. His teaching is more closely based on theory than some of the other teachers of the Forest Tradition, but he also uses a number of traditional ideas. He refers to different types of breath or wind and also lists various “focal points” along the lines of the bases of the *Porāṇa* tradition.[[23]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-421 "note") He is known for his own particular techniques of combining counting with “Buddho” and the breath.

### ***Criticism of the Forest Tradition***

Before concluding, let me add that admirable and inspiring as the Forest Tradition is, it is not without its critics. It advocates a rather intense lifestyle, which the majority of ordinary monks would probably see as somewhat extreme and not quite the middle way that the Buddhist path should be. Many monks following more traditional paths believe that a more balanced approach can be just as effective. It also leaves less scope for lay practice than some other traditions.

There is also a problem with the tendency within the traditions to claim *arahat*ship for some of its teachers and to present its temples as actively practicing *jhāna* and insight. This goes against the strong tradition that one should not make claims to spiritual status or attainment and indeed rejection of such claims is built into the *vinaya* rules. On the whole, however, any such criticisms tend to be rather muted, not least because of the strong support for teachers of the Forest Tradition given by King Bhumibol Adulyadej (1927–2016) and his family.

## **Meditation beyond Burma, Sri Lanka, and Thailand**

In the last three chapters we have touched on some of the many varieties of practice current in the Southern Buddhist countries today. A few areas have been omitted—notably Laos and to some extent Cambodia, which tend to be similar to adjacent areas of Thailand, and neighboring areas in Assam, Bangladesh, China, and Malaysia, where Theravāda Buddhism is traditionally practiced. I have also not addressed independent developments among Asians, Europeans, and in the Americas, of which there are many. On the whole, the varieties I have referred to tend to be seen as different routes to the same goal, although many teachers present their particular practice as either the best way to begin or as capable of going all the way to the goal and may doubt how others can do this. Many others would see such distinctions and claims as futile and stress that different methods suit different individuals.

<!-- Page 171 -->

# 11. *Jhāna* Meditation Instructions from the Theriya Tradition

*A Translation of Chapter Fourteen of Ācariya Buddhadatta’s*

Entrance to Abhidhamma

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

This last chapter is an article and translation written by the author for the *Samatha Journal*, a publication of his own meditation group, the Samatha Trust.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-422 "note")

Ācariya Buddhadatta is seen as one of the three great commentators of the Theriya tradition, along with Buddhaghosa and Dhammapāla. He probably lived around the same time as Buddhaghosa, during the fourth or fifth century c.e. Indeed, an account from many centuries later claims that Buddhadatta left Ceylon just as Buddhaghosa was arriving. Their boats are said to have met halfway between Ceylon and India. Certainly the writings of both are based on the same ancient commentaries from the Great Monastery (Mahāvihāra) in Anurādhapura, the then capital of Ceylon.

Buddhadatta’s main works are verse presentations of *abhidhamma* (systematic theory) and *vinaya* (monastic discipline). Although shorter than his treatment of *vinaya*, the *Abhidhammāvatāra*, or *Entrance to Abhidhamma*, is probably his most important work.

The chapter translated here, “The Bringing into Being of Form-Frequenting Concentration,” concerns the practice of *jhāna*. My translation is loosely arranged in the two-line verses of the original but without any further attempt to render it as English poetry. Pali line numbers have been omitted for ease of reading. Headings have been added to show the progression of the text. Some notes are added at the end of the translation to clarify points in the verses that may be unclear.

The work is addressed to *bhikkhu*s and it is clear that the account is intended for those who can devote themselves full-time to meditation practice. This is in contrast to some of the *sutta*s (discourses), which are often addressed to *bhikkhu*s as a form of respect but describe practices that may be undertaken by anyone. Even so, I present Buddhadatta’s teaching here in the belief that the presentation of an ideal form of practice can be helpful also for those following a less intense path.

# The Bringing into Being of Form-Frequenting Concentration

<!-- Page 172 -->

## **Form-Frequenting Concentration**

I will henceforth explain the supreme method of bringing into being[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-423 "note") that brings gain

and the well-trained mind that brings happiness—hear this delightful exposition.

## **Establishing *SĪla***

Knowing and seeing that is more than the *dhamma* of men—one desirous of attaining this should first purify *sīla*.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-424 "note")

Where practice is mixed with doubt, with wrong conduct devoid of *sīla*,

there is no *jhāna*—how could there be the path? Therefore one should purify *sīla*.

*Sīla* is reckoned as twofold in terms of what is to be done and what is to be avoided.

But without gap or lack, without blemish, blameless it

should be performed by one desirous of the goal and wishing for the happiness of solitude (*viveka*).[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-425 "note")

For *sīla* is for *bhikkhu*s—the incomparable finery,

the treasure, the sure refuge, the shelter, the haven, the resort,

and the fine wishing-jewel; *sīla* is the incomparable vehicle (*yāna*);

*sīla* is the cool liquid that washes away the stains of the defilements,

both as being the root of good qualities and a mighty destroyer of faults.

This too is the supreme, incomparable stairway that mounts to the heaven of the Three (times Ten)[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-426 "note")

and the sure path to the entrance to the city of *nibbāna*.

Therefore this *sīla*, twofold in characteristic, should be made

very purified by one desiring the goal, the *bhikkhu* to whom *sīla* is dear.

## **Obtaining the Meditation Subject**

When one in this teaching is established in purified *sīla*,

he should cut off any obstacle—but obstacles they say are ten.

The ten are: dwelling place, family, gain, a group, and work is fifth,

travel, kindred, sickness, study, *iddhi*.

After cutting off the ten kinds of obstacle,

he should approach the giver of the *kammaṭṭhāna*.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-427 "note")

Pleasant, commanding respect, revered, one who says [what is needed], easy to speak to,

both a speaker of profound discourse and not one who incites one to the wrong thing.

After approaching at the right time one endowed with these and similar qualities,

<!-- Page 173 -->

a seeker of the good, a good friend, the giver of the *kammaṭṭhāna*,

the *kammaṭṭhāna* should be obtained; but only after performing the duties due to him.

He should give it to the *bhikkhu* after knowing his character type.

## **Character Types**

This character type is reckoned as of six kinds

with regard to passion, hate, and delusion and to faith, intelligence, and thinking.

According to the method of mixing them, they are sixty-four,

but thinking there is no use to them, I have not shown them here.

The ten uglinesses, likewise mindfulness connected with the body—

these eleven are suited to someone of the passion type.

The four immeasurables, the color *kasiṇa*s[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-428 "note")—these

eight are always suitable for someone of the hate type.

For the very deluded character type and for the thinking character type[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-429 "note")

in-and-out breathing is the single one prescribed as suitable.

The set of the first six recollections are for the person of the faith character type.

Mindfulness as to death and peace, food-linked

recognition, discrimination of the elements—for the individual

of intelligence nature these four are pointed out as suitable.

Both the four linked to non-*rūpa*[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-430 "note") and the remaining *kasiṇa*s—these are explained as suitable for all character types.

It should be known by the clever that all this was said

from the standpoint of complete opposition and extreme beneficiality.

## **Subjects of Meditation**

One should indicate that all of the *kammaṭṭhāna*s [number] forty:

ten *kasiṇa*s, ten each of uglinesses and recollections;

four immeasurables and four connected to non-*rūpa*;

the discrimination of the elements, and the recognition to do with food.

Among these *kammaṭṭhāna*s, how many bring approach (*upacāra*)?

Leaving out mindfulness of in-and-out breathing and that connected with the body,

the remaining eight recollections, the recognition [of food] and the discrimination [of the elements]—

these ten are said to bring approach; the rest bring absorption.

Among those that bring absorption, the ten *kasiṇa*s

and also mindfulness of in-and-out breathing—these have four *jhāna*s.

<!-- Page 174 -->

The ten uglinesses, likewise mindfulness connected with the body—

these eleven *dhamma*s may be of the first *jhāna*.

The three first *brahmavihāra*s bring three *jhāna*s;

the fourth and those connected with non-*rūpa* are reckoned as belonging to the fourth *jhāna*.

Surmounting is twofold with reference to the support and the factors;

there is surmounting of the pasture in those connected with non-*rūpa*, surmounting of the

*jhāna* factors in those connected with *rūpa*.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-431 "note")

Only the ten *kasiṇa*s out of these should be increased;

but not to be increased are the uglinesses and the rest.

The ten *kasiṇa*s and also the ten uglinesses,

likewise mindfulness of in-and-out breathing, also mindfulness connected with the body

are supports that can be semblance *nimitta*s.

The rest could not be supports that are semblance *nimitta*s.

The ten uglinesses, recognition of food, mindfulness connected with the body—

these twelve do not at any time occur among the *deva*s.

Both these twelve and also mindfulness of in-and-out breathing—

these thirteen are not found in the *brahma* world.

Apart from the four connected with non-*rūpa* there are none among non-*rūpa* beings.

In the human world all certainly occur.

Omitting the fourth *kasiṇa* (air), the *kasiṇa*s and the uglinesses—

these nineteen are to be grasped only by the seen.

But in mindfulness as to body, the five beginning with skin are to be grasped by the seen, the remainder of the parts only by the heard.

Mindfulness of in-and-out breath is explained as to be grasped by the touched;

wind *kasiṇa* alone is grasped by [both] the seen and the touched.

But the remaining eighteen are to be grasped by the heard alone.

The immeasurable of balance[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-432 "note") and also the non-*rūpa*s—these five

have been made known as not to be grasped right from the beginning.

The remaining thirty-five can be grasped from the beginning.

Among these *kammaṭṭhāna*s, excluding space *kasiṇa*,

nine *kasiṇa*s are conditions for the non-*rūpa* [levels].

But ten *kasiṇa*s are conditions for the *abhiññā*s;[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-433 "note")

three *brahmavihāra*s are conditions for the fourth.

<!-- Page 175 -->

Each lower non-*rūpa* level is a condition for each higher one.

Similarly the fourth is a condition for cessation (*nirodha*).[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-434 "note")

But all of these forty kinds can be conditions

for insight, for success in achieving a state of being, and for happiness.

## **How to Practice the Earth *Kasiḷa***

To someone who has obtained the *kammaṭṭhāna* and dwells in the presence of the teacher himself,

it should be expounded [at the moment of coming of whatever comes].

But when a *bhikkhu* wishes to go elsewhere after obtaining it,

it should be expounded neither too succinctly nor in too much detail.

When he has obtained the *kammaṭṭhāna*, the weak point[[14]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-435 "note") of the mind-born *deva*,

in a suitable *vihāra*, free from the eighteen defects,

he should dwell constantly—one not too far from

the village nor too near, auspicious and endowed with five factors.

A minor obstacle too should be cut off. If there is

long hair, nails, or body hair, they should be cut by the *yogin*.

The robe should be dyed if it is stained.

If there is a mark on the bowl, it should be baked.

The *bhikkhu* who has cut off the obstacles

and dwells happily in a secluded place,

should avoid blue, yellow, red, and white soil and make

an attractive *kasiṇa* with soil that is smooth and the color of the dawn,

if he is wise and desires to attain *jhāna*.

Either in secluded accommodation or in a place like that outside,

in a concealed place or in a hollow place in a cave,

the *kasiṇa* should be made either movable or fixed.

One making a movable one should bind onto four sticks

either leather or matting or woven cloth

and make the *kasiṇa* there in accordance with the amount of soil.

Laying it on the ground, he should look upon it.

A fixed one should be made as a circle like the round part [calyx?] of a lotus

by striking stakes into the ground and covering it with creepers.

In detail it is fitting for one who wishes to be free from the circle

to make a circle measuring a span and four finger breadths.

After making the *kasiṇa* circle [*maṇḍala*] pleasant like the surface of a drum,

after thoroughly sweeping the locality, after going to bathe and returning,

the wise person sits there in a well-prepared place

<!-- Page 176 -->

within arm’s reach (*hattha*-*pāsa*) of the *kasiṇa* circle

on a well-covered seat, a span and four finger breadths high.

Having positioned the body erect and made mindfulness before him,

having seen the danger in *kāma*s,[[15]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-436 "note") having seen freedom from *kāma*s as safe,

having aroused the highest joy and gladness toward the three jewels,

having made a supreme effort with the [resolve]

“through this practice may I surely be a sharer in the happiness of seclusion (*paviveka*),”

he should open his eyes only to a moderate degree

and practice again and again, grasping its appearance (*nimitta*) well.

The color should not be looked at nor should the *lakkhaṇa* be seen,

but without releasing the color, the learned one should place the mind

predominantly upon the concept (*paṇṇatti*-*dhamma*) and practice

with one-pointed mind, repeating “*paṭhavī* [earth], *paṭhavī*.”

It is fitting to repeat any one among such names for earth as earth, wide one (*medinī*),

ground (*bhūmi*), giver of wealth (*vasudhā*), or bearer of wealth (*vasundharā*).

Alternately with open and closed eyes, one should advert to it again and again,

just so long as the acquired *nimitta* does not arise.[[16]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-437 "note")

## **Developing the *Nimitta*s**

At the time when, as he practices in this way with one-pointed mind,

it comes into the mind’s eye (*āpātha*) of the *yogin* who is adverting with closed eyes

in the same way as at the time when they are open,

at that time the acquired *nimitta* is said to have arisen.

But when the *nimitta* has arisen, henceforth the discerning

*yogin* should not sit in that place.

The wise one should enter his own dwelling place

and develop it seated there, as is comfortable.

But in order to avoid proliferation of washing the feet

two single-soled sandals are desirable.

If his immature concentration perishes due to something damaging (*asappāya*),

he should go to the same place and take it again.

He should practice again and again, seated comfortably on a stool

and it should be paid careful attention to and impacted by thought (*takka*).

But if the mind leaves the *nimitta* and runs without,

the mind should be restrained and fixed on the *nimitta*.

<!-- Page 177 -->

Wherever he is sitting, when that one rich in *tapas*[[17]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-438 "note") desires the *nimitta*,

in that same place, by day or by night, it arises in his mind.

All five hindrances are obstructed (*vikkhambhanti*)

stage by stage for the *yogin* who does so.

The mind is concentrated with approach concentration

and the semblance *nimitta* arises for the *yogin*.

But what is the difference between this and the previous acquired *nimitta*?

It is like the polished disk of a mirror drawn from a bag;

like the disk of the moon when it is completely full coming out from a cloud;

like cranes against a storm cloud is the semblance *nimitta*,

at that point, emerging as if breaking up the acquired *nimitta*.

It appears to him as much purer than the acquired *nimitta*.

But it possesses neither shape nor color.

It is a mere mode of appearance, born of recognition, made through bringing into being (*bhāvanā*).

When the semblance *nimitta*, made through bringing to being,

has arisen, the five hindrances too are obstructed.

Defilements are truly stilled for the *bhikkhu* earnest in work (*yuttayoga*).

The mind is concentrated with approach concentration.

## **Reaching Absorption**

But the mind becomes concentrated in two ways for the one who possesses concentration:

at the moment of approach and [at the moment of] obtaining.

By the abandoning of the hindrances at the moment of approach

but likewise by the appearance of the [*jhāna*] factors at the moment of obtaining.

“But declare what is the difference between the two kinds of concentration?”

At the moment of approach the factors have not become strong,

but in absorption factors that have become strong would arise.

Therefore that absorption mind occurs even for a day.

If in that very sitting he can develop the *nimitta*

and reach absorption, it is good.

If he cannot, that *nimitta* should be constantly guarded

as if it were a hard to obtain treasure like the embryo of a *cakkavattin*[[18]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-439 "note")

by the heedful *yogin* who possesses mindfulness.

Loss does not occur to one who guards the *nimitta* he has obtained.

When there is no guarding, what has been obtained perishes each time;

so it should be guarded. This is the way of guarding it:

<!-- Page 178 -->

Dwelling place, food source, conversation, person, food, temperature,

mode of activity (*iriyāpatha*)—he should avoid these seven unhelpful things;

he should cultivate the seven helpful kinds. For so practicing

a *bhikkhu* will before long have absorption.

The intelligent person who does not have absorption even so practicing

should perfectly accomplish skill in absorption.

This skill in absorption is of ten kinds

but it is not explained by me here out of fear of too long a book.

But absorption comes about when the *nimitta* has been obtained,

to one who accomplishes skill in absorption in this way.

If it does not occur to one who has practiced in this way,

he should even so not give up effort (*yoga*); the learned person must still strive.

Therefore the intelligent person would examine the manner of occurrence of his *citta*

and again and again unite evenness with vigor (*viriya*).

Let him take hold of the mind when it becomes just a little lax and

restraining the mind when it is too energetic, he should make it occur evenly.

After freeing the mind from lax and energetic states

he should bring it toward the semblance *nimitta*.

## ***Jhāna* Mind**

But when he brings it toward the *nimitta* in this way

with the idea “now certainly absorption will succeed,”

adverting *citta* is born to the *yogin*,

cutting off the passive mind[[19]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-440 "note") and likewise making that same earth *kasiṇa*

the support at the mind door; next, four or five

active minds[[20]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-441 "note") with the very same support are born to him.

Last of these would be a single *rūpa*-frequenting active mind.

The thinking and other *jhāna* factors are stronger than the previous ones.

These active minds are called preparatory and approach

due to being preparatory to and approaching absorption mind

and inclination (*anuloma*) due to inclining toward absorption.

The one that is last of all out of these is called “family membership” (*gotrabhū*).

By not taking those that have already been taken, preparatory is first;

second is approach; third is inclination;

the fourth is explained as “family membership”; the fifth is absorption mind.

Alternatively the first is approach; the second is inclination;

<!-- Page 179 -->

the third is explained as “family membership”; the fourth is absorption mind.

Either the fourth or the fifth makes the mind absorbed—not those after.

In the sixth or seventh absorption is not born;

due to the nearness of the passive mind the active mind is in process of falling.

Yet the Abhidhammika Godatta said as to this that

because of previous repetition, the sixth or the seventh become absorbed.

In fact, just as someone running toward a sheer drop

even if he wishes to stop at the edge is unable to do so,

it should be known by the clever that in the same way

one cannot enter absorption in the sixth or seventh mind.

It is pointed out that this absorption is for just one *citta* moment;

then there is falling into the passive mind;

then after cutting the passive mind, in order to recollect

adverting; then the mind that recollects *jhāna*.

## ***Jhāna* Factors**

Sensual purpose; ill will; sloth and sleepiness; excited

guilt; doubt—these five are abandoned.

Conjoined with thinking, exploring, joy, happiness,

and one-pointedness—this *jhāna* is fivefold.

The mind is greedy for various objects of sense due to sensual purpose,

wandering hither and thither like a monkey in the forest.

Because of unifying (*samādhāna*) the mind on just one object

concentration is called the opponent of sensual purpose.

Due to its goodness of feeling (*pāmojjabhāva*) and its nature being to cool,

joy is spoken of as the opponent of ill will.

Because it spreads out (*savipphārikattā*) as thought free from sensuality and the like,

thinking is explained as the opponent of sloth and sleepiness.

Because their nature is unquiet and because happiness is itself very peaceful,

happiness is the opponent of the pair of excitement and guilt.

Because it resembles wisdom, exploring that has the characteristic of constant scanning

is shown as the opponent of doubt.

Thus the first *jhāna* that is freed from five factors and joined with five,

fortunate, beautiful in three ways, joined to ten *lakkhaṇa*s,

has been reached by the *yogin*.

One who desires that *jhāna* to last a very long while

should attain it after purifying it of evils in every way.

If he attains it [in this way], it would be very long-lasting.

The *bhikkhu* desiring to fully develop his mind (*cittabhāvanā*)

<!-- Page 180 -->

should expand the semblance *nimitta* step by step.[[21]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-442 "note")

There are two planes for expansion: approach and absorption.

It is fitting to expand it either after reaching approach

or after reaching absorption. This is the course for expanding it:

just as a plowman circumscribes the place to be plowed

in just the same way the *yogin* should expand the *nimitta* after circumscribing

successively at distances of one and two fingerbreadths and so on as far as desired.

## **The Masteries**

When first *jhāna* has been obtained, in five ways

the practitioner of *tapas* should thoroughly master it.

The masteries are spoken of as five—the three of adverting,

attaining, and resolving (*adhiṭṭhāna*) with emergence and recollection.

One would perfect the five masteries by constantly

adverting, attaining, resolving, emerging, and recollecting.

For the *yogin* who wants the second without obtaining mastery of the first

would fall from both—from the first and from the second too.

## **Progressing through the *Jhāna*s**

If labeling and bringing to mind of sense objects occur to him,

the *jhāna* of that careless *yogin* is one “tending to decline.”

Mindfulness that is aligned to it becomes fixed in that—

this *jhāna* of the slow *yogin* is “tending to stability.”

If labeling and bringing to mind without thought occur to him,

the *jhāna* of that careful one would be “tending to distinction.”

If labeling and bringing to mind joined with disenchantment occur to him,

it has been explained that the *jhāna* is tending to penetration.

Therefore the wise one who has thoroughly mastered these five,

after emerging with proficiency from the first *jhāna*,

because this attainment has weak factors since it is near to

the opposition to the skillful and since thought and exploring are gross,

in this way sees the inferiority in the first. Rather the wise *yogin*

reflects on the second *jhāna* as peaceful.

Overcoming affection for the first *jhāna*,

he should undertake the course of practice to achieve the second.

## **Second *Jhāna***

Then when he has emerged from *jhāna* in the proper way

and, mindful and aware, is recollecting the *jhāna* factors,

thinking and exploring manifest to the *yogin* as gross;

the remaining three factors manifest to him as peaceful.

<!-- Page 181 -->

At that time, as the *yogin* is bringing to mind that same *nimitta*

in order to abandon the gross factors and obtain the peaceful ones

bringing it to mind as “earth, earth,”

he knows: “Now the second *jhāna* will arise.”

Cutting off the passive mind and making earth *kasiṇa*

the support at the mind door,

adverting *citta* is born to the *yogin*. Then four or five

active minds with that same support are born to him.

One active thought, the last of these,

is a form-frequenting second *jhāna* mind.

Clarified within, the *jhāna* has three factors —it

is joined with joy and happiness and one-pointedness.[[22]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-443 "note")

One should fully comprehend the rest in the way given above.

Thus, lacking two factors, linked with three factors,

second *jhāna*, beautiful in three ways, linked to ten *lakkhaṇa*s,

fashioned through bringing into being, has been reached by the *bhikkhu*.

## **Third *Jhāna***

When the second *jhāna* has been reached and one who is mindful

has thorough mastery in the five ways of the second,

after emerging from that second *jhāna* with proficiency,

he thinks, “This attainment is near to thinking and exploring

and it has weak factors due to the grossness of joy

since there is excitement of the mind due to joy.”

Having seen the inferiority of it and the third *jhāna* as peaceful,

overcoming affection for the second *jhāna*,

he should undertake the course of bringing into being to achieve the third.

Then when he has emerged from the second *jhāna*

and, mindful and aware, is recollecting the *jhāna* factors,

joy manifests as gross and happiness, and the like as peaceful.

At that time in order to abandon gross factors and obtain peaceful ones

the *yogin* brings to mind the very same *nimitta*,

again [repeating] “earth, earth.”

He knows, “Now the third *jhāna* will arise.”

Cutting off the passive mind and making earth *kasiṇa*

the support at the mind door,

adverting *citta* is born to the *yogin*. Then four or five

active minds with that same support are born to him.

One active thought, the last of these,

is a form-frequenting third *jhāna* mind.

Joined with mindfulness and awareness, the third is linked

with happiness and one-pointedness and reckoned as having two factors.

One should fully comprehend the rest in the way given above.

<!-- Page 182 -->

But, deprived of one factor, linked with two factors,

third *jhāna*, beautiful in three ways, linked to ten *lakkhaṇa*s,

fashioned through bringing into being, has been reached by the *bhikkhu*.

## **Fourth *Jhāna***

When the third *jhāna* has been reached and one who is mindful

has thorough mastery in the five ways of the third,

after emerging from that third *jhāna* with proficiency,

he thinks, “This attainment is near to the defect of joy

and when there is directing (*ābhoga*) of the mind concerned with happiness

it has weak factors due to the grossness of happiness.”

Seeing the inferiority of the third *jhāna* in this way,

and seeing the fourth *jhāna* as peaceful,

overcoming affection for the third *jhāna*,

he should undertake the course of bringing into being to achieve the fourth.

Then when he has emerged from the third *jhāna*

and, mindful and aware, is recollecting the *jhāna* factors,

the happy mind manifests to him as gross, and

balanced viewing and one-pointedness of mind as peaceful.

At that time, in order to abandon the gross factor and obtain a peaceful one

the *yogin* brings to mind the very same *nimitta*,

again [repeating] “earth, earth.”

He knows, “Now the fourth *jhāna* will arise.”

Cutting off the passive mind and making earth *kasiṇa*

the support at the mind door,

adverting *citta* is born to the *yogin*. Then four or five

active minds with that same support are born to him.

One active thought, the last of these,

is a form-frequenting fourth *jhāna* mind.

Deprived of one factor, because it is united with two factors

the third is reckoned as having two factors.

In this way fourth *jhāna*, beautiful in three ways, linked to ten *lakkhaṇa*s,

fashioned through bringing into being, has been reached by the *bhikkhu*.

Because happiness is not a repetition [condition] for balanced viewing

and instead active minds associated with balanced viewing are active,

therefore the fourth is reckoned as joined with balanced viewing.
