This is the difference with it; the rest is as said previously.

## **The *Abhidhamma* Method**

Dividing into two the *jhāna* that is second in the method (*naya*) of the set of four

the second and the third in the method of the set of five are made;

the third becomes the fourth and the fourth fifth in that method.

<!-- Page 183 -->

The first remains first—this is the difference between them.

## **In Conclusion**

In this way up to here I have spoken not too concisely nor in too much detail

of the bringing into being of the form-frequenting.

A very sweet and choice utterance.

What man would these words not delight,

words that are to be experienced by people

whose tranquil understanding is very sharp and clear.

# Appendix A

*Buddhaghosa’s Classification of Forty Objects*

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

by Sarah Shaw

There have been all sorts of ways of classifying meditation objects, as seen in this book. The most influential paradigm of such a categorization is Buddhaghosa’s, in his fifth-century manual *The Path of Purification* (*Visuddhimagga*). This table shows how he sorts out the objects into lists. Although there are one or two instances in the Pali Canon of objects not classified by Buddhaghosa, by and large all found there are included here and have been practiced historically in Southern Buddhist contexts.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-444 "note") Buddhaghosa also stresses that temperament is key to the teacher’s assignation of a particular subject or range of meditation objects.[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-445 "note") The teacher assigns each of the subjects an “upper” limit of attainment. Such a designation is implied in the canon, but not stated. For some meditations, historically, different conclusions could be and have been drawn, as the meditations have been adapted in different places and times. Buddhaghosa, for instance, places the upper limit of *Buddhānussati* at access or approach (*upacāra*) *jhāna*, as the complexity of the subject does not allow for the unification of a deep meditation. In other traditions, variations on this practice are felt to disclose or allow to unfold the whole Buddhist path.[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-446 "note") Historically, it would be fair to say that the range and content of this practice has varied, as it has for some others.

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | **Meditation object** | **Level of attainment suggested by Buddhaghosa** |
| 1–10 | Ten devices (*kasiṇa*s): earth, water, fire, air, blue, yellow, red, white, light, and limited space | All four *jhāna*s and from there the formless meditations; these are the classic *samatha* objects |
| 11–20 | Ten uglinesses: usually associated with the ten stages of the decomposition of a corpse: bloated, livid, festering, cut-up, gnawed, scattered, hacked and scattered, bleeding, worm-infested, and skeleton | First *jhāna* |
| 21–26 | The six recollections: of the Buddha, the Dhamma, the Sa*ṅ*gha, *sīla*, generosity (*cāga*), and the sense-sphere gods (*deva*s) | Access or approach *jhāna*; Buddhaghosa maintains that these practices arouse faith, cheerfulness, and direction but are too diverse and complex as subjects to bring the mind to *jhāna* |
| 27–30 | Four mindfulnesses: death, body, breath, and peace | Mindfulness of death and of peace are said to lead to access or approach *jhāna*; the body leads to the first *jhāna*; the breath leads to all four *jhāna*s |
| 31–34 | Four divine abidings: loving kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity | First three *jhāna*s; the last one (equanimity) the fourth *jhāna* |
| 35–38 | Four formless spheres: sphere of infinite space, sphere of infinite consciousness, sphere where nothing is owned, sphere of neither labeling nor non-labeling | The formless spheres, associated with the fourth *jhāna* as a basis |
| 39–40 | The loathsomeness of food and the perception of the four elements | Access or approach *jhāna* |

<!-- Page 184 -->

## **The Seven Factors of Awakening**

The seven factors of awakening make up a core list in the *sutta*s. The *Bojjhaṅgasaṃyutta* section of the *Saṃyuttanikāya* is devoted to passages about them; these focus especially on the seven factors’ effectiveness in overcoming the five hindrances to meditation—desire, ill will, sloth and torpor, excitement and remorse, and doubt (SN V 63–140). They comprise seven of the *bodhipakkiyadhamma*s, the *dhamma*s contributing to awakening.

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| **Factor of awakening** | ***Bojjhaṅga*** |
| Mindfulness | *Sati* |
| Investigation of *dhamma*(s) | *Dhammavicāya* |
| Vigor | *Viriya* |
| Joy | *Pīti* |
| Tranquility | *Passaddhi* |
| Concentration | *Samādhi* |
| Equanimity | *Upekkhā* |

## **The Seven Purifications**

The seven purifications form the basis of the structure of the *Visuddhimagga* and are found in the *Rathavinita Sutta* (M 24), where the Buddha makes a comparison with a king making a journey by means of a relay of chariots from Sāvatthi to Saketa, where the horses are replaced at each stop. The path is like seven phases of the journey, with one of the purifications being taken over by the next until the goal. The seven are:

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| **Purification** | **Purpose** |
| Purification of morality, ethics (*sīlavisuddhi*) | Purification of mind (*cittavisuddhi*) |
| Purification of mind | Purification of view (*diṭṭhivisuddhi*) |
| Purification of view | Purification through overcoming doubt (*kaṅkhāvitaraṇavisuddhi*) |
| Purification through overcoming doubt | Purification of knowledge and vision of what is path and what is not path (*maggāmaggañāṇadassanavisuddhi*) |
| Purification of knowledge and vision of what is path and what is not path | Purification of knowledge and vision of the practice (*paṭipadāñāṇadassanavisuddhi*) |
| Purification of knowledge and vision of the practice | Purification of knowledge and vision (*ñāṇadassanavisuddhi*) |
| Purification of knowledge and vision | Final entry into *nibbāna* without clinging (*anupādāparinibbānaṁ*) |

## **The Ten Defilements of Insight**

Buddhaghosa discusses these in the section on what is path and what is not path (*Vism* 633–638/*The Path of Purification*, XX 105–130). They arise after overcoming doubt. They represent stages of insight practice which, while good and beneficial in themselves, can give rise to attachment and views—that, for instance, one is enlightened when one is not. After they have been overcome, the practitioner knows what is path and what is not path.

<!-- Page 185 -->

|  |
| --- |
| **The ten defilements of insight (*vipassanŪpakkilesa*)** |
| 1. Illumination (*obhāsa*) due to insight |
| 2. Knowledge (*ñāṇa*) due to insight |
| 3. Joy (*pīti*) due to insight |
| 4. Tranquility (*passaddhi*) due to insight |
| 5. Happiness (*sukha*) due to insight |
| 6. Commitment (*adhimokkha*) is faith arising in association with insight |
| 7. Exertion is energy arising in association with insight (*paggaha*) |
| 8. Assurance is mindfulness (*sati*) arising in association with insight |
| 9. Equanimity (*upekkhā*) about insight, and equanimity in adverting to formations |
| 10. Attachment (*nikanti*) due to insight |

# Appendix B

*Commonly Cited Passages*

![](../images/9780834844438_Page_v_Image_0001.jpg)

by Sarah Shaw

Lance mentions a few lists in the book that may not be known to some readers. I give three of them in full here, in the form of translations of canonical passages that describe them. These passages are “pericopes,” or pieces of text found in a number of places that remain essentially the same in each context.[[1]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-447 "note") As will be seen, the lists relate closely to one another too; it is a characteristic of Pali categorization that lists overlap and interleave in various ways, so that elements that occur in one evoke, or shed new light on, those in others.

## **The Spheres of Mastery or Transcendence (*Abhibhayātana*s)**

This first pericope is a list that Lance felt was very ancient, the somewhat mysterious “spheres of mastery or transcendence.” This appears to describe areas where the practitioner can “master” a particular field of experience and knowledge through their meditation, freeing themselves from attachment to or rejection of the objects of meditation and their counterparts in the physical realm. It relates particularly to the first twenty objects of Buddhaghosa’s list, shown in Appendix A. The *kasiṇa*s, the first ten of Buddhaghosa’s objects, are known as “beautiful.” The “ugly” or “foul” meditations that focus on the unattractive side of physical appearance, such as the decomposition of corpses, comprise the second decad in Buddhaghosa’s list. The four colors of the *kasiṇa* practice—blue, yellow, red, and white—are taken as the basis of the second half of this list of *abhibhayātana*s.

<!-- Page 186 -->

> Perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, limited, of beautiful and ugly appearance. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the first sphere of mastery.
>
> Perceiving forms externally, they see forms externally, immeasurable, of beautiful and ugly appearance. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the second sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, limited, of beautiful and ugly appearance. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the third sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, immeasurable, of beautiful and ugly appearance. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the fourth sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, blue, of blue color, characterized by blue, and of blue luminosity. Just as the flax flower is blue, characterized by blue, and of blue luminosity, or again as fine Benares muslin, smoothly finished on both sides, is blue, of blue color, characterized by blue, and of blue luminosity, even so, not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, blue, of blue color, characterized by blue, and of blue luminosity. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the fifth sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, yellow, of yellow color, characterized by yellow, and of yellow luminosity. Just as the *karnikara* flower[[2]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-448 "note") is yellow, characterized by yellow, and of yellow luminosity, or again as fine Benares muslin, smoothly finished on both sides, is yellow, of yellow color, characterized by yellow, and of yellow luminosity, even so, not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, yellow, of yellow color, characterized by yellow, and of yellow luminosity. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the sixth sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, red, of red color, characterized by red, and of red luminosity. Just as the jungle geranium flower[[3]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-449 "note") is red, characterized by red, and of red luminosity, or again as fine Benares muslin, smoothly finished on both sides, is red, of red color, characterized by red, and of red luminosity, even so, not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, red, of red color, characterized by red, and of red luminosity. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the seventh sphere of mastery.
>
> Not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, white, of white color, characterized by white, and of white luminosity. Just as the morning star is white, characterized by white, and of white luminosity, or again as fine Benares muslin, smoothly finished on both sides, is white, of white color, characterized by white, and of white luminosity, even so, not perceiving forms internally, they see forms externally, white, of white color, characterized by white, and of white luminosity. “Mastering these, I know them, I see them”; they perceive in this way. This is the eighth sphere of mastery.

<!-- Page 187 -->

## **The Eight Freedoms (*Vimokkha*s)**

This list is often found with the one above, though is probably slightly later in date.

The first element is explained as the freedom that arises through practicing meditation on the material form of one’s own body; the second on material forms—the *kasiṇa* for instance, in the external world. The “release on to the beautiful” is the full practice of the *kasiṇa* meditation, or additionally, perhaps, the meditation on the divine abidings, also sometimes known as “beautiful”; it suggests the freedom found when such a meditation releases the mind from particularity and the sense-sphere. The next four are the four formless spheres of meditation, as any perception of diversity and differentiation is transcended; the last involves the cessation of feeling and perception, in a state experienced only by *arahat*s.

> Possessed of material form, they see forms; this is the first freedom.
>
> Not perceiving material forms internally, they see material forms externally; this is the second freedom.[[4]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-450 "note")
>
> They release their mind onto the beautiful;[[5]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-451 "note") this is the third freedom.
>
> By completely surmounting the perception of material forms, by leaving behind perceptions of sensory impingement, and by not paying attention to perceptions of diversity, reflecting “space is infinite,” they enter on and abide in the sphere of infinite space; this is the fourth freedom.
>
> By completely surmounting the sphere of infinite space, reflecting “consciousness is infinite,” they enter upon and abide in the sphere of infinite consciousness; this is the fifth freedom.
>
> By completely surmounting the sphere of infinite consciousness, reflecting that “there is nothing owned,” they enter on and abide in the sphere where nothing is owned; this is the sixth freedom.
>
> By completely surmounting the sphere where nothing is owned, they enter on and abide in the sphere of neither labeling nor non-labeling; this is the seventh freedom.
>
> By completely surmounting the sphere of neither labeling nor non-labeling, they enter on and abide in the cessation of labeling and feeling; this is the eighth freedom.

<!-- Page 188 -->

## **The Seven Abidings of Consciousness (*ViÑÑāṭṭhiti*s)**

This list describes the differences and consonances between beings of various planes of existence. The first, for instance, describes beings in the sense sphere, such as animals, humans, and some gods, whose bodies and minds vary greatly from one another and whose minds and mental states are very diversified. The next three involve beings in the form sphere, or Brahmā heavens, who have subtle bodies.[[6]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-452 "note") Following that, the list moves to three formless realms, where there are no physical bodies. The kinship and differences between beings at each level is noted:

> There are beings that are diverse in body and diverse in perception, such as humans, some *deva*s, and some in the lower realm.[[7]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-453 "note") This is the first abiding of consciousness.
>
> There are beings that are diverse in body but identical in perception, such as the gods of Brahmā’s company that are reborn on the basis of the first *jhāna*. This is the second abiding of consciousness.[[8]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-454 "note")
>
> There are beings that are identical in body but diverse in perception, such as the gods of Streaming Radiance. This is the third abiding of consciousness.[[9]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-455 "note")
>
> There are beings that are identical in body and identical in perception, such as the gods that are Pervaded with Beauty.[[10]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-456 "note") This is the fourth abiding of consciousness.
>
> There are beings that, with the surmounting of perception of forms, by leaving behind perceptions of sensory impingement, and by not paying attention to perceptions of diversity, reflecting “space is infinite,” arise in the sphere of infinite space.[[11]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-457 "note") This is the fifth abiding of consciousness.
>
> There are beings that, with the surmounting of the sphere of infinite space, reflecting “consciousness is infinite,” arise in the sphere of infinite consciousness.[[12]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-458 "note") This is the sixth abiding of consciousness.
>
> There are beings that, with the surmounting of the sphere of infinite space, reflecting that “there is nothing owned,” arise in the sphere where nothing is owned.[[13]](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_nts_r1.xhtml#nts-459 "note") This is the seventh abiding of consciousness.

## A Note on Abbreviations and Sources

Abbreviations of the names of Pali and Sanskrit texts follow the system of the *Critical Pāli Dictionary*: <http://cpd.uni-koeln.de/intro/vol1_epileg_abbrev_texts>.

The exception to this is for the four major *nikāya*s. When specific *sutta*s are cited from the *Dīgha-nikāya* or the *Majjima-nikāya*, for example, the abbreviations for these two works are D and M, respectively, followed by the number of the *sutta*; the respective abbreviations are DN and MN when a reference to a particular part of a *sutta* is given. Full references from the *Saṃyutta-nikāya* and *Aṅguttara-nikāya* are given as SN and AN, respectively. Page references for Pali texts are to the Pali Text Society (PTS) edition (Ee), where available, otherwise to the Burmese edition (Be) as given on the Vipassanā Research Institute (VRI) CD, unless otherwise indicated. B1 refers to the *Saccasaṅkhepa* manuscript from the Mandalay collection now in the British Library.

Square brackets ([]) have been adopted to indicate pieces of text where the content is inferred, as in the case of fragmentary texts; where the text has been restored from parallel passages; or in instances where another word is needed to supply the full meaning in English.

The symbol < precedes a root, prefix, or word from which a term is derived.

Reference works are cited with the following abbreviations.

AMgD = Ratnachandraji, Shatavdhani (1927–1932), *An Illustrated Ardha-Magadhi Dictionary*. 4 vols. With an introduction by A. Woolner. (Varanasi: Amar Publications).

Apte = Apte, V.S. (1890), *Practical Sanskrit-English Dictionary* (Poona: Shiralkar).

BHSD = Edgerton, F. (1953), *Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit Dictionary.* 2 vols. (New Haven: Yale University Press).

BR = Böhtlingk, Otto von, and Rudolf von Roth (1855–1875), *Sanskrit-Wörterbuch*. 7 vols. (St. Petersburg: Buchdruckerei der Kaiserlichen Akademie der Wissenschaften).

<!-- Page 190 -->

DP = Cone, Margaret (2001; 2010; 2020), *A Dictionary of Pāli* (Bristol: Pali Text Society).

PED = Rhys Davids, T. W. and William Stede (1921–25), *Pali-English Dictionary.* Reprinted 1992 (Oxford: Pali Text Society).

PTC = Woodward, F.L. et al. (1955; 1973; 1993), *Pali Tipitakam Concordance.* 3 vols. (Bristol: Pali Text Society).

SED = Monier Williams, Monier (1899), *Sanskrit English Dictionary* (Oxford: Clarendon Press).

PED is clear here, but BHSD s.v. *auddhatya* seems to miss the mark. Abhidhkbh (Ne 1970) II 26 has *auddhatyaṃ cetaso vyupaśamaḥ*, which the *Vyākhyā* explains as the *caitasika* dharma that gives rise to bodily restlessness (*kāyauddhatya*) such as dancing, singing, sexual passion, and adornment of dress. For *kukkucca*, see Dhs 205, 14–17 and BHSD s.v. *kaukṛtya*. Historically the meaning is “evil deeds,” but in the Buddhist literature it normally refers to one’s response to one’s supposed evil deeds—remorse or guilt concerning imagined wrong acts of commission or omission.

Apart from the standard lists of the five *jhāna* factors and the contact pentad, the other six *dhamma*s mentioned are purpose (*chanda*); commitment (*adhimokkha*); vigor; mindfulness, equanimity; bringing to mind (*manasikāra*). Several of these are introduced into the *abhidhamma* lists only in the commentarial period or a little earlier. They are not given in the standard registers of the *Dhammasaṅgaṇī*.

42. Lance left *buddhi* untranslated. In the context cited here, “discernment” seems the best translation. Bhikkhu Bodhi provides careful discussion of this term in a recent article where he points out it is rare in Pali *sutta*s, being found largely in *Jātaka*s and the *Apadāna*. He explains it as a kind of spiritual intelligence or discernment, a capacity rather than the attainment indicated by the word *bodhi*. As he notes, commentarial literature supports this hypothesis (Bhikkhu Bodhi, “On Translating ‘Buddha,’ ” *Journal of the Oxford Centre for Buddhist Studies* 19 (2020), 52–78). Bodhi defines *buddhi* as an “intelligence with a moral and spiritual orientation” (p. 67) [SS].

Atha khvāssa pubbeva tathā cittaṃ bhāvitaṃ hoti yan taṃ tathattāya upanetī ti.* We could perhaps also compare the use of *sasaṅkhārena* in early *abhidhamma* literature: Dhs §146, etc.; Vibh 164, etc. Earlier, there is frequent mention of the type of *anāgāmin* known as *sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyin*. *Sasaṅkhāraniggayhavāritagato* is found describing a type of *samādhi* at DN III 279; SN I 28; AN I 254; III 24; IV 428; cf. also SN V 213ff.; AN I 82 where *sasaṅkhāra* is equivalent to *sanidāna*, etc.

That the interpretation as a deliberate process was current in ancient India is clear from Artha-v-n 239: *Apare tv āhuḥ: dīrghaṃ vā hrasvaṃ vā āśvasann iti prayogakṛtam atikrāntaprākṛtikaṃ dīrgham, prakṛtād ūnaṃ ca hrasvam*. “But others say, ‘Breathing long or short,’ i.e., long is [breathing that is] deliberately made to exceed the normal, short is less than the normal.”

15. Similarly, the *Śrāvakabhūmi* (Śbh II 95ff.), but this is because it presents the set of sixteen in the context of the practice for a *śaikṣa*.

<!-- Page 217 -->

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 15](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_c06_r1.xhtml#nts_285)
16. Methods of counting are very varied in Buddhist tradition nowadays and that seems also to have been the case in ancient India. The *Śrāvakabhūmi* (Śbh II 84ff.) describes methods of counting:

1. Counting each in or out breath up to ten.
    2. Counting each pair of in-and-out breaths up to ten.
    3. Numbering in one or other of these ways in ascending order up to ten.
    4. Numbering in one or other of these ways in descending order from ten to one.

It recommends developing proficiency in all of these.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 16](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_c06_r1.xhtml#nts_286)

Vism-mhṭ I 327 explains *anuvāhanā* as proceeding along continuously with mindfulness by following the in breaths and out breaths. Cf. Sp-ṭ II 11. Abhidh-k-bh VI 12: *anugama*.

8. The author says he has made five headings, but he had not yet assigned them as such on the table. I have tried to reconstruct it. So, for instance, I have put the divine abidings under loving-kindness (*maitrīcitta*), as he suggests in the chapter. This system is, broadly speaking, the pattern of the Chinese “Five Gates” discussed by Ruegg (1967) in an article where he records variations in this order in different texts. It is discussed in this chapter. The five there are mindfulness through in-and-out breathing (*ānāpānasmṛtsi*) to counteract “distraction of the mind” (*citta*) or “*vitarka*”; meditation on ugliness (*aśubabhāvana*) to counteract greed; loving-kindness (*maitrīcitta*) to counteract hatred; conditioned orgination (*pratītyasamupāda*) to counteract attachment to self; and recollection of the Buddha (*Buddhanusmṛti*) to counteract “submersion of the *citta*” (*hsin mu*) or “all the preceding obstacles” (Ruegg 1967, 159). [SS]

28. On the pinnacle or catafalque or extension (*kūṭāgāra*); see Bollée 1989. Here this would be a building with a separate roof as part of the palace complex. The front end would curve to a point, perhaps surmounted by a finial. The original may have been a small building outside a village, used by male sodalities for initiations, etc. Later, similar buildings were used by mendicants and were hence understood to have been used by the Buddha. They then became used to enshrine symbols and representations of the Buddha, giving rise to the ubiquitous shrine hall.

In the first, Ap 1, Gotama Buddha evokes a visualized palace, with one floor of silver and others of different kinds of jewels; it is covered with golden bells, nets, pictures of birds, beasts, the sun, the moon; the bells and nets “sing in the wind” in the manner of those in the *Mahāsudassana-sutta* (D 17), as indicated in this chapter. Hundreds of Buddhas from the past, as well as the Buddha and his followers in the present, come to sit on golden thrones in a vast assembly: (<http://apadanatranslation.org/text/chapter-1/poem-001.html>). 32. There is a collection of poems delivered by female *arahat*s on their awakening. Yasodharā, the Buddha’s wife, delivers such a poem for her ex-husband, the Buddha, shortly before she dies at the age of 78 and enters *nibba?na.* In these verses, as the author describes here, she pays a final homage to the Buddha. She transforms herself into different appearances, finally revealing herself as the whole universe, with the sun, moon, and stars. After this display she dissolves the images she has conjured up and teaches on emptiness (Thī Ap 28 *Yasodharā Apadāna*, also translated on Jonathan Walters’s website *Legends of Buddhist Saints* (<http://apadanatranslation.org/text/chapter-4/poem-028.html>). *kathañ ca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca appaṭikūle paṭikūlasaññī vihareyya? mā me rajanīyesu dhammesu rāgo udapādī ti—idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca appaṭikūle paṭikūlasaññī vihareyya.*

*kathañ ca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca paṭikūle appaṭikūlasaññī vihareyya? mā me dosanīyesu dhammesu doso udapādī ti—idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca paṭikūle appaṭikūlasaññī vihareyya.*

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 34](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_c07_r1.xhtml#nts_354)
35. These are discussed further in chapter 1. The *nimitta* is a canonical concept, as seen in the discussion in AN III 316, where six times are given when one should consult a meditation teacher; the appearance of the *nimitta* in meditation is the sixth. It is developed further by commentaries. In a recent article, which includes material that Lance would not have seen, Kate Crosby explores the usages of the *nimitta* as a “diagnostic tool” in Ceylon (Sri Lanka) from the nineteenth century, on the basis of manuscript evidence post-dating the Siam Nikāya movement’s arrival from what is now Thailand (Kate Crosby, “Abhidhamma and *Nimitta* in Eighteenth-Century Meditation Manuscripts from Sri Lanka: A Consideration of Orthodoxy and Heteropraxy in *Borān Kammaṭṭhān*,” *Contemporary Buddhism*, 20 (1–2), 2019, 111–51). This manuscript material is significant for detailing practices and techniques that have survived in other Southeast Asian contexts in oral or modernist adaptations. She notes that in Theravāda regions “*nimitta* are understood as signifying some kind of truth about the present, past or future, providing information that those suitably qualified may accurately interpret. The term *nimitta*, meaning ‘mark/sign’ in Pali and Sanskrit, is used in multiple contexts: omens, visions in dreams, and the visual/physical experiences of meditation are all referred to with this one term” (p. 124). Crosby’s “evidence for the extensive range, variety and sophistication of pre-modern meditation found in the Theravada world” (p. 113) enriches Lance’s argument here. [SS]

<!-- Page 225 -->

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 35](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_c07_r1.xhtml#nts_355)

8. Māra is the god of delusion, whose domain extends to the highest sense-sphere heaven. He is usually routed simply by being recognized—by those alert enough to do so—for what he is (DN II 262). The five Māras, a later development, are usually taken as the five aggregates, that “delude” all beings. [SS]

5. “Strong support,” or “powerful dependence” (*upanissaya*) is the ninth of the twenty-four causes or conditional relations (*paccaya*s) that constitute all possible relationships within the conditioned world. The *Paṭṭhāna*, the seventh book of the *abhidhamma*, delineates the nature of these twenty-four. It is the condition whereby a strong event or factor can arouse a radical change in consciousness; examples given in the *Paṭṭhāna* are weather, food, a particular person, or a lodging place. The prior development of states of consciousness can also act as a strong support. See, for instance, DhA II 33, and Vism 324, 343. “Basic support” is the translation of *upanissaya* in *The Path of Purification,* IX 120. See also Vism 143/*The Path of Purification*, IV 95. [SS]

## **Chapter 11. *Jhāna* Meditation Instructions from the Theriya Tradition: A Translation of Chapter Fourteen of Ācariya Buddhadatta’s *Entrance to Abhidhamma***

<!-- Page 230 -->

1. The article and translation first appeared in *Samatha: Insights from a Meditation Tradition* (Llangunllo, Wales: Samatha Trust, 2014), pp 6–22.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 1](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_c11_r1.xhtml#nts_422)
2. “Bringing into being” is a literal rendering of *bhāvanā* “meditation practice,” namely the development of the eightfold path.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 2](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_423)
3. *Sīla*, “character,” is what is developed by keeping the precepts—that is, living a wholesome life. In the eightfold path *sīla* is right speech, action, and livelihood.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 3](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_424)
4. *Viveka*, “apartness”—that is, separating the mind from sense objects and the hindrances.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 4](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_425)
5. “Three times Ten” is poetic language for the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 5](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_426)
6. *Kammaṭṭhāna*, “occupation,” in this context refers to one of the objects utilized to develop *samatha* meditation.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 6](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_427)
7. *Kasiṇa*, “totality”—that is, a meditation object such as a color when experienced as comprising everything there is. A *kasiṇa* disk or circle is an external object—circular or another shape—that is used initially to develop meditation on a totality.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 7](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_428)
8. The thinking character type refers to someone with a discursive type of mind.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 8](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_429)
9. *Rūpa* refers to everything we experience as color or form. In extended use, it refers to materiality in general. Non-*rūpa* is that which is without color or form and immaterial.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 9](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_430)
10. *Ārammaṇa*, “support,” connotes an object of the mind in a given moment. *Gocara*, “pasture,” namely the field in which the mind is “browsing” is an alternative word for the object of the mind.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 10](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_431)
11. “Balance,” *upekkhā*, “equanimity” literally means looking on at what is happening without getting caught up in it.

<!-- Page 231 -->

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 11](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_432)
12. *Abhiññā*, “higher knowledge”—that is, psychic powers and such things as clairvoyance and remembering past lives.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 12](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_433)
13. *Nirodha* is the meditative cessation of the second and third aggregates that transcends both form and formless meditation.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 13](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_434)
14. “A weak point” signifies a place where the opponent is vulnerable in martial combat. The meditation subject is metaphorically where the God of Love (Kāmadeva) is vulnerable.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 14](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_435)
15. *Kāma*s in this context are the objects of sense: color, sound, smell, taste, and things that can be touched.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 15](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_436)
16. Unlike the object of breathing mindfulness, the *nimitta* in *kasiṇa* practice has a fixed form.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 16](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_437)
17. *Tapas*: usually in South Asian religious tradition this refers to a kind of heat or energy generated by ascetic practice. Here the energy arising from meditation must be meant.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 17](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_438)
18. *Cakkavattin*, literally “discus-wielder” or “master of a sphere (realm),” is a legendary universal monarch.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 18](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_439)
19. “Passive mind” (*bhavaṅga*)—the rest state to which the mind returns after every activity.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 19](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_440)
20. Active minds (*javana*)—normally mental states of a skillful or unskillful kind. Here the mental process (*cittavīthi*) leading to *jhāna* is described.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 20](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_441)
21. Expanding the *nimitta* is not recommended for breathing mindfulness. See v. 821.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 21](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml#nts_442)
22. The mind becomes still and clear.

## **Appendix B: Commonly Cited Passages**

1. The translations for this appendix are by Sarah Shaw, with key words based on Lance’s preferred translations for those terms. For discussion of this list and the following, the freedoms, see Shaw, *Buddhist Meditation*, 86–100.

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 1](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_447)
2. The flowering *karnikara* tree (*pterospermum acerifolium*; *kaṇṇikāra* in Pali). [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 2](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_448)
3. The *bandhujivaka* flower (*ixora coccinea*). It is sometimes known as “scarlet jungle flame” and is popular now in Florida, USA. [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 3](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_449)
4. Buddhaghosa explains that the second freedom arises when one can find an object of meditation as the basis for the attainment of *jhāna* in the external world, whereas one finds the first freedom through objects in oneself (Asl 188). [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 4](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_450)
5. The word used for “release the mind” is *adhimutto.* The “beautiful” is the object of the first ten meditation objects in Buddhaghosa’s system, the *kasiṇa*s. It could possibly include the divine abidings of loving kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity (objects 31–34 in Buddhaghosa’s system), when developed into *jhāna*; these are also termed beautiful. These meditations can then too be used as a basis for formless meditation, as this list suggests. [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 5](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_451)
6. The list is found in Nidd II 151–52; AN IV 39–40; DN III 253. \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*), a very early commentary dating from around the 1st century b.c.e., discusses the meaning of terms used here. Not yet translated into English, it is regarded as canonical. Bhikkhu Bodhi has provided particularly helpful translations of the explanatory passages concerned (Bhikkhu Bodhi, *Sutta Nipāta*, 1305, and 1554–55, nn. 2145–48). [SS]

<!-- Page 233 -->

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 6](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_452)
7. This is explained by \**The Exposition* (*Niddesa*) as meaning that beings in human realms, sense-sphere heavens, and some lower realms have very different kinds of body, as well as diverse perceptions and mental states (see translation in Bhikkhu Bodhi, *Sutta Nipāta*, note 2145, page 1554). [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 7](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_453)
8. The beings reborn here (*Brahma-kāyikā*) live in the highest plane of the heaven associated with rebirth on the basis of the first *jhāna*. The commentary explains that this abiding refers also to the two other, lower planes of the Brahmā heavens associated with the first *jhāna.* The bodies of beings in these planes vary according to the level and depth of their radiance, and according to which of these three planes they have been reborn in; the perceptions of beings in these planes, however, are identical in that they all depend on the attainment of the first *jhāna* (see Bodhi, *Sutta Nipāta*, 1554–55, n. 2146). [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 8](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_454)
9. The beings reborn here (*ābhassarā*) live in the highest plane of the Brahmā heavens attained through the practice of the second *jhāna*; the *Niddesa* says that the two lower planes of these heavens are implied. Employing the *abhidhamma* method of five *jhāna*s, whereby the first *jhāna* is split into two with the second characterized by *vicāra* but not *vitakka*, this becomes the level of the second and the third *jhāna*s according to that system. Beings here have radiance streaming from them like the flames from a torch. They are identical in body as they all have the same pervasion of light, but different in perception in that some have taken rebirth on the basis of the *abhidhamma* second *jhāna*, which has *vicāra* but not *vitakka*, and others have taken rebirth on the basis of the third *jhāna*, which has no *vicāra* (see Bodhi, *Sutta Nipāta*, 1555, n. 2147). [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 9](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_455)
10. The beings here (*subhakiṇṇā*) live in the highest plane of the Brahmā heavens associated with the third *jhāna* in the *sutta* system and the fourth *jhāna* in the fivefold *abhidhamma* system. The *Niddesa* says that this abiding includes also those reborn on the basis of the fifth *jhāna* in the fivefold scheme, the fourth in the *sutta* method. Those who are gods of “great fruit,” the planes above, except for those without consciousness in the non-percipient realm, arise in this fourth abiding. Their radiance does not flicker and is unwavering; because of that they are identical in body, and their perception is identical (see Bodhi, *Sutta Nipāta*, 1555, n. 2148). [SS]

<!-- Page 234 -->

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 10](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_456)
11. This is the sphere of existence associated with the first formless meditation, attained within the Buddhist system after the fourth *jhāna* in *sutta* understanding, or the fifth *jhāna* in *abhidhamma.* In this and in the next three “abidings,” beings have no form (*rūpa*) body. [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 11](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_457)
12. This is the sphere of existence associated with the second formless meditation. [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 12](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_458)
13. This is the sphere of existence associated with the third formless meditation. [SS]

[BACK TO NOTE REFERENCE 13](Cous_9780834844438_epub3_appB_r1.xhtml#nts_459)
